II

To the memory of the Prophet 

Joseph Smith, whose very name is lovely as a song.

And the book is delivered to him that is not learned, saying, Read this, I pray thee: and he saith, I am not learned. 

Wherefore the Lord said, Forasmuch as this people draw near me with their mouth, and with their lips do honor me, but have removed their heart far from me, and their fear toward me is taught by the precept of men: 

Therefore, behold, I will proceed to do a marvellous work among this people, even a marvellous work and a wonder: for the wisdom of their wise men shall perish, and the understanding of their prudent men shall be hid. 

Isaiah 29:12-14 III

INTRODUCTION 

In offering this book for the consideration of the members of the Church, the authors realize the limitations under which they have labored in its preparation. 

There is first their own shortcomings as to ability and training. In the second place they have found themselves compelled at some points to question seriously the patterns for the BOOK OF MORMON that have for a generation been supposedly established as final. This knowledge has at times undoubtedly resulted in some hesitation on their part to follow out inexorably some lines of thought presented by the study. This in itself may or may not have worked to the detriment of the finished product. 

And, finally, the writers realize, perhaps few better, the meagerness of the information of the record. 

Geography includes so many things that it is likely the present work does not cover even many of its aspects. It comprehends, as applied to any given people or land, travels, locations, climate, distances, directions, topography, vegetation, animals, rivers, and natural and human resources, to mention only some of the most important. Some even of these fundamental things are touched upon here only slightly or incidentally, owing to the lack of detail in the BOOK 

OF MORMON. 

On most phases of the subject of geography the text has only a limited amount of information. Only in a general way can we determine boundaries and lands. With our present knowledge we cannot with certainty locate a single city of the BOOK OF MORMON in America. Local divisions of the lands can be but approximated. On only one point do students of the record seem to agree to any very great extent; namely, that the lands between the two great continents of the western hemisphere were the principal homelands of the ancient Americans. 

We know the starting points of all three colonies in the old world. The Jaredites began their journey at Babel; the 

Mulekites and Nephites came from Jerusalem. Of the

Mulekites nothing more is told. Of the other two some facts are given, and other details can be determined with a fair degree of certainty. Other matters are left altogether to conjecture.

And yet, while the present volume does not deal with all the phases of geography, it is in a sense more than geography. Some attention is given here to practically all aspects of the BOOK OF MORMON except doctrine. 

This dearth of definite information, to which reference 

IV

has been made above, is one of the reasons for the multitude of theories and opinions, some of which have been widely disseminated, which exist today and which to some extent cloud the issues involved. No two writers agree upon all points. In this jumble of ideas one is likely to become confused and is inclined to lay the whole matter aside as unfathomable or valueless.

It is this seeming uncertainty and wide difference of opinion that has for some time past prompted the writers to make a rather thorough and independent investigation of the subject.

A number of considerations have entered into the making of the study. The authors have been interested in discovering whether the information in the BOOK OF MOR- MON is sufficient to justify definite conclusions on the matters of geography, whether the record is consistent with itself in all its details, whether it was possible that Joseph Smith could have written the book, and whether there is any need for the bewildering confusion that attends this subject generally. There was one other purpose that will be presented shortly. These matters are not treated as headings in the book; they are brought out in connection with the unfolding of the material. 

While the present writers have an acquaintance in a general way with the existing body of writing on the subject of geography of the BOOK OF MORMON, they have here purposefully endeavored to keep this material in the background until the study was completed. Their purpose in this was, so far as possible, to remain uninfluenced in their conclusions. No attempt was made to read the record in terms of scientific discoveries. This treatise is in no sense a discussion of the external evidences of the BOOK OF MORMON. It is gratifying, however, to the writers, to know that to a remarkable extent their conclusions are in accord with the best findings of the scientists.

They have tried to approach and complete this task, if task it may be called, with no preconceived notions, with only the desire to find the truth. Those who read these pages will be no more surprised at some of the conclusions than the writers themselves have been.

Many points have arisen which are not easily understood or explained, and there are some matters at which no attempt at explanation is made here. The authors do not feel that it is part of their present undertaking to explain everything. If their conclusions are wrong, no explanation is necessary. If they are right, it becomes the obligation of every reader to enquire into the truth of it all for himself.

The writers are convinced that such difficulties as are encountered are apparent rather than real, that they arise out of our present inadequate knowledge, and that additional light and study will remove all uncertainty regarding them.

The other reason for the study, mentioned above, arises out of a long-neglected need.

Granted that the doctrinal value of the Bible is of first importance, it must be conceded that the literary value is next. And in the literature of the Bible few things are more precious than the Bible stories. But how hardly shall we appreciate or understand these stories without some knowledge of country, climate, and customs? How can we get the full power and beauty of the stories of Daniel or Esther without some knowledge of Babylon and Persia? Even the psalms and parables arise out of the country of their birth. 

This is equally true of the BOOK OF MORMON. Many people who are well-versed in its doctrines know little or nothing of its literature. 

Which direction did the four sons of Mosiah and their friends go when they left Zarahemla to preach to the Lamanites? How far did Corianton go when he left his mission for the company of the fascinating harlot Isabel? From what part of the country did the two thousand young Lamanite soldiers come who joined Helaman in the war on the west? What sort of journey was contemplated by King

Lamoni when he gave orders for the preparation of his chariots for a visit to Nephi? Through what sort of country did Alma travel when with his followers he traversed the distance from Mormon to Zarahemla in twenty-one days?

Is it not obvious that a knowledge of such details would aid greatly in the understanding of the BOOK OF MORMON and the appreciation of the wonderful stories to be found therein? The authors believe that it would.

They desire to make clear to everyone at this point that such opinions as are given here are their own and are expressed entirely on their own responsibility. This does not mean that they were the first to reach many such conclusions; it means simply that no one else may be held to account for their presentation here. While some of the General 

Authorities of the Church know of this investigation and are aware of the nature of the material contained in the book, they are in no way involved in any statement. 

The authors desire to make this acknowledgment, inadequate though it be, of their obligation to all who have by their encouragement and suggestions given aid in the preparation of this volume, and they are many. 

The method of study has been the only one seemingly VI

that can yield the proper results. The authors have read the entire BOOK OF MORMON carefully, keeping an accurate record of all references having any bearing upon the subject of geography. These were subsequently painstakingly compared and classified. No attempt has been made to build a set-up of geography upon a few so-called "key" passages. Such procedure, apparently followed too often, has contributed a large measure of the confusion and controversy that now surround the subject. 

It is natural that there should be differences of opinion on minor matters at least. Perhaps complete unanimity is not even desirable except on fundamental questions. Furthermore, views change from time to time on many points. 

The writers desire their point of view to be clearly understood. They have no thought whatever that they here dispose of the problems of BOOK OF MORMON geography and settle them for all time. Their title is used with a purpose. This is indeed an approach to the study of BOOK OF 

MORMON geography. If it presents a challenge and leads to the wide-spread reading of the record, the authors will be more than satisfied. It is not necessary that others agree with them; it is necessary that further investigation result until the truth be reached. Whether the work here has been done well or badly, the writers trust that others more capable will take up the challenge of these pages and carry on the study.

With respect to the form of the material it need only be said that there are two kinds of type. The black-face portion is text, the record itself, and is in no wise open to question or controversy. The rest is made up of the authors' opinions, explanations, and speculations and should be read carefully and critically.

One additional thought might be added. If we are to impress scientific men and women, those from whom we may hope for the most help in external evidences, we ourselves must be orderly and scientific in our presentation of the BOOK OF MORMON. Only then perhaps can we expect archaeologists to use the record as a basis for their researches.

The authors sincerely desire that all readers understand at the outset that criticisms and suggestions are welcome. 

They will appreciate letters and comments from all who desire to offer them. There are doubtless errors here. The book should be read as an approach to the problems involved in the study. At best it is perhaps but a definite guide to such study and may approach the truth only remotely or at some points.

VIII

PART ONE THE JAREDITES

CHAPTER ONE  

A word of explanation should be given before any attempt is made to present the history of the Jaredites. 

Throughout the study of those ancient people the reader will do well to remember constantly that in their record we have the history of 1,600 years condensed into fifteen chapters. The narrative is frequently interrupted by the abridger, the Prophet Moroni, with his comments. Shortened as it is, the story is almost lacking in detail. We should not expect, therefore, to find in it any great wealth of historical or geographical information. 

The purpose of this first chapter is to determine whether anything definite can be ascertained regarding possible routes of travel and probable landing places for the Jaredites.

I. The Old Home. 

In point of time the Jaredites were the first people of whom the BOOK OF MORMON is a record who came to 

America from the old world.

Their history begins with the confusion of tongues at the tower of Babel. (Ether 1:3, 5, 33) By a special kindness of the Lord their language was preserved, and they were promised that they would be led to a land which was "choice above all the lands of the earth." (Ether 1:42.)

The company consisted of Jared, his brother, their families, and a few of their friends and their families. 

The exact number is not known, but, as we shall see later, there were probably not more than one hundred in all. 

The city Babel was built under the direction of Nimrod, the mighty hunter, who was the son of Cush and grandson of Ham. Babel later became Babylon the Great. (Genesis 

10:6-19; Ether 2:1.)

II. Which Way Did the Jaredites Travel? 

The Great Sea 

(Mediterraneun) 2017 

They go northward

Egypt

Leaving Babel 

Babe 

First home of the Jaredites Persian gult Leaving Babel, the Jaredites, under instructions from the Lord, went northward down into the valley of Nimrod. (Ether 1:42; 2:1) They drove with them their flocks and herds, and on the way they caught fowl, fish, and bees. (Ether 2:1-4.) 

But they could not have continued to go northward and arrive at their destination in the promised land. At some point they would have had to turn to the west or the east. The map given here suggests the routes over which they might have traveled as viewed by the present writers and others.

Had they gone far enough to the north, they could have gone down one of the rivers that flowed into the Caspian Sea, assuming, as we do assume in connection with the old world, that the physical features of the country are fundamentally the same today as they were in olden times. This would have required a land journey, to the Caspian, of about seven hundred miles and would have taken them east to the Pacific Ocean.

It has been suggested also that the Jaredites might have continued on to the north until they reached the Black Sea through which they could have pushed into the Mediterranean Sea.

Again, they could have traveled west, perhaps from the valley of Nimrod, to the valley of the Orontes, and then into the Mediterranean. This, like the route through the 

Black Sea, would eventually have had them arriving at the

Atlantic Ocean instead of the Pacific. Some variations of these have been suggested. One proposes that the Jaredites took their course along the northern boundary of the Mediterranean.

It will be noted that all the routes suggested above include some water. This insistence upon a water voyage is necessary according to the text. But the record gives no indication of the water over which the Jaredites traveled. 

Somewhere in their journeying the Jaredites built barges or boats in which "they did cross many waters." 

(Ether 2:6.) 

This term "many waters" probably does not mean many rivers or bodies of water. As used elsewhere in the BOOK 

OF MORMON, it means a large body of water. (I Nephi 

13:10; 17:5.)

The likelihood of the Jaredites' having taken the four suggested routes will now claim our attention for a time. 

It is certain they did not follow all of them, and any one may be right only in a general way, or all may be quite wrong.

A. The Eastern Route to the Pacific. 

If the Jaredites took the eastern journey to the Pacific and built the first barges on the shore of the Caspian, the only water seemingly they could have crossed, they would have had a land trip from Babel to that point of about seven hundred miles, again assuming no great changes in topography.

As to the water, if they went directly across the Caspian, they would have needed boats for about two hundred miles, a very short distance certainly for all the labor required in the building of the boats. From the eastern shore of the 

Caspian there would have been another land journey, this time of nearly four thousand miles, to the Pacific coast of eastern Asia. This would have made a total distance from 

Babel to the ocean of about five thousand miles, only two hundred of which would require the use of boats. Would not the relative insignificance of water to land render it highly improbable that they went that way? 

B. The Black Sea Route. 

If the Jaredites went by way of the Black Sea and then into the Mediterranean, they simply increased the length of

their travels to no apparent purpose. Of course, we know much more about the country today than they knew in their time, and purpose or no purpose they might well have taken either of the routes suggested above. At

There is something to be said in favor of this Black Sea journey. The language of the record could easily be interpreted to justify such a course. "And it came to pass that the Lord did bring Jared and his brethren forth even to that great sea which divideth the lands" (Ether 2:13) first glance this would seem to refer to the Mediterranean. 

And in truth it might well mean just that. Such being the case, the first boats would in all probability have been used to cross the Black Sea. Moriancumer would, therefore, have been somewhere along the country of the Dardanelles or in present-day Greece or Italy. The main question here is: whether the expression, "that great sea which divideth the lands," has reference to the Mediterranean Sea or the ocean..

C. The Land Journey North of the Mediterranean. 

The authors think the possibility of a land journey north of the Mediterranean rather untenable in view of the fact. that apparently no boats would have been needed. Surely the Jaredites would hardly have built barges to cross the Hellespont.

D. The Mediterranean or Western Route. 

From Babel the pilgrims could have moved northward' up the Euphrates. It is quite possible that they would have experienced little difficulty in reaching from there the valley of the Orontes, a river that flows northward in the Lebanon Mountains and turns westward into the Mediterranean. At the point where the stream turns to the west, Antioch was later built, about sixteen miles from the sea. 

From before the days of Abraham caravans have traveled this way. For ages it has been a favorite route for traffic and armies from west to east and from east to west. ¹

The distance from Babel to the Mediterranean over this road is about five hundred miles, much less than from Babel to the Caspian, and much less than would have been required through the Black Sea. 

1. Hurlbut, J. M.,Bible Atlas, pp. 3-6 

Kramm, International Atlas, pp. 86-87, 90-91 

Smith, George Adam, Historical Geography of the Holy Land, pp. 4-7, 46

If we assume that the first Jaredite barges were built at this convenient point, the mouth of the Orontes, it is easy to see that they could have been used on the entire voyage through the Mediterranean to the ocean. Easy access would have been had to land for the replenishing of the food supply at many points along the voyage to the temporary resting place at which the first boats were discarded. The entire distance from Babel to what is now north Africa, Spain, or Portugal would have been about 2,700 miles, two thousand of which would have been by water across the Mediterranean.

At some point the Jaredites discarded their first boats. 

Obviously they had no further use for them. They must have taken to the land. Indeed, the record strongly indicates this. "And now I proceed with my record: For behold, it come to pass that the Lord did bring Jared and his brethren forth even to that great sea which divideth the lands. And as they came to the sea they pitched their tents; and they dwelt in tents, and dwelt in tents upon the seashore for the space of four years." (Ether 2:13)

South America 

Jaredite Travels 

Apparently the Jaredites abandoned their first boats and journeyed by land. After traveling by land for a certain time they came to the sea, the great sea (ocean) which divides the lands, and there they pitched their tents. This land might well have been what is now Spain or Portugal, or northern Africa, or any part of either of them.

To summarize: The eastern journey would have required a land trek of nearly five thousand miles with only about two hundred miles of water for the use of the first

boats. It would have necessitated an exceedingly long ocean voyage, across the Pacific, to the western coast of the Americas, provided always, of course, that they kept on eastward after reaching the Pacific.

The western route would have been much shorter, both by land and water, and much more direct, and would have given sufficient reason for the building and use of boats, the long voyage across the Mediterranean. The subsequent ocean voyage, across the Atlantic, would have been much shorter than that across the Pacific, and it would have brought the company to the eastern shore of America. 

For the reasons already given, and others yet to be presented, the authors believe the western route to be the one that is most reasonable, the one that agrees with the facts, particularly the use of a temporary set of boats. 

Somewhere on the shores of that "great sea which divideth the lands" the Jaredites stopped four years at a place they called Moriancumer...(Ether 2:13)

The place Moriancumer was probably named for the brother of Jared. Nothing is known, beyond the suggestions already given, as to the location of it, nor is there any indication in the record of the time required to make the journey to it. 

E. Did Some of the Jaredites Stay in Spain?

As an exceedingly interesting, thought not necessarily vital part of the study, the authors present here a thought that has been in their minds for some time. It may eventually develop into something of value, or it may be shown to be altogether unwarrantable.

Is it possible that some of the Jaredites could have remained in Spain? This is a strange question, to be sure. Yet it must be answered in the affirmative; it is possible. Is it at all likely that such a thing happened? Again the answer is affirmative.

It has long been a matter of general knowledge that the Spanish Basques and their language and history challenge the learning of the world. To the present date no satisfactory explanation has been given of them. Their true place in history has not yet been found. The best scholarship in the world has failed to account for them. 

Who are the Basques? Whence did they come? Why

is their language totally unlike any other in all the earth? 

How does it happen that their history goes farther back than the nations around them? 

These questions cannot be answered yet. But since there is a great deal of speculation regarding these remarkable people, the authors here give their guess which may not be so absurd as at first it appears. 

Is it unreasonable to suppose that the Basques are descendants of some of the Jaredites who failed or refused to continue the journey to America?

It is true that the record gives no hint of any such thing. 

But that is of little or no consequence. The Jaredite story as we have it is in fifteen chapters. Yet it covers the whole life of the Jaredite nation, a period of 1,600 years. And even these fifteen chapters are not all narrative or history. 

Much of the space is devoted to the teaching and prophesying of the abridger, the last Nephite prophet, Moroni. 

None has realized the shortness of the history more than he. Here are his words: "And the Lord spake unto Ether, and said unto him: 

Go forth. And he went forth, and beheld that the words of the Lord had all been fulfilled; and he finished his record; (and the hundredth part I have not written) and he hid them in a manner that the people of Limhi did find them.” (Ether 15:33) "And the hundredth part I have not written." If we had something over 1,500 chapters, we should know much more than we do about the Jaredites. 

But we must get back to the Basques. sibly have descended from the Jaredites? facts about them.

This is interesting! When we remember that Nimrod, the great hunter, who directed the building of the Tower of Babel, was the son of Cush, who was the son of Ham, we do not find it altogether improbable that some of his race

tongue spoken by the primitive Iberians, before the Kelts began to flow over and around the Pyrenees; and also that the physical characteristics of this people are the same as their ancient progenitors; small-framed, dark, with a faint suggestion of the Semitic in their swarthy faces. Is this guess that the tongue of the Basques is the language of the angels so very far-fetched? If it was a descendant of the original language of the people of the Bible, it would come very near to being just that. And what of the suggestion that it came to Spain before the confusing of tongues at Babel? 

Why is it unlike any other language on earth? If the original tongue was preserved with the Jaredites, and all the others were changed, should we not expect it to be different? When the Jaredites were destroyed, their language died with them, so there is no way of telling what it was like. Might the speech of the Basques bear a resemblance to the language of Adam?

Aside from interest in the subject for its own sake, the authors here present this fascinating possibility for whatever of weight it might add to the probability that the Jaredites followed the western route through Spain rather than the eastern, across Asia to the Pacific Ocean and the west coast of America. 

8. Parmele, Mary Platt, A Short History of Spain, pp. 4-5

CHAPTER TWO FROM MORIANCUMER TO THE PROMISED LAND 

After four years had passed, the Lord said to the brother of Jared, "Go to work and build, after the manner of barges which ye have hitherto built. And it came to pass that the brother of Jared did go to work, and also his brethren, and built barges after the manner which they had built, according to the instructions of the Lord." (Ether 2:16)

From the foregoing quotation it becomes evident that the barges which the men had "hitherto built" and discarded were not mere canoes that could be carried from river to river, for the Jaredites embarked on the ocean, we may be sure, in no such craft. It is easy to see why new boats were to be built. The long voyage across the Mediterranean (if they went that way) and the subsequent four-year period could have weakened the old ones and rendered them unseaworthy, even provided that they were still available which is doubtful if the people traveled far overland. 

I. The Jaredite Barges.

A discussion of the probable nature of those Jaredite boats is justified here because of the bearing their structure has upon the question of a landing place in the new world. 

While the description is extremely indefinite, it is very interesting and offers material for imagination and conjecture. 

Here are the facts as set forth in the record, in the second chapter of the Book of Ether. 

1. There were eight boats in the fleet. 

2. They were light like fowl upon the water. 

3. They were tight on top, sides, and bottom, and would hold water like a dish. 

4. When they were built, there was no provision for either light or air. 

5. The ends of the boats were peaked.

MORIANCUMER TO THE PROMISED LAND 11 

6. The length of each boat was "the length of a tree."

The deficiencies of light and air were disposed of in the following manner: Holes were cut in top and bottom of each boat that could be left open or could be closed when the sea was rough. Light was provided by the use of two luminous stones for each craft, stones that were made bright by being touched by the finger of the Lord.

Some matters pertaining to the size, management, and use of the boats that are interesting are presented by the authors in another work, and a brief discussion is quoted here.

SIDE CROSS SECTION ANIMACE' DEPARTMENT SUPPLIES PEOPLE'S DEPARTMENT SUPPLIES 

Jaredite Boats TOD

BOTTOM "The boat is divided into two compartments, one for the people and one for the animals. Under the floor and at each end are rooms for storing seeds and provisions. The hole extends entirely through the structure from top to bottom. 

The top hole admits light and air when the door is open. 

Through the bottom end of the hole all waste can be dumped and soon find its way out into the open sea. When the doors are closed, the boat can be submerged, but water cannot get into it. It should be necessary to close the doors only in times of rough weather, and that might not be often. "Now, what was the size of these boats? We can only conjecture. They were peaked at the ends and as long as a tree. In my front yard is a tree three feet tall. But we read of trees more than 200 feet tall. I am sure that no such tree as either of these is meant. I am sure also that those boats were not canoes made of one log each. "Suppose we imagine a boat forty feet long by twelve feet wide. The relative dimensions might be wrong, but that will not matter. If the boat had square corners like a box, there would have been 480 square feet of floor space.

But the craft was peaked at the ends, and this would have cut the area to about 250 square feet. If a bit of each end were used for storage, and a partition were built in the middle, the space might be reduced to 180 square feet, or 1,440 square feet for the eight boats.".

The authors show that such boats as they describe could probably carry ninety tons each which would surely have been sufficient.

It is enough to say that the barges were probably not large enough to be unwieldy. They were like submarines in that they could be submerged. The notion that they turned over from time to time is hardly tenable. Though they might have had rudimentary sails for use in steering, they were in no sense sail boats, yet they were almost wholly dependent upon the wind and waves for locomotion. 

II. The Voyage. 

We come now to some of the most interesting aspects of the story.

After equipping and provisioning their boats the Jaredites "set forth into the sea, commending themselves unto the 

Lord their God. (Ether 6:4). . . . And the Lord caused that there should be a furious wind blow upon the face of the waters, toward the promised land; and thus they were tossed upon the waves of the sea before the wind." (v. 5) The crossing required 344 days. (v. 11) 

CARBELL COLLETT 

This furious wind that blew continually toward the promised land was not sent specially to deliver the Jaredites. 

It was, however, no less the result of the power of the Lord. 

It blew in the same direction or directions long before the Jaredites trusted themselves to the mercy of the Lord upon 

9. Washburn, J. A., and J. N., From Babel to Cumorah, p. 12

MORIANCUMER TO THE PROMISED LAND 13 

the great waters. "For.... the winds have gone forth out of my mouth, and also the rains and floods have I sent forth. And behold I prepare you against these things; for ye cannot cross this great deep save I prepare you against the waves of the sea, and the winds which have gone forth, and the floods which shall come." (Ether 2:24-25)

This mention of floods in the ocean is very interesting. 

The winds and the floods are eternal. The men were instructed to build their boats so as to make it possible to take advantage of them. The winds and floods are still at work under the mandate of God.

At this point some very intriguing questions naturally arise. Where did the Jaredites embark? Which way did they go that the eternal floods and winds bore them along? 

Why were they so long upon the water? Where did they finally land?

The following brief discussion will help. For a more thorough-going treatment the student should consult any authoritative work on physical geography. It appears that the author and the translator of the record have met all the requirements of modern scientific knowledge having any bearing upon this subject, whether or not they had any actual information on it themselves. 

A. The Winds and Floods. 

Ocean currents are large-scale movements of the water in certain parts of the oceans. They are the result of many forces chief among which are the blowing of the prevailing winds and the pull of the earth in rotation. But the changes of the seasons, configuration of coast lines and ocean bottoms, salt content of the water, depth of water, and absence or presence of ice are all factors that cause or influence the ocean currents.

Since the winds are the most important of the causes of currents, it will be necessary to examine in some detail the factors that bring about the prevailing winds. 

Winds are caused by atmospheric pressure, and atmospheric pressure comes from the interplay of heat and cold. 

The territory at or near the equator is the warmest on the earth. There the heated air expands and rises, being lighter in its expanded condition than the surrounding cold atmos-

phere. Cold currents rush in from the north and south to take the place of the rising warm air. This movement is so strong and regular that it is one of the most important phenomena of nature.

These cold currents move over the surface of the earth. until they come to the heat equator where they in turn become heated and rise in a belt of low pressure to cool again as they return poleward.

By the time these returning winds reach the zone about thirty to forty degrees of latitude both north and south of the equator, they are so cold that they descend again to the surface of the earth in belts of extremely high pressure. 

Here each current divides into two. One part of each turns again toward the equator to become heated, and the rest moves on toward the poles to areas of low pressure that are due to the slow movement of the earth at those points. 

Below and between the ascending currents of air at the equator there is a large area of little or no wind that has been known to sailors time out of mind as the Equatorial 

Calm Belt. 

Near the tropics, roughly from thirty to forty degrees, there are areas of high atmospheric pressure resulting from the descent of the heavy cooled air lately from the equator. 

But where the currents divide to push on poleward or return equatorward there are two other belts of calm, one in each hemisphere, that are called the Belts of High Pressure Calm or the Horse Latitudes. And again in the neighborhood of both poles there are zones of low pressure and irregular wind that result from the slow movement of the earth. "The westerlies and the trades together with the regions of calm give rise to nine wind belts. In the center lies the equatorial belt of low pressure, rising air and calms. On the north side of this belt lie the northeast trades and on the south the southeast trades. Next to them come the two subtropical belts of high pressure, descending air, and calms. 

Still farther toward the poles come the westerlies blowing generally from the southwest in the northern hemisphere and from the northwest in the southern hemisphere. Finally around the poles we have two areas of irregular winds and calms." 10 

10. Huntington and Cushing, Principles of Human Geography, p. 214.

MORIANCUMER TO THE PROMISED LAND 15 

These facts all have tremendous importance to any student of geography. 

Now, if the earth were stationary and fixed solidly, the winds would blow due north and south. But the earth turns, and in turning it causes the winds to be deflected or turned aside. It is necessary, however, to observe that the rate and angle of deflection are not the same in all places. They depend upon the speed at which the globe is revolving at any given place.

Near the poles there is practically no movement at all of the surface of the earth. There the speed of rotation is so slight that the atmosphere is only indifferently influenced. 

But toward the equator the speed of rotation increases rapidly because of the growing circumference of the earth. 

If we put a wheel on an upright post and turn it, we discover that the hub is moving slowly whereas the tire is moving at a high rate of speed. This is necessary if the outer part of the wheel is to maintain its relative position with the central.

This is likewise true of the world. At the exact poles there may be said to be no movement at all, but at the equator the surface of the earth is going at a tremendous speed, approximately one thousand miles an hour. And between the two points are all degrees of variation of speed. 

This movement of the earth "pulls" on the air and water and causes them to follow. If the speed is not too great, the more substantial water can keep pace with it, and even the air can move fast if the pull is regular and continuous. 

But there comes a point at which neither the water nor the air can keep up, a point at which the rush of the land leaves the air and water far behind. This falling behind is termed a lag.

To return to the illustration of the wheel, if the wheel be set in motion slowly, a small quantity of water can be placed on the hub and remain there. But a like amount of water put on the rim will quickly be lost. The wheel will move too fast for it to follow.

This principle accounts for the apparently contradictory fact that the rotation of the earth causes winds to blow both eastward and westward.

From the poles to the tropics the air keeps fairly close

relation to the land with the result that the prevailing winds blow in general from west to east with the turning of the globe. But it must not be forgotten that while the earth is pulling the air eastward, the interplay of heat and cold is driving it northward and southward. The result is, as already shown by the quotation from Huntington and Cushing, that there is a strong wind blowing generally from the southwest in the northern hemisphere and from the northwest in the southern hemisphere. These winds, since they blow in general from the west, are known as the prevailing Westerlies.

And now let us look into the apparently contradictory situation of winds, in a sense side by side with the Westerly 

Winds, blowing from east to west.

At the point at which the rotation of the earth, owing to its greater size and circumference, is such that the air cannot keep up, an interesting phenomenon is observed.

We shall employ again a simple illustration. Suppose an individual were walking on a straight line from south to north across a floor that moved from west to east. He would start at the south end of the line, but would he stop on the north end of it? By no means. Every time he lifted a foot to move forward, the moving floor would change his position with respect to the line, and by the time he reached the north side, the north end of the line would be way to the east or right of him. His progress, with respect to the floor, would be from southeast to northwest. If the start were made at the north end, the course would be from northeast to southwest.

This illustrates what happens to the air when it comes to the place at which the surface of the earth "runs away from it." With a stationary earth, as we have seen, the winds would blow from north to south and from south to north. But the rotation of the earth alters this simple relationship. In those latitudes in which the speed of the earth is not too great the winds tend to follow its direction. But those winds blowing nearer the equator move in the opposite direction because of the lag already mentioned. They bear to the west just like the man walking on the moving floor. 

It is, of course, at the equator that they attain their maximum westward velocity.

These southeast-to-northwest and northeast-to-southwest winds have been known for centuries as the Trade Winds. Their importance to shipping and ocean travel can-

MORIANCUMER TO THE PROMISED LAND 17 

not be overstated. Huntington and Cushing say this of them: "The regularity and strength of the Trades is so great that for centuries sailing ships from England and France have found it to advantage to go south to the trade wind zone in order to be blown to America. On the return voyage, on the other hand, if a ship started from Florida, for instance, it went north at first in order to get out of the trade winds into the westerlies which would blow it back to Europe. 

With the increasing use of airships the direction of the winds is becoming of still greater importance. It is worth while for an airship to go hundreds of miles out of its direct course in order to find favorable winds." (p. 214)

It is true that the water of the ocean follows the direction of the earth only to a certain extent. That in the depths is controlled by the pull of the earth in rotation, but the movement of the surface water, usually to a depth of a few hundred feet, is governed almost entirely by the winds. At any given place, consequently, one might find the deep water flowing east. But that on the surface would as likely as not be going west. And there would be variable rates between to a place, called the friction point, where there would be no movement at all to speak of. It has been definitely proved that sunken ships move east under the very places where sailing ships are traveling west.

One other thought may be added to the foregoing. One might naturally wonder that ships and ocean currents go west whereas the winds that control them go southwest and northwest. Studies by Ekman and others have shown conclusively that the water moves at an angle of about fortyfive degrees to the direction of the wind. This is probably due to a lag between the swifter wind and the slower water. 

At any rate, the winds that blow northwest and southwest force the surface water westward.

Another brief explanation is in order. The terms "equator" and "heat equator" have been used in this discussion. For present purposes they may be employed interchangeably. The geographical equator is, of course, always the same. The heat equator shifts slightly with the seasons but at most only a few degrees.

To state briefly the facts relative to winds and surface currents: Throughout the oceans of the world, wherever there are prevailing winds, there are mighty ocean streams or drifts-the first having clearly defined boundaries, the

latter wide and sluggish-that move in general with the air currents until acted upon by some force. The greatest obstacles to currents are land bodies-continents and islands. 

When a current comes to a coast, the natural thing is for it to divide. Part goes one way, and the rest goes the other. One portion or the other returns over the same zone after completing a tremendous whirlpool. These pools are thousands of miles in extent, and they follow fairly definite patterns. Asia Jap Japan

Ocean Currents and Jaredite Journey 

This is particularly true in the Atlantic Ocean. A strong westward current sets in from the neighborhood of Spain and the Canary Islands. It flows slightly southward in a wide sweep to influence the surface water between the equator and the Tropic of Cancer. This is the North Equatorial Current. Driven by the Northeast Trades it moves westward like a vast river until it nears the shores of America. 

It first touches northern South America then sweeps past

Central America and the Caribbean area and out through the Gulf of Mexico. Here it is known as the Gulf Stream.

Moving north of the Sargasso Sea, it reaches Europe where it divides, part to go on northward as the Gulf Stream Drift, and part to return past Spain as the North Equatorial Current again.

It is of interest in this connection to note that all the voyages of Columbus and those of many other early explorers followed this course from Spain and Portugal to the new world.

There are similar whirlpools in all the oceans though not in all cases with the same uniformity of flow and regu-

MORIANCUMER TO THE PROMISED LAND 19 

larity of direction.

As regards the application of this material to the subject at hand-the routes of travel of the ancient American people-a further explanation should be made. 

Between the North and South Equatorial Currents, in both the Atlantic and the Pacific, corresponding exactly with the Equatorial Calm Belt, and owing to the rising of the winds, a current flows eastward with the earth, pulled by the deeper water with no wind to interfere. So far as this counter current in the Atlantic is concerned, we have no present interest in it since no one assumes that it figured in the travels of any of the colonies under consideration. In the case of the Pacific Counter Current, however, it is not impossible that it might have been important as will be shown later. 

How fast do these ocean streams move?

As we should expect, there are different rates of speed under different conditions and in various places. In mid- 

Atlantic the North Equatorial Current moves about eighteen miles each twenty-four hours. The maximum is approximately thirty miles for the twenty-four hours, and the minimum is ten. " "Where the equatorial current comes out from the Gulf of Mexico between Florida and Cuba it begins to be known as the Gulf Stream. It is the strongest ocean current that we know of, the only one that moves like a great river. In the 

Straits of Florida it has a depth of 2,000 to 3,000 feet, a width of about 40 miles in the narrowest part, and a velocity of nearly five miles an hour. As it comes out into the main Atlantic, however, it quickly loses its river-like quality and spreads out into a broad shallow sheet which moves more and more slowly until its rate is only half a mile an hour." 12 

It has been estimated that three years are required for the water to complete the circuit in the Gulf Stream and the North Equatorial Current from a given point to that same point again. 13

The student desiring a more detailed discussion of this matter of winds and currents might do well to study such

B. The Jaredites on the Flood. Let us now suppose that the Jaredites launched their boats into the North Atlantic Current from what is now 

Spain, Portugal, or northern Africa. They might well have been driven by the wind and ocean floods past the Canary Islands toward the new world. As a result they could have reached land at some point between the two great continents of the west. The distance covered would have been more than six thousand miles. If we divide this by the number of days, 344, we find that the boats would have made an average speed of about fifteen to eighteen miles a day, a rate very closely approximating the speed of the currents. 

If the Jaredites took the eastern route suggested above, through the Pacific, they probably got into the Japanese Current and landed somewhere on the western coast of North America after a very long voyage of more than eight thousand miles. 

Whether the foregoing conclusions are right or wrong, the western route would have been a perfectly natural one. 

And in view of these considerations it seems safe to assume, for the present at least, that the Jaredites made their landing at some point on the eastern coast of Central America or Mexico. It will be very interesting to see whether subsequent findings agree with this. 

The authors agree cheerfully that there are many ques-

MORIANCUMER TO THE PROMISED LAND 21 

tions thus left unanswered. All they ask is that the reader will follow them to the end and then draw his own conclusions. 

Lending the "Jarednes 

Did Joseph Smith know these facts?

In spite of the fact that Franklin, and perhaps others, had made some study of ocean currents, it is quite improbable that in his early years Joseph Smith had read anything at all concerning ocean currents and their movements. Yet the description of Jaredite boats and the time involved in their journey from the old world to the new world are entirely in harmony with more recent scientific discoveries. 

This is not a mere coincidence, but a direct evidence in favor of the divinity of the record.

CHAPTER THREE JAREDITE  

For the purpose of making the events in the lives of the Jaredites clearer in relation to their surroundings, the authors give here a very condensed account of the most important doings of that ancient people for 1,600 years.

Events move rapidly with the Jaredites as a result of the abridgment of their record. More than forty rulers are named or mentioned, but there were times when more than one claimed the throne at once. And there may have been other periods when there were no kings at all though this is doubtful.

While the people bickered and divided into factions, it is to be doubted that they ever developed widely-different nations such as later appeared among the descendants of the Lehite colony. Groups seem to have moved from one leader to another with no very permanent allegiances. Furthermore, there is no way of determining time with them very accurately except for the beginning and the end. 

I. The Jaredite Kings.

The rulers of the Jaredites will claim our attention for a moment. As noted above, more than forty, at one time or another, claimed the office of king. Fortunately, however, the historian has simplified for us the problem by giving an apparently approved list of twenty-eight rulers. It is contained in the first chapter of Ether.

It is not clear on just what basis he accepted the kings to this legitimate group. Some whom many readers would doubtless include are left out, and there are a few on the list whose names, in the opinion of the present writers, should perhaps be excluded. Some mentioned as kings were captives all their lives, and others who are withheld from the selected group exercised all the rights and enjoyed the doubtful honors of kingship. Nevertheless, the writers accept this regular selection, but they add one because of the necessity of completing the time element. This is not altogether correct, however, for a reason that will shortly be noted.

It seems that the lives of those twenty-eight, and Coriantumr, the twenty-ninth, covered the whole period of the Jaredites in the new world. The inaccuracy suggested above comes from the fact that the last king on the approved list, Coriantor, lived in captivity all his life, and during part of that time Coriantumr ruled as king. As a matter of strict truth, therefore, two men are included here who lived practically the same time. This irregularity appears justified in view of the scarcity of information about Coriantor, particularly the time at which he died. The inclusion of Coriantumr makes the history complete. 

A. The Matter of Gaps in the Genealogy. 

An incidental matter is of interest in connection with the study of the Jaredite kings. It has been suggested above that there may have been times when no king at all ruled. 

That such may have been the case will doubtless be admitted by all. But is there reason to believe that it was so? A superficial reading would seem to justify the conclusion. 

But a more mature consideration casts much doubt upon it. 

In the register of kings given in Ether 1 there appear to be two such instances, from Heth to Aaron, and from Riplakish to Morianton. This assumption arises out of the fact that in all other instances the word "son" is used whereas in connection with those two the word "descendant" is employed, suggesting the lapse of time.

In Ether 10:1 and 31, however, it is made clear that in one of those instances there was no lapse of time at all. 

Aaron was son of Heth. Similarly, in Ether 11:11 Ethem is called the son of Ahah. Again "descendant" means "son," and in Ether 1:6 Ether, the son of Coriantor, (11:23) is designated as his descendant. In the case of Morianton, however, there might have been a break though there is good reason to doubt it. No other king is named for the time, and it is about the only case of the kind in the record. 

Moreover, Morianton fought as Riplakish would have fought -in opposition to the people. A careful reading of the text adds weight to the likelihood that Morianton was the son of Riplakish. 

B. Length of Reigns of the Jaredite Kings.

This question of possible breaks in the time is important only to the extent to which it would influence the length of time that each king ruled. Since no dates are given for the Jaredites, we must arrive indirectly, if at all, at a decision as to about when each monarch occupied the throne on the basis of the average length of the reigns of his predecessors. If the gaps were long, they would materially affect this average rule; if they were short, they would not. 

If there were no such gaps at all, the time element can be determined with some degree of accuracy. 

Shall we say that the twenty-nine kings covered the entire period of the Jaredites in the new world? The total number of years divided by twenty-nine would give an average reign of about fifty-five years. 

Now, lest someone think this is too long, let us look at a few facts.

Orihah, the first king, was probably the youngest son of Jared. It appears beyond dispute that in general the Jaredites followed the practice of passing the kingship from father to youngest son. This in itself would have tended to render the average length of the rules very long. 

Orihah begot Kib in his old age. (Ether 7:3) He was one of the youngest (if not the youngest) of thirty-one children. (v.2) Shule was born when Kib was very old. (v. 7) Sons and daughters were born to Shule in his old age, and Omer was one of them. (v. 26) Omer spent half his life in captivity, (8:4) yet he occupied the throne during two rebellions by his son Jared and the time between them and lived as king until Emer, one of his youngest sons, was grown

and able to succeed him. (9:15)

Much the same thing could be said of every king of the 

Jaredites. Only two or three other instances will be mentioned, however, since that will be sufficient. 

Emer reigned at least sixty-two years. (9:16) Coriantum, his son, lived 142 years. (v. 24) Com reigned fortynine years before Heth was born, and he was still king when Heth was old enough to murder him and take the throne. (vs. 26-27) 

In view of these facts it does not seem that fifty-five years was too long for the average Jaredite reign. 

II. The Kings as a Guide to the Study of the Jaredites. 

Just before the death of the original leaders, Jared and his brother, Orihah, Jared's son, was chosen to be king. His grandson, a man named Corihor, left the land of their first inheritance and drew many of the people away with him to the land of Nehor. (Ether 7:4)

The rightful king at that time was Kib, the father of Corihor, and the second ruler in regular descent. When 

Corihor, in Nehor, had gathered an army, he returned to the land of Moron, where the king dwelt, and took him captive. (7:5) 

Corihor was, of course, still established at Nehor, and it was from here that he went against his father Kib.

This gathering of an army by Corihor is a very interesting and somewhat puzzling circumstance. It gives rise here to an explanation that the interested reader will do well to keep in mind and examine critically from time to time since it is something of a key to the interpretation of much else that appears in the BOOK OF MORMON.

If the company that came to America numbered no more than one hundred at the beginning, the entire nation could hardly have counted more than four or five hundred in the days of Corihor, the son of the second king. Is the word "army" misleading? Is there a more accurate term that might have been used? Or could there have been an army in the sense in which we understand that term now? If so, where did the men come from?

An interesting parallel is found in the story of the group of men in the history of the Church of Jesus Christ of

Latter-day Saints known as Zion's Camp. This company of men is more than once designated an army though there were fewer than three hundred in the group. Could Corihor have had an "army" of two or three hundred? Interestingly enough, the language in both cases comes from the Prophet 

Joseph Smith.

A. A Key to the Understanding of the Record. 

The explanation to which reference is made above will be called into use frequently and should be given at this point. The writers are convinced that it aids greatly in making the BOOK OF MORMON consistent with known facts and in showing that it is consistent with itself throughout even in cases in which it appears to be at variance with both itself and known facts. 

Let us refer to another citation. In Ether 6:18 we read: "And it came to pass that they began to spread upon the face of the land, and to multiply and to till the earth; and they did wax strong in the land."

Here is a statement that upon first reading would seem to justify the impression that the people had become very numerous, and a casual reader would almost certainly get that understanding from it without a full knowledge of its setting. As a matter of fact, that seems not to have been the case at all. The paragraphs that follow (19-20) tell that the brother of Jared had twenty-two sons and daughters while

Jared had but twelve. Together their families numbered thirty-eight including fathers and mothers. The friends of 

Jared and his brother were in number twenty-two, (v. 16) "and they also began to have sons and daughters before they came to the promised land; and therefore they began to be many." Without the sons and daughters of those friends the company comprised about sixty. Surely forty would be a sufficient number to add for the increase, bringing the total up to one hundred. But suppose this number be doubled. Could there have been more than two hundred at the time when the people "began to spread upon the face of the land, and to wax strong in the land?"

The point is this: It appears very likely that the language of the text may be misunderstood as to the number of people and extent of land at any given time. It is only natural that we should read the record in terms of our own experience. We shall see whether this helps in the understanding of other difficult passages.

Another point is extremely interesting in connection with these facts that tends to prove, with other overwhelming evidence, that Joseph Smith was not the author of the BOOK OF MORMON, that, indeed, it is beyond the scope of any mortal mind. 

In Ether 2:41 we read this statement: "Go to and gather together thy flocks, both male and female, of every kind; and also of the seed of the earth of every kind; and thy 

FAMILIES; and also Jared thy brother and his FAMILY; and also thy friends and their families, and the friends of 

Jared and their families."

Is there something significant in the mention of the brother of Jared's "families"? Apparently there is, for later, in the enumeration given above, we discover that the brother of Jared was father of twenty-two sons and daughters whereas Jared had only twelve. Twenty-two in two families would have been practically in the ratio of twelve for one family. He might, indeed, have had three families. 

Would any man, with as much on his mind as the writer of such a book would have, remember such a detail to confirm it later? Or, for that matter, why should it come up in the first place since it has no significance in the history? 

Another point should be mentioned. If Joseph Smith had written the BOOK OF MORMON, is it not probable that he would have avoided the obvious problems presented by the matter of the army to which reference has been made already? It would have been so much easier to keep away from all questionable issues.

B. The Story Resumed. 

While he was in captivity, Kib, the second king, begot 

Shule. Shule later defeated his brother Corihor and returned the kingdom to their father. (Ether 7:9-10) Shule later became king. Corihor's son Noah rebelled against Shule, gave battle, defeated him, and drove him from the land of their first inheritance of which he (Noah) took possession, and in a later battle he captured Shule and carried him captive to Moron. (7:17)

The importance of the foregoing facts can hardly be overstated. Especial attention will now be called to them. 

It is almost certain that Moron was the landing place of the Jaredites in the new world, the place of their first inheritance. This is the view of the writers of this book. It

is the opinion behind the cross reference on verses 16 and 17 of the seventh chapter of Ether, and it is also the belief of 

Elder B. H. Roberts as given in the New Witness for 

God. 14

Another interpretation is possible from verses 16 and 18 that is in a sense contradictory to the one just given. 

One might gather that Moron, instead of being the land of the first inheritance, was near it. This slight difference in interpretation is of no importance in fact. 

Note the significance of the following declaration: "Now the land of Moron, where the king dwelt, was near the land which is called Desolation by the Nephites." (Ether 7:6)

Does this mean that Moron and Desolation were different lands? This is probably not the meaning, at least in a certain sense. Both were parts of the land of the Jaredites. 

Moron was the capital, and Desolation was rendered desolate by the people. The explanation may be that only part of the land was wasted, the southern part. There were, of course, many small lands and cities within the whole territory occupied by the Jaredites of which Moron and that later called Desolation by the Nephites were two. 

It is worthy of note that up to the present nothing is given in the record of any migration or extended movement whatever. The conclusion seems unavoidable that to the time of the fourth king the Jaredites were still living in their old home, the place of their first inheritance. If they moved at all, it was subsequent to the reign of Omer and either between then and the beginning of the last war or after the beginning of that war.

A quotation from the pen of that great thinker and writer, Elder B. H. Roberts, will suffice to express the views of practically all, if not all, writers with respect to this matter-the migration of a people from one land to another thousands of miles distant. "It is evident that the land of Moron, north of Desolation, was the chief center of Jaredite civilization, and the principal seat of government from the time of their first landing in America.... to the last civil war which ended in the destruction of the nation in the sixth century B. C. The evidence of the foregoing statement is seen in the fact that 

14. Roberts, B. H., New Witness for God, Vol. II, pp. 140-5

Moron is the land of their first inheritance and also that nearly all their great civil wars throughout their national existence, down to, and including the last, raged in and about Moron except the last great battles of the last war which were fought about the hill Ramah, the Cumorah of the Nephites. This fixes the center of Jaredite Civilization for a period of some sixteen centuries in Central America.” 15

If it be granted that no northward migration took place before the last half dozen years of the nation's life, we shall have to examine closely the reasons for thinking that one occurred at that late date.

After numerous troubles king Shule turned the kingdom over to his son Omer who was thus the fourth king in regular descent. No definite statement is given as to the place from which he reigned.

It is almost a foregone conclusion, however, that Omer lived and reigned in Moron. Here are the reasons for such a declaration in addition to the fact that Moron was the capital city.

Corihor, the son of Kib, the second king, led followers (from Moron?) to Nehor. (Ether 7:4) He later returned to Moron, the king's capital, and took his father Kib into captivity (presumably to Nehor). Shule, Corihor's brother, fought with Corihor and defeated him after which he returned the kingdom to their father. (7:9) For this act Kib conferred the kingdom upon Shule. (v. 10) Shule's nephew

Noah, the son of Corihor, rebelled against him and drove him from his throne in the "land of their fathers' first inheritance."

The land of their fathers' first inheritance was, of course, Moron. So Noah drove Shule from Moron. 

In Moron, Noah set himself up as king. (v. 16) Again

Noah gave battle to Shule and took him captive to Moron. (v. 17) Shule's sons killed Noah (v. 18) and gave part of the kingdom back to their father. Cohor, one of the sons of 

Noah, still retained the rest of the nation. (v. 20) Shule killed Cohor in battle and regained control of all the kingdom.

Back on the throne again, beyond any question in Moron, Shule was the sole monarch. It was during that time that he begot Omer. Omer became king, and it seems 

15. Op. cit.

perfectly clear that he lived and ruled in Moron, the traditional capital of the nation. 

Jared, one of Omer's sons, rebelled against his father and drew away half the kingdom to Heth where he set himself up as king. (Ether 8:3). Then Jared went to battle against his father and took him into captivity. 

It appears reasonable to assume that Jared took Omer to Heth where he had established himself. 

Jared's brothers succeeded in liberating their father. (vs. 4-6) He at once took up his interrupted rule. Jared pined over the loss of his power and finally, with the connivance of his wicked daughter, bargained for the head of his father Omer. (vs. 8-18)

Observe now the language of the explanation by Moroni, the Nephite condenser of the Jaredite history. "And the Lord warned Omer in a dream that he should depart out of the land: Wherefore Omer departed out of the land with his family, and traveled many days, and came over and passed by the hill of Shim, and came over by the place where the Nephites were destroyed, and from thence eastward, and came to a place which was called Ablom." (Ether 9:3) 

Everyone will recognize at once the problem that is presented by the foregoing statement. If King Omer, in fleeing eastward, from Moron, the capital of their first inheritance, passed by the place where the Nephites were destroyed, where was all this taking place?

All students of the BOOK OF MORMON agree that at that time the Jaredites were still in their old homeland. No one has ever supposed, so far as the present writers know, that they moved northward until hundreds of years later. 

Can such a view be supported by other facts in the recordthe view that the Jaredites were then in the land of their first inheritance and that the Nephites, consequently, were destroyed in that land? Is such a view in harmony with the rest of the text? This is indeed an intriguing thought, and it demands the earnest consideration of all thoughful students of the BOOK OF MORMON. It is treated in greater detail later.

While Omer was in Ablom by the seashore, Jared was slain by his son-in-law Akish who made himself ruler. Akish became engaged in a devastating civil war with his sons

that lasted until all the Jaredites were killed but thirty souls (Ether 9:10-12) and those who fled to Ablom with Omer and subsequently. (vs. 3, 9)

Thus it appears that as late as the days of the fourth king, perhaps between 225 and 250 years after the landing, allowing fifty-five to sixty years for each king, the Jaredi es, so far as population was concerned, had to start all over again. And there may be a reasonable doubt, from the language, that there were then as many as at the beginning. 

Is it not clear that they were not numerous regardless of how many there had been previously?

Omer returned from Ablom, probably after a number of years, and Emer succeeded him as king. (9:15) Emer ruled righteously and turned the kingdom over to his son Coriantum.

It is in connection with Coriantum that another very intriguing problem arises. 

Ether 9:23 has this to say: "Coriantum did walk in the steps of his father, and did build many mighty cities, and administer that which was good unto his people all his days." 

In view of the possibility, strongly supported by the foregoing facts, that the Jaredites might have become nearly extinct in the days of King Omer, the grandfather of Coriantum, this building of many mighty cities is somewhat hard to understand. Does it seem to present evidence of inconsistency in the record? The authors think not and make some suggestions with regard to this matter. 

1. There might have been more people surviving with 

Omer than the context suggests. Yet, the record definitely says (Ether 9:3) that only Omer and his household fled to

Ablom. These were joined later, of course, by Nimrah, the son of Akish, and a few companions. (Ether 9:9) This was a "small number," and no further indication is given as to the actual count. 

2. There is also a probability that there were other

Jaredites than those at Ablom and Heth. What was the relation of Heth to Moron? Were the people of Moron, and perhaps other lands the cities, involved in that civil war? 

If this was true, there can be no question about the practical wiping out of the nation. If, however, there were other people under other rules, or others who refused to join the struggle, the large population necessary to the building of

many mighty cities would have been available.

Furthermore, if there were other Jaredites, this fact would very materially affect conclusions to be presented later in this book, and the reader would do well to keep this in mind. For this reason everyone should make a very careful study before coming to any hard and fast decision on this important issue.

The facts relative to the problem will be presented again briefly.

The text seems to indicate that the people were living in Heth and Moron though there may have been many other cities, and probably were. However, no others are mentioned at this time.

Jared rebelled against his father Omer and drew many people with him to Heth. "And it came to pass that he did flatter many people, because of his cunning words, UNTIL HE HAD GAINED THE HALF OF THE KING- 

DOM." (Ether 8:2) If this means, as it seems to do, half the Jaredites, there is but one conclusion possible. In the civil strife that followed, he was defeated and forced to give up his stolen liberties. (8:6) Still later, through a conspiracy engineered by his daughter, he once more came into power and "DID ÖVERTHROW THE KINGDOM OF 

OMER." Was this the entire kingdom of the Jaredites? It would seem so.

It was then that the Lord warned Omer in a dream that he should flee to the east seashore for safety. After th⚫ departure of Omer and his household Akish slew his father-inlaw Jared and made himself king. It was then that the disastrous civil war began. 

Note now the language of Ether 9:12 which presents the problem. "AND THERE BEGAN TO BE A WAR BE- TWEEN THE SONS OF AKISH AND AKISH, WHICH LASTED FOR THE SPACE OF MANY YEARS, YEA, UNTO THE DESTRUCTION OF NEARLY ALL THE PEOPLE OF THE KINGDOM, YEA, EVEN ALL, SAVE IT WERE THIRTY SOULS, AND THEY WHO FLED WITH THE HOUSE OF OMER." 

Does this not indicate that the Jaredites were killed, at least all who were within the knowledge of the historian, except thirty, those who fled with Omer, and the few friends of Nimrah?

3. There is always the possibility, as suggested more and more frequently as time goes on, that there were other people in the land than those of whom the BOOK OF MORMON is a record. These could have been of two classes, those who at no time had come within the knowledge of the historians and those who might have moved outside that knowledge. If Zarahemla had not been accidentally, or providentially, discovered, we should have had no knowledge of its existence.

With respect to the first class, those people who might have lived outside the knowledge of the historians of the 

BOOK OF MORMON, there is little to be said. That such people could have inhabited the land no one will deny. 

We must, however, take into consideration a statement in 

II Nephi 1:3: "And behold, it is wisdom that this land should be kept as yet from the knowledge of other nations; for behold, many nations would overrun the land, that there would be no place for an inheritance."

The next verse contains the promise that if they would keep the commandments of the Lord, they would be kept from the knowledge of all other nations and possess the land.

It might also be pertinently asked whether immigrants could not have come into this new world from other nations without bringing this land to the knowledge of those nations. Historians are certain that Norsemen came to these shores hundreds of years before Columbus. Might not others have likewise come? Yet, those remaining in their homelands could only have supposed they had perished. The record itself tells of many who set sail in the sea in ships and did not return. They might easily have peopled other lands, and perhaps did, and yet those lans, the islands, remained unknown to the main body of Nephites.

If there were many contacts between the old and new worlds throughout the centuries, bringing people of differing cultures and periods, there could well have been increases in population of which we have no account. This might also explain other things that are difficult to understand. We may not, perhaps, say that no one came to this world, but we are almost certain that no one went from the new world to the old, bearing information of the civilization here.

As to the possibility that some people moved away from the central land to adjacent and remote places, there can be no question. Indeed, the record tells of many who did thus move away as will be shown subsequently in the story of the Nephites. The same might have been true of the Jaredites, and some who previously departed might have returned to swell the dwindling population. 

4. A fourth possible explanation for the increased population of the Jaredites in the period under discussion lies in the possibility that the time element was longer than that provided in the present calculations. But if we add some years to the earlier period of the history, we shall almost certainly have to deduct a corresponding number from the later centuries, for we know with considerable certainty the dates for the beginning and the end. 

5. Again, it may be that our meager information may offer the explanation for these difficult passages.

Does the fact that the Jaredite story is tremendously shortened supply the answer to our uncertainties? This may well be, but to what extent can it affect the question of time? 

Whether we know much or little, does it not seem that 

Omer and Coriantum, grandfather and grandson, would have had to come at a certain time with respect to each other and the whole history? And is not the royal genealogy given in Ether 1 a good indication of that relationship? 

6. Finally, we come again to the explanation offered previously: That the language may be misunderstood in the light of our own experience. "AND IT CAME TO

PASS THAT CORIANTUM DID WALK IN THE STEPS OF HIS FATHER, AND DID BUILD MANY MIGHTY 

CITIES." The words do seem to suggest a tremendous and extensive activity, and this is in part confirmed by verses 16 to 20 wherein we read of the wealth and commerce of the Jaredites. Verse 26 makes it clear that the people had again spread over all the land.

Was it a large land? If it was, how could the people in 120 years or so have come from practical extinction to sufficient numbers to spread all over it? And what of the many mighty cities? Does it mean MANY and MIGHTY in the sense in which we understand these terms in 1939? 

There is something very much to the point in Ether 

9:16. "And the Lord began again to take the curse from off the land, and the house of Emer did prosper exceed-

ingly under the reign of Emer; and in the space of sixty and two years they had become exceeding strong, insomuch that they became exceeding rich." Incidentally, this period of sixty-two years is a long one, in keeping with the long reigns of the Jaredite kings, and relatively near that already established as an average, fifty-five to sixty years. 

We may not be able as yet to explain how a practically extinct people (if the nation was nearly wiped out) could in sixty-two years become exceedingly strong. It might, however, be very pertinent to inquire what was the standard of judgment of the time.

But the purpose of the present work is to determine whether the BOOK OF MORMON is consistent, whether it tells the same story throughout, regardless of time, and not to strain at interpretations that in the light of our own knowledge may not be altogether clear.

Com followed his father Coriantum as king. Nothing is recorded that happened in his reign. In the days of the eighth king, Heth, the son of Com, a plague of serpents spread over the land and drove the flocks toward the land southward "which was called by the Nephites Zarahemla." Observe that the only movement mentioned is to the south. It is also very interesting to note that the land of the Jaredites and Zarahemla, the land of the Nephites, were apparently very near each other.

A terrible condition now arose. A dire famine followed the scourge of serpents. So severe was the famine that the people were forced to eat the carcases of the fallen animals. (v. 34) King Heth and all his household perished except 

Shez. (10:1) Shez began to "build up again a broken people." (v. 1)

Here again, as late as the days of the ninth king, is evidence of some depopulation. That it was severe is fairly clear; that it was tremendous or far-reaching is far from certain. At any rate, the Jaredites were "a broken people." 

Shez reigned in righteousness and "did build up many cities upon the face of the land, and the people began again to spread over all the face of the land." (v. 4)

From the fact that they had to begin again to spread over the land we might well believe that their recent trials had made considerable inroads into their numbers. 

How big a land did the people inhabit in their broken condition? Is there a discrepancy here between the language of "many cities" and "all the face of the land” and the fact that the people were broken? If there is, is it inherent in the text, or do we perhaps read it into the story? 

Is the difficulty in the record or in our interpretation of it? 

This question is worthy of a great deal of thoughtful consideration.

Riplakish, the son of Shez, was a wicked ruler. Because of his exactions the people rose against him in rebellion. (10:8) He was killed, and his family was driven from the land. Sometime later a descendant of Riplakish, Morianton by name, came back into the land with an army of outcasts and set himself up as king. (v. 9) ..In a war that lasted many years, and was a bitter one, Morianton established his sovereignty. But he was a benevolent ruler and “did build up many cities.” (v. 12) Under his reign the people prospered.

Kim, Morianton's son, and Levi, Kim's son, lived in captivity under Kim's unnamed brother. Finally Levi threw off the yoke of bondage and became king in his own right. (vs. 17-19)

During the reign of Lib a great city was built at the narrow neck of land which separated the land southward from the land northward. (v. 20) The land southward was full of game, and the land northward was full of inhabitants. (v. 21)

The present writers believe that the land at the narrow neck, wherever it was, was the land of Bountiful, and the reasons for this view will in due time be given. But the land south of the narrow neck was the Nephite land of 

Zarahemla. (Ether 9:31) The land northward was, of

course, the home of the Jaredites and included the lands of Moron, Heth, Nehor, and many others. It, or part of it, was later known to the Nephites as Desolation. 

There is, however, some little confusion or uncertainty surrounding these details. In Ether 7:6 we read that Moron was near the land called Desolation by the Nephites. This seems to suggest that it was not properly a part of Desolation, but there is extreme likelihood that it may be thus considered though this is open to question. 

This period, covering the reign of Lib, was probably the high point in the national history of the Jaredites. Ether 

10:27 gives a splendid statement of the accomplishments of the people.
