The years following the reign of Lib were years of sorrow and civil war. Hearthom, Aaron, Amnigaddah, and 

Coriantum lived in captivity. (Ether 10:31)

Com, the son of Coriantum, drew away half the kingdom and in a civil struggle later took the remainder of it from Amgid. (v. 32) He was constantly engaged in fighting robbers against whom he did not prevail. (vs. 33-34) phets Warn ne morate ニクニー ​Prophets were sent in the days of Com to warn the people that destruction was coming upon them. (11:1)

Shiblom became king and engaged in "an exceeding great war in all the land" with his brother. (v. 4) Shiblom's brother had all the prophets put to death. (v. 5) But the people remained wicked.

One might be justified in asking how big a land it was

over which Shiblom directed one side of a civil war. Is it likely that it was a whole continent, or even a considerable portion of one? The writers believe this is important. "And they hearkened not unto the voice of the Lord, because of their wicked combinations; wherefore, there began to be wars and contentions in all the land, and also many famines and pestilences, insomuch that there was a great destruction, such an one as never had been known upon the face of the earth." (v. 7)

Whether this actually means that this was the greatest. disaster that had ever come upon the people is not exactly certain. Perhaps it means the greatest destruction upon the land, the new world. Nor does it necessarily mean that the nation came nearer to extinction than ever before. The probability is that there were more people destroyed than at any previous time because there were more people to be destroyed, for by that time there could have been a mighty nation indeed.

Here again we see, do we not, the necessity for harmonizing the language with the record and with known facts? 

Allowance must surely be made for figurative and expressive language. A destruction such as had never before been known upon the face of the whole earth could hardly have visited the land at that time because the destruction at the time of the Flood was the greatest possible. 

Shiblom was slain, and his son Seth lived in captivity. 

His son Ahah gained the kingdom. He was a wicked man. 

After a period for which no details are given, a descendant of Ahah became king. He too was wicked. In his time more prophets came. 

Moron, Ethem's son, succeeded his father. He ruled in wickedness. A robber leader gave battle to him and gained half the kingdom. Moron then overthrew him and regained supremacy. A descendant of the brother of Jared defeated 

Moron and kept him in captivity. While he was in captivity, Moron begot Coriantor who lived in captivity all his life. 

Wickedness was rampant; prophets came and were rejected. (vs. 20-22) 

While he was in captivity, Coriantor begot Ether, the last prophet of the Jaredites. (v. 23)

CHAPTER FOUR THE LAST STRUGGLE I. Tentative Conclusions Presented. 

Of the three phases of this study that are of the greatest importance-landing places, location, size, and relationship of lands, and size and extent of populations-some conclusions will now be offered regarding two on the basis of facts presented thus far, such conclusions to be altered from time to time if and as information warrants. 

With respect to the size of the lands of the Jaredites, does it not appear fairly evident that the territory occupied by them was relatively small? To the time of Ether and

Coriantumr there seems to be nothing that would suggest any migration whatever. In one instance a movement to the southward is mentioned.

There is always the possibility that there was an imperceptible movement or progress to the north year after year that escaped the notice of the historians, or of which they took no notice. But there is nothing, seemingly, in the record to give the impression that such was the case. And subsequent developments make such a view practically out of the question. It seems very evident that the Jaredites, at least to the time of the last bitter war, lived in a comparatively small place in close proximity to the place of their first landing. This is discussed fully in later chapters. 

And as to the size of the Jaredite nation little need be said beyond the information already given. The original small number must have increased rapidly. But there were few times in the history of the people when war and destruction were not going on. Evidence has already been given to show that at different times, some relatively late in the nation's existence, they were almost wiped out or materially diminished in numbers by war and plagues. The

question of population, however, is treated in a separate chapter. 

For the present it need only be said that, on the basis of the information thus far given, there seems to be reason to believe that the nation was relatively small. 

But let us return to the story.

II. The Closing Years. 

VHANSEN 

During the early years of his reign Coriantumr was engaged constantly in fighting. (Ether 13:15-22) His enemies of that period are not named. It was at that time that the Lord sent Ether to him to say that if he would repent with all his household, the Lord would give unto him the kingdom and spare the people. (v. 20) Ether was instructed to inform Coriantumr that if he would not humble himself, he alone would live to the end of the struggle and see another nation of people possess the land. (v. 21) 

At this time Shared arose to lead the forces against Coriantumr. For a number of years these two mighty men fought bitterly with varying success. In the end Shared was killed, and Coriantumr was severely wounded. (vs. 

23-31) After a two-year armistice the war was resumed with Gilead, the brother of Shared, opposing Coriantumr. (Ether 14.3) Many were the men who fell in the conflicts. (vs. 4-5) 

And now we come to one of the most significant passages in the whole book to the student of geography. "And he (Gilead) came forth to the land of Moron,

THE LAST STRUGGLE 

41 and placed himself upon the throne of Coriantumr.” (14:6)

Was this not beyond any question the same Moron of which we have heard so much? The cross reference makes this very clear. It was the capital of the first Jaredite king, and it was likewise the capital of the last! Thus for 1,600 years the Jaredites remained in the very place in which they began as a nation.

What shall be said, then, of the view of many readers of the BOOK OF MORMON that there was an extensive migration or transfer of people from one land to another thousands of miles distant? The first and last kings ruled in the same place!

It will be advisable now to follow in some detail the movements of the last few years. They can be determined with considerable accuracy, and they are extremely illuminating. 

Lib, Gilead's high priest, killed Gilead and took the throne for himself. (Ether 14:9-10) During his first year as king, Coriantumr came against him in Moron. Lib fled with his army to the (east) seashore and there made a stand. 

Coriantumr was defeated and forced to flee to the wilderness of Akish. From there he was driven to the plains of 

Agosh. As he fled, Coriantumr took with him all the people from the places through which he passed. In the plains of 

Agosh, Lib was killed, but his brother continued the struggle which was a devastating one. Coriantumr continued to retreat until he again found himself by the east seashore. 

So great was the slaughter that the bodies of the dead covered all the land, and the stench of the dead went forth until it troubled the living day and night. (vs. 9-26)

How shall we explain this statement that the dead covered all the land? How big a land was it? Does it mean the whole continent? Or does it refer to the fields in which the activity took place?

For three days on the seashore the battle raged. Coriantumr was victorious, and the armies of Shiz, the brother of Lib, fled to the land of Corihor, killing along the way all who would not join. With Shiz in Corihor, Coriantumr pitched his camp in the valley of Shurr at the hill Comnor and sent forth a challenge to battle. Three times the forces of Shiz advanced to the contest; twice they were repulsed. 

On the third attempt the army of Shiz met with some degree of success. Coriantumr was severely wounded and

was carried from the struggle as though dead. The battle began to go against Corianthumr, but so great had been the slaughter of men, women, and children that Shiz did not press the struggle longer. (vs. 26-31)

When Coriantumr recovered, he found that two million of his men had been killed as well as their women and children. Attempts to bring about peace failed, and the war was continued. Coriantumr was again overwhelmed and fled to the waters of Ripliancum, (a word that meant large). 

Next day the conflict went on, and Coriantumr was again so seriously wounded that he fainted from loss of blood.

Nevertheless, his men pushed the fight and drove Shiz southward to Ogath. (Ether 15:1-10)

The army of Coriantumr, after traveling southward, made camp at the hill Ramah. "And," says Moroni, "it was that same hill where my father Mormon did hide up the records unto the Lord, which were sacred." (v. 11) 

According to the statement of Mormon (Mormon 6:6) it was in the hill Cumorah that he hid the records. The hill 

Cumorah of the Nephites was thus the hill Ramah of the Jaredites.

This demands the patient consideration of every thoughtful student of the BOOK OF MORMON! 

Ether at the "ave CXHansen

The hill Ramah, if we have followed the story carefully, and interpreted the facts correctly, was very near the original home of the Jaradites, but south of it. 

Does this mean that the Jaredites were destroyed, then,

THE LAST STRUGGLE 

43 in the old homeland or south of it? This seems to be in perfect harmony with the important details of Ether 9:3.

III. The Final Battle. 

The Jaredites had reached the twilight of their career. 

For four years, while yet encamped at the hill Ramah, the opposing armies recruited until everyone in the land had come to one standard or the other. Then "they did march forth one against another to battle; and they fought all that day, and conquered not." (Ether 15:15) 

From his place of safety in a cave Ether watched the fateful contest, recording in detail the events he saw. From day to day he followed the struggle so closely that he was able to give the actual number of men left on each side toward the end. His is the testimony of an eye witness. 

At the end of the sixth day only Coriantumr was left alive of the combatants. (15:16-35)

These events took place near and at the hill Ramah, apparently, as will be shown in the next chapter, in the homeland of the Jaredites. It is a fascinating yet pitiful tale, one that men might with profit read again and again. 

In closing this chapter the authors desire once more to call attention to the fact that there appears to be not a line leading to the conclusion that the Jaredites marched away from their old home. No one supposes that they left. it till toward the end of their existence. Where is the evidence that they migrated during the last ten or twelve years? The only direction mentioned in the movements was southward. They fled southward from Ripliancum, the great water, to Ogath and Ramah. (Ether 15:1-11)

CHAPTER FIVE  I. Evidence of Moron and Associated Places. 

Since the lands of the Jaredites and Nephites were so closely related, as will appear beyond any question from the study of the text, it will be helpful, not only in connection with the study of the early colony, but of the Mulekites and Nephites as well, to enquire more fully into the extent of the territory occupied by them. 

Some of the material of this chapter has already been given, but the authors feel it advisable to present it here again in order to assemble in one place as much as possible of the information on this phase of the subject. 

The first item will be a brief review of the principal movements of the Jaredites in the new world. 

It is accepted for the purposes of the present study that they began their national life on the eastern coast of Central America or Mexico. In all probability their first home was 

Moron. The first division among them took some to the land of Nehor.

The other places mentioned in the entire Jaredite story are the hill Ephraim, the land of Heth, the hill Shim, the land of Ablom, the plains of Heshlon, the valley of Gilgal, the wilderness of Akish, the plains of Agosh, the valley of Corihor, the valley of Shurr, the hill Comnor, the waters of Ripliancum, the hill Ramah, and the place Ogath. These may be very conveniently divided into two groups according to the time element involved. Nehor, Ephraim, Heth, Shim, and Ablom are associated with the reigns of the first four kings of the Jaredites, and all are mentioned as in close proximity to Moron. (Ether 7:4, 9; 8-2; 9-3) The others are given in connection with the last eight or nine years of the nation's existence. And they too are associated with Moron in a manner that leaves little doubt as to their

having been near the landing place of the Jaredites or a part of it. (Ether 13:28-29; 14:3, 6, 11, 14-15, 28) And in this connection it is worthy of note that the east seashore was easily and quickly accessible from these points, both in the first instance and in the last. (9:3; 14:12-13, 26) 

Does this offer good reason to believe that the Jaredites landed on the eastern coast? How else could the eastern seashore have been so near their first home, the land of Moron?

The foregoing facts also seem to suggest that the people lived and died in a relatively restricted area. 

A. Was Moron on the Seashore?

An incidental problem claims our attention for a moment. If Moron was the landing place, how could Omer flee many days eastward from it? And how could he have passed two hills-Shim and Cumorah? Here is a suggestion. The Jaredites need not have landed on the shore of the ocean, or stopped permanently there. They might easily have gone some distance inland, either following the course of a large stream or overland. 

II. The Day-and-a-Half's Journey. 

The conclusion that the main activities of the Jaredites were confined to a comparatively small place is in part confirmed by other facts.

It will be necessary to leave the Jaredite record for a moment and go to the Nephite. But the connection between the lands of the two peoples is so close that we cannot separate them. To know the one is to become acquainted with the other. To study the one is to study the other. 

Alma, in the twenty-second chapter of his writings, verses 27 to 30, makes it clear that the Nephites and Jaredites lived in the same place. This agrees with the declaration in Ether 7:6: "Now the land of Moron, where the king dwelt, was near the land which is called Desolation by the Nephites." 

How big was this land?

The BOOK OF MORMON contains a simple yet clear cut statement which seems to point to the fact that it was comparatively small, at least in one direction. "And now, it was only the distance of a day and a half's

journey for a Nephite, on the line Bountiful and the land 

Desolation, from the east sea to the west." (Alma 22:32)

This is in complete agreement with another text, Helaman 4:7: "And there they did fortify against the Lamanites, from the west sea even to the east; it being a day's journey for a Nephite, on the line which they had fortified and stationed their armies to defend their north country." 

And yet this is not surprising. Indeed it is what we should expect. Attention has already been called to this narrow country in the story of the Jaredites. "And they built a great city by the narrow neck of land, by the place where the sea divides the land. And they did preserve the land southward for a wilderness to get game. And the whole face of the land northward was covered with inhabitants." (Ether 10:20-21) No one can read the history of the Jaredites without being conscious of the nearness of the people to the narrow neck of land.

III. Ramah and Cumorah. 

The next bit of material will be a brief recapitulation of the incidents in the struggle between the good king 

Omer and his rebellious son Jared.

Omer was the fourth king of the Jaredites. In order of their succession the first four were Orihah, (Ether 6:27) 

Kib, (7:3) Shule, (7:7, 10) and Omer. (8:1) Omer in all probability lived at Moron since that was the capital of the 

Jaredite kings.

Let us connect up again the principal developments in the lives of these rulers.

Corihor, the son of Kib, the second king, led followers (from Moron?) to Nehor (7:4) and later went to Moron, the king's capital, and took his father Kib into captivity, presumably to Nehor. Shule, Corihor's brother, fought with Corihor and defeated him after which he returned the kingdom to their father. (7:9) For this support Kib conferred the kingdom (Moron, of course) upon Shule. (7:10) 

Shule's nephew Noah, a son of Corihor, rebelled against him and drove him from his throne in the "land of their fathers' first inheritance." That was, of course, Moron. 

And there Noah set himself up as king. (7:16) Again Noah gave battle to Shule and captured him. He then took 

Shule captive to Moron (7:17) where his throne was. 

Shule's sons killed Noah (v. 18) and gave back to their

father part of the kingdom. Cohor, one of the sons of Noah, ruled over the other part. (v. 20) Shule killed Cohor in battle and regained full control of the kingdom. It is clear that Shule was again in Moron. And he begat Omer who reigned in his stead. Does it not, therefore, seem beyond doubt that Omer lived and reigned at Moron, the original home of his people? The significance of this fact cannot be too much stressed, and its significance at once becomes clear. 

Jared, Omer's son, rebelled against his father. Since he could not longer live at Moron, he fled with his followers to Heth, (Ether 8:2) dividing the kingdom again. He went to war against his father and took him captive. Where would he take him but to Heth? Jared's brothers liberated their father and returned his kingdom (Moron) to him. (vs. 5-6) Jared plotted against his father's life so that 

Ömer had to flee for safety. (Ether 9:3) 

Leaving his kingdom (Moron, beyond any doubt), Omer fled with his household to the land of Ablom by the east seashore. (v. 3) Here again, incidentally, we have reason to believe that the Jaredites landed on the east. 

In his flight to the east seashore from Moron, Omer traveled many days and passed the hill Shim and the place where the Nephites were later destroyed. One can hardly help wondering how Omer traveled from Moron, the landing place of his people, the thousands of miles to the eastern part of the United States in "many days," or whether, indeed, he did make that long journey. If he did make such a journey, why did he do it? And where is the reason in the record to believe that he did? This would all be easily explained, of course, if there were evidence that the Jaredites landed on the eastern coast of northern United States, but no one suggests this.

IV. The Matter of Northward Journeys. 

At the risk of devoting unnecessary time and space to a subject that seems clear already, the authors refer again, though briefly, to the question whether the Jaredites moved northward any appreciable distance during the closing years of their race.

We are still trying to determine whether the record tells where the Jaredites were destroyed, whether in what is now eastern United States or in the land of their first inheritance.

In Ether 14:15 we read: "And Coriantumr had taken all the people with him as he fled before Lib in that quarter of the land whither he fled." This is sometimes interpreted. to mean that Coriantumr took all the people and fled northward toward or into what is now the United States. 

That such is not the intent seems to be clear from this declaration: "Wherefore, they were for the space of four years gathering together the people, that they might receive all the strength which it was possible that they might receive." (Ether 15:14)

This gathering, of course, took place after the one mentioned above, and this was after the southward flight: (Ether 15:10) It was the last gathering of the people. But the fact that they had to be gathered at that time shows, does it not, that they had not been gathered before? They did not march after this gathering began but remained in the same place. They were then at the hill Ramah, the hill that was between Moron and Ablom by the seashore, Moron, the capital city of the land of their first inheritance. The first citation obviously means, the writers believe, that in the little local movements Coriantumr took the people with him to keep them from the wrath of Shiz against whom a cry had gone up from all the land.

Does all this show that the Jaredites of the record lived in a relatively small land? Or, if it be admitted that they scattered over a large country, did they not return to the original home to die? Is there anything here to lead to the conclusion that they traveled thousands of miles as a people only to die in another land? Can such a contention be made to fit into the scheme apparently provided in the 

BOOK OF MORMON? 

V. Ether in the Cave. 

There is yet much to be said.

The apparently obscure and little-regarded position of Ether during the last struggles of his people is very pertinent. It seems to confirm the view that no change in territory took place at least during the last twelve years. 

In the early years of Coriantumr's reign Ether preached repentance to the Jaredites. His exhortations had such power that the people drove him from their midst. 

Verse 13 of chapter 13 reports the important fact that

Ether hid himself in the cavity of a rock "by day, and b night he went forth viewing the things which should come upon the people." Verse 14 repeats this fundamental statement and adds the tremendously significant detail that while he lived in the cavity of the rock, he "made the remainder of this record." "This record," of course, was the 

Jaredite history on the twenty-four gold plates found by the people of Limhi, the record translated by Mosiah II and abridged by Moroni. 

Verse 18 mentions Ether's first year in the cave. Verse 

24 contains a reference to a fourth year. Two more years elapsed according to verse 31. Chapter 14, verse 7, tells of two more; verse 11, of one; and chapter 15, verse 14, of four.

This period of from eleven to thirteen years covered the careers of Coriantumr's unnamed enemies, of Shared, Gilead, Lib, and Shiz, all the enemies of Coriantumr. Our first-hand knowledge of all the things that happened comes from Ether who viewed them as an eye witness. 

At no time, therefore, did the war take the participants more than a short distance from the vicinity of the cave, or Ether could not have gone out and returned in safety before day. All the movements of the contending armies were sufficiently restricted and close enough that the prophet could observe them at night and then return to the comparative safety of the cave.

The language seems clearly to indicate that Ether was in the same cave all the time, a period of about twelve years. 

If, of course, this was not the case, it might have some bearing on the conclusions. It must be remembered, however, that it was probably in the cave to which Ether fled at first that he finished the record. (Ether 13:14) 66 ... and he hid himself in the cavity of a rock by day, and by night he went forth viewing the things which should come upon the people. And as he dwelt in the cavity of a rock he made the remainder of this record, viewing the destructions which came upon the people, by night." (Ether 13:13-14) "Wherefore, it came to pass that in the first year that

Ether dwelt in the cavity of a rock, there were many people who were slain by the sword of those secret combinations, fighting against Coriantumr that they might obtain the

kingdom." (v. 18) "And it came to pass that Coriantumr repented not, neither his household, neither the people; and the wars ceased not; and they sought to kill Ether, but he fled from before them and hid again in the cavity of the rock." (v. 22) "And the Lord spake unto Ether, and said unto him: 

Go forth. And he went forth, and beheld that the words of the Lord had all been fulfilled; and he finished his record; (and the hundredth part I have not written) and he hid them in a manner that the people of Limhi did find them." (15:33) 

The people of Limhi found the twenty-four gold plates when they attempted to locate Zarahemla and get help. It seems impossible to read the story and believe that they were gone from Nephi any great length of time. Yet, they found the battle-ground of the Jaredites. Did they travel thousands of miles? Does it not seem that the battle-ground of the Jaredites (and, of course, Nephites) was relatively near Nephi?

There is another phase of this matter that should be touched upon for a moment. While watching the final battle from his cave in the rocks or from whatever other vantage point he sought, Ether was able to follow the contest with such great detail that he knew how many men were left toward the end. "And on the morrow they fought again: and when the night came they had all fallen by the sword save it were fifty and two of the people of Coriantumr, and sixty and nine of the people of Shiz . . . . And on the morrow they fought again . . . . And when the night came there were thirty and two of the people of Shiz and twenty and seven of the people of Coriantumr." (Ether 15:23-25) "And it came to pass that when Coriantumr had leaned upon his sword, that he rested a little, he smote off the head of Shiz. And it came to pass that Coriantumr fell to the earth, and became as if he had no life." (vs. 31-32)

Such great particularity is certainly the observation of an eye witness.

Yet, however convincing the foregoing appears to be, we must not forget that the Book of Ether is tremendously shortened from the original. Much information that we should like, and that would be very helpful, has been with-

held. Perhaps it has been withheld for a good reason; who shall say? In view of these things, should we not move slowly and with all due caution before we say, "Such and such is true?"

Furthermore, the authors desire to quote from the record a statement of first importance to the end that we may all keep as close as possible to the spirit and fact of it. "And when thou hast done this thou shalt go at the head of them down into the valley which is northward. And there will I meet thee, and I will go before thee into a land which is choice above all the lands of the earth. "And there will I bless thee and thy seed, and raise up unto me of thy seed, and of the seed of thy brother, and they who shall go with thee, a great nation. And there shall be none greater than the nation which I will raise up unto me of thy seed, upon all the face of the earth." (Ether 1:42-43)

The writers sincerely trust that nothing in the present work is out of harmony with this.

CHAPTER SIX  

Though it comes close to the theme of this whole discussion, and necessarily runs throughout, this question of the location of the land of the Jaredites will now be treated in a separate chapter. Its importance is such that too much can hardly be said about it. It will be the purpose of this chapter to assemble as much as possible of the information on this matter with a view to determining whether the homeland of the Jaredites can be definitely located, and to discover what, if any, pattern is given that may be kept in mind for comparison with later phases of the study.

LAND OF THE JAREDITES WEST SEA 

Heth Landing place

EAST SEA MORON The permanent Capital 

Hill Shim 

Was this the land of many rivers, lakes and fountains? 

Hill Ramah

Ether 9:3 Ablom 

This, or at least the southern por tion of it, was known to the 

Mephites as Desolation.

Relationships are only sugges tive in the mam

The authors desire that it be kept in mind that they are aware of the limitations under which they work, both as to personal qualifications and the more or less fragmentary nature of the material. If the subject they are here discussing were altogether clear, there would be no need for discussing it at all. They are keenly aware of the many interpretations placed upon these same citations by good men, and freely admit that they may be no nearer the

truth than any or all the others.

I. Review of Facts Relative to the Place of Landing. 

A reference to the discussion of the routes of travel will refresh our minds on this matter of the landing place and provide us with a point of departure for later consideration. 

If the Jaredites took the eastern journey from Babel, as some thoughtful students of the BOOK OF MORMON believe and have believed, they would probably have landed on the western coast of the Americas at some point between what is now California and the northern part of South America. This view has been held by some of the best writers on the subject.

If the Jaredites took the western route, as suggested by the authors, they would have reached the new world in all probability on the eastern coast of Central America or Mexico. This seems at the present time to have been the only way of travel that would have justified the time and effort required to build the first set of barges. Attention has already been called to the fact that the Jaredites appear to have been in close proximity to the eastern coast whereas there is probably not a line to suggest nearness to the west. This is simply corroborative evidence; it does not prove nor settle anything.

The writers desire, however, to reaffirm their conviction that the Jaredites landed on the eastern coast of Central 

America or Mexico, probably the latter.

II. Coriantumr and the Mulekites. 

Alma (22:30-32) makes it clear that either the Mulekites or Jaredites landed at the narrow neck of land that divided the land northward from the land southward. 

Which of the two colonies it was is not altogether certain though it was likely the Mulekites.

But in the last analysis it is not important to which group Alma had reference, for to locate one is to locate the other. They occupied the same general section of the country. Indeed, to an extent their lands overlapped. 

Though we anticipate just a little, it is perfectly safe to say at this point that the land called by the Nephites Desolation was the homeland of the Jaredites, or part of it. The land of the Mulekites, on the other hand, was Zarahemla.

But Desolation and Zarahemla joined at the narrow neck

of land, and it is quite clear that the boundary line was never very stable nor clearly drawn. Thus a small extent of territory lying at or near the narrow neck of land was perhaps alternately in Desolation and Zarahemla. 

We have already seen that the Jaredites remained for 

1,600 years in the new world, seemingly living and dying in the same place. Moron appears to have been their capital throughout that long period. 

It will be remembered that Ether made a prophecy to Coriantumr that if he would repent, his people would be spared, but that if he would not repent, he alone would survive to see the coming of another people to possess his land. (Ether 13:20-21) And that was but the repetition of a prediction that had been made before the days of 

Ether. (Ether 11:20-21) The burden of these messages was that in the event of the destruction of the Jaredites another people would come to possess the land, either the land in a general way or in a particular sense the exact land of the Jaredites. As it happened, both developments took place. 

Omni 21 records the fulfillment of the prophecies. The 

Mulekites came to the same land in which the Jaredites had dwelt, at least in a general way though it seems that their particular home was immediately adjacent to that of the Jaredites with no very distinct boundary between. Desolation

It was in that land of Coriantumr that the Jaredites had been destroyed, for their bones lay scattered in the land northward, (Omni 21) meaning, of course, Desolation.

It was doubtless there that Ether's record was found by 

Limhi's men. (Mosiah 8:7-9) It was there that they found the ruins of buildings of every kind and of weapons of war. 

It was there that the stone was discovered on which was written the story of Coriantumr. (Omni 20)

Where was that land? This is a question that demands a thoughtful answer.

According to the writings of Alma (22:30-32) it was immediately north of the narrow neck of land. 

Is this not both strange and interesting? 

Where was the narrow neck of land? An entire chapter is devoted to this subject near the end of the present volume, but a few comments may well be given here regarding it.

For many years it has been the invariable practice of those who have written or spoken on this matter to assume that the narrow neck of land has reference to the Isthmus of Panama or some other similar place in Central America. 

Recently other locations have been suggested. The point is that no one seems to know. How many places are there in the western world that could qualify as narrow necks of land?

The authors frankly confess that they do not know where the narrow neck of land was located. They have their views and feel that their reasons are sound for entertaining such views. They desire, however, to make one further observation by way of caution. It is not necessary simply to discover a narrow neck of land. It is necessary to find a place that fills this requirement and that also meets with a multitude of other tests. The narrow neck of land was associated in a very definite manner with other physical features and points of geography all or most of which must be found in their proper setting when at last the truth is known. Does it not appear to be the height of folly to attempt to build a geographical structure on a single passage or even a number of them without taking into consideration the whole body of facts?

Alma places the land Desolation, part or all of the homeland of the Jaredites, just north of the narrow neck of land, and the whole story of the BOOK OF MORMON appears to be in agreement. So far as the writers know, there is not a statement in the record to the contrary. 

If, then, the Mulekites landed at the narrow neck of land, and if they found Coriantumr in his own land, as they would have had to do if the prophecies were to meet with fulfillment, does it not seem that all the events in the history of the Jaredites took place there? Is this not in

harmony with the story of that people as we have followed it to date? Elder B. H. Roberts placed the land of the 

Jaredites in Central America. 16

The present writers agree with this though they feel that it need not be confined to Central America. Is there a more logical or consistent interpretation? 

III. Brief Review of Other Facts. 

Let us recall briefly a few significant details already given.

We are still trying to determine whether the BOOK OF MORMON gives sufficient data for a definite location of the land of the Jaredites.

A. The Day-and-a-Half's Journey. 

On the line between Bountiful (the northern end of Zarahemla, the land of the Mulekites) and Desolation (the land of the Jaredites) the distance from sea to sea was a day-and-a-half's journey for a Nephite. (Alma 22:32) This was somewhere in the narrow neck of land that looms more and more significantly as the study progresses. How many places are there in the western would of which this could be said? If the word "sea" means ocean, as it appears to do, the question is answered before it is asked. If it does not, we shall probably have to reconstruct much of our thinking on this subject.

The Mulekites landed near this place, the narrow part of the land where the distance from sea to sea was so small. 

Near there they found Coriantumr, in all probability among the ruins of his people's civilization, if the prophecies were fulfilled. Does this not appear to place the land of the 

Jaredites near the narrow neck of land? If the Jaredites lived and died in the same land, as the record appears to show, all this is perfectly clear and consistent. But if the Jaredites were destroyed somewhere else, how did the 

Mulekites find Coriantumr in his homeland? Is it possible that the Jaredites went elsewhere and Coriantumr wandered back? If this is true, how did the records of the people, Ether's records, made at the cave while the fighting was going on, get back into the land of the destruction where 

Limhi's men found them?

If the premises upon which this study is made are 

16. Roberts, B. H.,New Witness for God, Vol. II, pp. 140-145

sound, and if the inferences are fairly drawn from the facts, how are we to avoid the conclusion that the Jaredites lived and died near the narrow neck of land, wherever it was?

B. A Problem Presented. 

When King Omer fled from the danger of Jared, he traveled east, and he passed the hill Shim and the place where the Nephites were destroyed. (Ether 9:3) This was, of course, as well the place where the Jaredites were destroyed since the place of destruction was the same in both cases. It was there too that Mormon hid the sacred records. It has been assumed for many years that Moroni hid the records in his possession in the same hill, but the 

BOOK OF MORMON does not say this.

The foregoing facts bring into relief a problem of tremendous significance that must some day be answered.

Whether we are able to answer it at present depends upon the information at hand and a wise, consistent, and unbiased interpretation of the facts. Let us present the problem by an orderly approach to it, examining carefully each detail in that approach.

It is an established fact that the Jaredites and Nephites were destroyed at the same place. "And it came to pass that the army of Coriantumr did pitch their tents by the hill Ramah; and it was that same hill where my father Mormon did hide up the records unto the Lord, which were sacred." (Ether 15:11) 

By referring to Mormon 6:6 we find that Mormon hid most of the records in the hill Cumorah. "And it came to pass that when we had gathered in all our people in one to the land of Cumorah, behold I, Mormon, began to be old; and knowing it to be the last struggle of my people, and having been commanded of the Lord that I should not suffer the records which had been handed down by our fathers, which were sacred, to fall into the hands of the Lamanites, (for the Lamanites would destroy them) therefore I made this record out of the plates of Nephi, and hid up in the hill Cumorah all the records which had been entrusted to me by the hand of the Lord, save it were these few plates which I gave unto my son Moroni."

Thus it becomes clear that the two civilizations came to an end at the same hill. The Jaredites fought their last

battle at Ramah. But Ramah was the hill in which Mormon hid the records. Mormon hid the records in hill 

Cumorah, and it was at the hill Cumorah that the Nephites fell.

Mormon, however, did not deposit all the records in 

Cumorah. He gave to Moroni (Mormon 6:6) a portion of them, and he put them somewhere; (Mormon 8:14; Moroni 

10:2) he does not say where. One wonders in passing whether Moroni hid his records in the same place in which his father put the others.

We come now to another aspect of the matter, and what possibilities for study or speculation it presents! 

Where were the Jaredite records deposited and found? “.... and he (Ether) finished his record; and he hid them in a manner that the people of Limhi did find them." (Ether 15:33)

Ether made the last additions to his record while in the cave to which he fled in the early years of Coriantumr's reign, eleven to thirteen years before the end. It was from the cave that he watched the last struggles of his people. 

It was, of course, in the land of the destruction that Ether hid his history. Why should he have hidden it elsewhere?

Where was this? It was among the ruins of buildings of every kind, for the forty-three scouts found the buildings. (Mosiah 8:8) Where are such buildings to be found today? 

Where were those buildings?

They could have been, seemingly, in but one of two places. They could have been in the homeland of the 

Jaredites, the place in which they had lived for centuries, from which they had taken all the timber, the place later called Desolation by the Nephites, the place of which Moron had been the capital for sixteen centuries, or of which it was a near neighbor.

If the buildings were not there, they must have been in the land to which the Jaredites moved before the last war, granting, of course, that they moved at all, a thing for which is no evidence apparently. But supposing they did migrate northward, one might be justified in indulging some speculation regarding the manner of the migration. 

Could hundreds of millions of people-to use the esti-

mate of some or even millions, as there must surely have been, have moved even their persons and some food the three or four thousand miles assuming always that the original home was in Central America as most students have assumed-built buildings of every kind sufficiently substantial to last for centuries, and fight to their utter extinction, all in the period of four years, the period almost universally agreed upon as the correct one?

The actual journey would have required at least a year.

On April 23, 1925, a Mr. A. F. Tschiffely set out from 

Buenos Aires with two tough criollo horses, riding and packing them alternately, on a journey to New York. Two and one-half years later he arrived in Washington, D. C., having traversed 9,600 miles, at least half of which was relatively or absolutely primitive. Approximately six months of the elapsed time was spent in resting, leaving almost exactly two years actual traveling time. 17 

Much of the territory thus covered was in a natural state, but it was no more so, of course, than in the days of the Jaredites. Moreover, Mr. Tschiffely had the benefit of bridges and roads, of trails and ferries, of military guards, and even of steamships. And he was lucky as well as wise.

Knowing something of the lands through which he intended going, he made preparation. He was fortunate in missing the rainy seasons along the way. His horses were sturdy and strong. Yet, traveling half the distance over improved highways and through improved country, he averaged only thirteen miles a day. 

Could the Jaredites have done any better, or as well?

Would not their vast numbers have multiplied their difficulties almost in proportion? 

And how would they have traveled? Did they all go together, their differences forgotten by common consent, peaceful by agreement, only to break out in open hostility upon arriving at their destination? Or did one faction travel ahead of the other?

Such questions are in a sense impertinent, but are they not reasonable? To be sure, we are left entirely to speculation; there is not a word about these things. Indeed, there is not a word about the migration at all, and one might be 

17. "Buenos Aires to Washington by Horse," A. F. Tschiffely, National Geographic Magazine, February, 1929

fully justified in doubting that such a tremendous trek ever took place. 

C. Coriantumr and the Mulekites Again.

There is a widespread understanding throughout the Church that after the destruction of the Jaredite nation Coriantumr wandered back to some land from which the people had previously gone. Quite apart from any mention in the record of such going, let us examine into this matter a moment. If there is no statement in the BOOK 

OF MORMON that would seem to give the idea of such a migration, where did the notion originate? Is it from the necessity of getting the people into the north country? 

Does it arise from the fact that the gold plates of the BOOK 

OF MORMON were found in the north? Is it a matter of revelation and record or of apparent expendiency?

Let us return for a moment to the prophecies of another people to come. "And the Lord God would send or bring forth another people to possess the land, by his power, after the manner by which he brought their fathers." (Ether 11:21) "Otherwise they should be destroyed, and all his household save it were himself. And he should only live to see the fulfilling of the prophecies which had been spoken concerning another people receiving the land for their inheritance; and Coriantumr should receive a burial by them." (Ether 13:21)

If the foregoing prophecies were fulfilled—and who can deny it?-the newcomers, the Mulekites, came to the land that had been occupied by the Jaredites. The only difficulty in interpretation, possibly, lies in what is meant by the land. If it means the hemisphere, the western world, there would seem to be no point to any argument since the peoples could have been separated by many thousand miles and still be in the same land.

But if it means that the new colony was to come to the very place in which the Jaredites had lived and died, we have a basis for something very significant. Three possibilities at once become apparent. (1) The Mulekites landed on the eastern coast of what is now the United States, traveled inland and found Coriantumr in the land of the present hill Cumorah. (2) The Mulekites landed somewhere much farther south, and Coriantumr wandered back to that place. (3) Neither Coriantumr nor the Jaredites

traveled anywhere, and the Mulekites, reaching the new world somewhere near the middle of the hemisphere, came upon Coriantumr in his homeland. This last view fulfills all the requirements of the text. It must be remembered that the new colony was to occupy the land of the wicked Jaredites who lived at the time of Coriantumr, very near the end of the nation.

The whole difficulty, if difficulty there is, seems to resolve itself into a question whether the Jaredites migrated northward during the last ten or twelve years of their existence.

Let us look at the fulfillment of those prophecies. "And it came to pass in the days of Mosiah, there was a large stone brought unto him with engravings on it; and he did interpret the engravings by the gift and power of God. "And they gave an account of one Coriantumr, and the slain of his people. And Coriantumr was discovered by the people of Zarahemla; and he dwelt with them for the space of nine moons. (Omni 20-21)

A number of very pertinent questions (since so much that follows is part and parcel of this) at once force themselves upon our attention. 

1. If the Jaredites traveled northward a vast distance, when did they do so, and where is the reason in the record to believe it? 

2. If the Jaredites traveled north, did the Mulekites find Coriantumr in the north country? 

3. If the Mulekites did not find Coriantumr in the north, when did he return, and where is the record of it? 

4. When Coriantumr returned from the north, did he bring with him the twenty-four gold plates and the stone of Coriantumr? 

5. Did Coriantumr wander into the colony of the 

Mulekites, or did the Mulekites come to the land of the

Jaredites and find him there? The record says that the 

Mulekites discovered Coriantumr.

Here, then, is the problem: If, as many find, it is impossible to think that the forty-three Limhi scouts went beyond Central America or Mexico to find the twenty-four

gold plates, the record of the Jaredites, how shall we harmonize this with the certain fact that the Nephite record was found in New York State? Does it not seem that one record was moved inasmuch as both peoples were destroyed in the same place? It would be interesting in the extreme to find out whether Coriantumr moved the Jaredite history south or Moroni, the Nephite history north. Many very thoughtful Latter-day Saints are conscious of a challenge here.

Now, lest anyone, particularly the unwary, feel that the foregoing is altogether convincing, the authors wish here to call attention to another fact that will need explanation and for which at the present time they have no very satisfactory answer.

If many, or all, of the events discussed to date occurred in Central America and Mexico, as there is much reason to believe, where the vegetation is so rank and fast-growing, how shall we account for the fact that more than five hundred years after the destruction of the Jaredites, about 46 B. C., the land of Desolation was still desolate of timber? (Helaman 3) Would not trees grow in that length of time anywhere on the western hemisphere?

This is not an argument against the probability that Central America and Mexico were the land of the peoples of the BOOK OF MORMON so much as it is a problem for thoughtful men and women to solve. 

D. Location as Indicated by Climate and Products. 

There is another evidence as to the location of the land of the Jaredites that has perhaps not been sufficiently treated that shows the internal harmony of the BOOK OF MORMON. It also tends to prove the utter impossibility that Joseph Smith could have been the author of all the material in the record. This is the matter of climate and products.

During the time of Omer, already discussed at length, and of his son Emer, the people were prosperous and active. They were rich and had “all manner of fruit, and of grain, and of silks, and of fine linen, and of gold, and of silver, and iron, and brass, and of precious things." They also had oxen, cows, sheep, swine, goats, and many other kinds of animals which were useful for food, and they had horses, asses, cureloms, cumoms, and elephants. (Ether 9:17-19)

Again, "they were exceedingly industrious, and they did buy and sell the traffic one with another, that they might get gain. And they did work in all manner of ore, and they did make gold, and silver, and iron, and brass, and all manner of metals; and they did dig it out of the earth. And they did have silks and fine-twined linen." (Ether 10:22-24) This is in marked agreement with other statements regarding the land of the Nephites, which was substantially the same. (I Nephi 18:25; Helaman 6:13) ....

Here is a very significant statement from the story of the Nephites: "And there were some who died with fevers, which at some seasons of the year were very frequent in the land-but not so much with the fevers, because of the excellent qualities of the many plants and roots which God had prepared to remove the cause of diseases, to which men were subject by the nature of the climate." (Alma 46:40)

Where in the western world, or the whole world, for that matter, can we find a place that has an abundance of gold, silver, and copper, where silk, cotton, and linen grow, where fevers, the universal attendants of hot climates, are very frequent, and where herbs exist with which to combat fever? We might indulge some interesting speculations regarding elephants, and if cureloms and cumoms, as some have suggested, were similar to our llamas and alpacas of today, need we search much farther for the land of the Jaredites?

The foregoing sounds, in many respects, like South America, and it is for that reason, that nothing altogether definite is known about the subject under discussion, that the writers here append this word of caution.

It is very easy for men to read and form conclusions, sound and otherwise, but it is much more difficult to maintain an open mind until all the facts are weighed. 

Surely no one would urge that all the facts are in here. 

The truth is that owing to the fragmentary nature of our source of information we have relatively few facts. Is it not the part of wisdom to suspend judgment where there is a dearth of material?

The last thing in the world the present writers have in mind is to convince someone of something. They have tried in their weak way to examine the BOOK OF MOR- MON critically to determine so far as possible what its

somewhat meager record actually does say regarding geography and kindred subjects. They here offer their findings as clearly and impartially as they can. They do not make the claim that they are the first thus to read the record, and they are keenly aware that in many things they differ with others who have read it. They are trying to follow the divine counsel to search the Scripture, and they earnestly recommend that procedure to all who are interested in the truth. But they desire to say that it is their opinion that men should form conclusions slowly, and in the light of all the facts that can have any bearing upon any matter under consideration. When the vast majority of data are interpreted alike in the minds of the vast majority of students, then, and perhaps only then, can a thing be said to have been proved. Up to that point observations are largely the opinions of men and women. 

E. Desolation.

Before leaving this question of the location of the land of the Jaredites it might be advisable to call attention to another aspect of the matter. Some more quotations from the record will be helpful. "And they (the Jaredites) built a great city by the narrow neck of land, by the place where the sea divides the land. And they did preserve the land southward for a wilderness, to get game. And the whole face of the land northward was covered with inhabitants." (Ether 10:20-21)

There is no citation that gives the name of this Jaredite city. Nor is there evidence that it existed, or the remains thereof, in the days of the Nephites. One fact, however, is clear beyond any possibility of question or doubt; namely, the Jaredites at sometime or other lived at the narrow neck of land. The Nephite city of Desolation, built in the wasted Jaredite land, was right at the narrow neck of land. (Mormon 3:5-7; 4:8)

As one reads and reads, it becomes more and more difficult to avoid the conclusion that the Jaredites were associated throughout their entire existence with the country adjacent to the narrow neck of land.

CHAPTER SEVEN  

Most of the material of this chapter, it should be noted and borne in mind, is not textual information from the record. It is altogether incidental to the discussion and should be given only such weight as it appears to merit. 

How many people were there in the Jaredite nation at its highest peak? This question can be answered only by indirection. Even so some fairly definite conclusions can be reached.

I. Comparison with the United States. 

The Jaredites lived upon the land approximately 1,600 years, probably from about 2,200 B. C. to 600 B. C. Though a small company at first, they must have grown in that vast period of time into a mighty nation. In a little over four hundred years the whole of the United States has been populated, or at least settled.

But is this a valid comparison? By no means. To accept it would be to build a structure on an altogether inadequate foundation. It would lead us far from the truth. 

There are a number of factors that need to be taken into consideration. 

A. The United States is not truly populated yet. One need but travel through some of our western states to realize this. Our population of about 130,000,000, divided by our land area of three million square miles, gives about forty-three to the square mile. That is anything but a dense population.

B. The Mayflower company may have compared favorably in number with the Jaredite colony though it is unlikely that the earlier group was so large as the later. 

It could hardly have counted more than one hundred. 

Moreover, there were many other groups that came to our

shores.

C. So far as the record goes, ancient America had no immigrants during the time of the Jaredites. Not only did the United States receive many early colonies, it has taken into its borders a constant stream of men and women from prolific nations. Between 1820 and 1930 the total number of immigrants was 47,737,313. Their descendants would constitute a very high percentage of our present population.

D. The rise of scientific knowledge has increased the life span in the United States from twenty-five years to sixty years. While the birth rate has dropped off noticeably in the western world during the past hundred years, the death rate has also fallen to the extent that there has been a steady increase in population right up to the past two or three years.

E. The application to the United States of the principles and practices of the Industrial Revolution following hard upon the discovery of America brought about the most remarkable increase in population in the history of mankind according to Dr. Henry Pratt Fairchild. 18

F. It is one of the fundamental principles of population growth that births must be in excess of deaths in any given case. The main causes of death are excessive births, leading to a high infant mortality, war, disease, and vice. 

The United States has not been troubled with any of these in a serious form, comparatively speaking. 

Owing to our standards of living, infant mortality is relatively low. There have been no devastating plagues in our national history. Our cost of war, in comparison with other nations and other times, has been remarkably low. 

And even vice, demoralizing and terrible as it is, has not taken a tremendous toll in human life. 

But we are not left altogether to speculation in forming some conclusions as to the population of the Jaredites. 

Another quotation from Dr. Fairchild will serve as a basis for further consideration. "No demonstration has ever been given as to what the possible rate of increase is," he says, "but it is a conservative estimate that the human race is physiologically capable, under favorable conditions, of doubling every twenty years.” 19 

18. Fairchild, Henry Pratt, Applied Sociology, p. 206 

19. Op. cit., p. 202

The race is physiologically capable of doubling every twenty years, under favorable conditions. But when has a nation increased at such a rate? When has a people had such favorable conditions? 

Here are a few interesting facts. "Accepting this estimate (Mechelot's) it can be calculated that between 1845 and 1914 the average annual increase of the world population was about .7%. It was in other words increasing at a rate which would double the population in less than 100 years. This rate of increase is considerably less than that found among the more advanced countries which compile registration statistics. Taking 26 such countries and calculating the rate of increase for the period 1906 to 1911 it works out at over 1.1%. These 26 countries were increasing at a rate that would double their population in about 60 years.' 99 20 These twenty-six most highly favored nations in all history, during the most favored period in all history-and a short one at that-would double in sixty years! A longer period would inevitably decrease the rate. "During the century following 1814, the population of the world more than doubled, an increase made possible by the opening of new lands in North and South America, Australia, New Zealand, and South Africa. Never before had such a rapid increase in population taken place.” 21 "As a result (of greatly improved methods of agriculture and the settling of vast areas in the Americas, Australia, and South Africa) world population doubled between 1800 and 1900." 22 ". . . . It seems probable that the population of Europe stood practically stationary during the Middle Ages and increased only slowly down to the nineteenth century; but during the nineteenth century the population of the leading industrial nations increased very rapidly. This was due primarily, without doubt, to improved economic conditions." 23 "It is certain that disregarding these fluctuations the rate of growth in former times taken over several centuries 

20. Encyclopedia Britannica, Vol. 18, p. 231 

21. World Book Encyclopedia, Vol. 13, p. 5329 22. Gavian, Gray, and Groves, Our Changing Social Order, p. 303 

23. Ellwood, Sociology and Modern Social Problems, p.

185

was very small and that the rate of growth during the last century (1800-1900) was wholly abnormal.” 24 "The population of the United States is about twentyseven times what it was when the first census was taken in 1790. It has just about doubled within the last thirtyfive years, (prior to 1922) whereas the population of England has doubled in the past sixty years, and that of France has increased only about one third in the past one hundred years.' 99 25 But----- "In fact, we are now filling two cradles for one coffin. 

Were there no immigration, the present margin of births over deaths, if maintained, would suffice to double our number in a century." 20

The population of our nation, the most fortunate in all human history, without immigration, would double in one hundred years! Surely here is something authentic on which to base estimates for other peoples and times. 

Now, where in all this maze of facts shall we discover a situation to which the career of the Jaredite nation was comparable?

Would it be the Middle Ages with their constant warfare, their recurring devastating plagues, and their stationary population?

This could hardly be true because the Jaredites were a mighty and a blessed people despite their wickedness, their secret combinations, their slaughters, and their pestilences.

Would it be those twenty-six favored nations of the unparalleled nineteenth century?

This too is improbable. Here sixty years would be required for doubling. Certainly it would not be the United

States with its multitudes of immigrants and modern standard of living.

Let us take another close look at those Jaredites. 

Kib, the second king, fought a civil war against his son 

Corihor. (Ether 7:4-7) Shule, Corihor's brother, fought 

24. Encyclopedia Britannica, Vol. 18, p. 232 

25. Towne, Social Problems, p. 19 

26. Ross, Civic Sociology, p. 11

against Corihor. (v. 9) Corihor's son Noah rebelled against 

Shule and contended with his forces. (v. 15) This struggle was bitter and prolonged. (vs. 16-18) Noah's son carried on his father's war against Shule. (v. 15)

Omer, Shule's son, the fourth king, had almost continual warfare, as already shown. It was in his reign that the people were practically wiped out and had to start over again. (Ether 9:9, 12)

Emer, Omer's son, had peace throughout his long reign. (9:15-21) Coriantum and Com also enjoyed peace.

But the population would in the very nature of things have been very small. This period of peace lasted probably between 150 and two hundred years.

Heth fought against his father Com and killed him (9:26-28) In Heth's time there was a severe famine that destroyed many people. Here also was the terrible plague of poisonous serpents. (9:30-31) The people repented to keep from perishing. (v. 34)

Shez, Heth's son, reigned in righteousness while the memory of the sufferings was green in the people's minds. (10:2) His son Shez rebelled and caused trouble again. (v. 3) Riplakish, another son, was wicked and troubled the people. They rebelled against him, and there was war again. (10:8) Morianton, a son or descendant of Riplakish, fought against the people with an army of outcasts. (v. 9) 

Morianton's son Kim was engaged in civil war with his brother. (v. 14) His son Levi fought the self-appointed ruler. (v. 15) Corom, Kish, and Lib apparently reigned in peace. All this time, however, the people had been tormented with the serpents. (v. 19)

Hearthom fell before an unnamed foe and lived in captivity. (v. 30) Heth, Aaron, Amnigaddah, and Coriantum lived in captivity all their lives. (Ether 10:31)

Com fought Amgid many years. (v. 32) In his days the nation and the government were terrorized and threatened by robbers. (v. 34) It was in the time of Com that many prophets were sent to tell the people that their murders and secret combinations would bring destruction upon them. (11:1)

Apparently there were many well-planned homicides and wholesale slaughters through the hundreds of years that the secret combinations existed, all of which added

immeasurably to the hazards of human life. 

Yet, as we look at our own nation with its gang wars and thousands of murders annually, we discover that they are perhaps insignificant when it comes to influencing the population.

Shiblon took Com's place and fought with his brother in "an exceeding great war." (11:4) There were great calamities in all the land, (v. 6) and there was destruction such as had never been known in all the world. (v. 7) 

Should we pause here again for a moment to call attention to the language? Does the foregoing declaration actually mean that that destruction was the greatest in all the history of the world up to that time? Or is this rather the statement of a great truth in somewhat figurative language? Does it not rather mean that so far as the writer knew, it was the greatest destruction, or the greatest among the peoples of whom he had direct knowledge? Only on such a basis, it appears, can the BOOK OF MORMON be interpreted to be consistent with itself and with known facts. But surely the record itself must not be condemned, nor misunderstood, because of peculiarities within it. 

Seth lived in captivity, and Ahah did wickedness in which he did shed much blood. (v. 10) Ethem and the people at his time were wicked. (vs. 13-14) Moron was likewise corrupt and faced continual trouble and rebellion. (vs. 14-15)

Coriantor, his son, the father of Ether, lived in captivity all his life, (v. 19) and Coriantumr, the last ruler, spent much of his life in war and bloodshed. (Ether 13:15; 15:32)

To what extent could the people have increased under such conditions as those set forth above? Only six or eight of the twenty-nine kings reigned in peace. Are such circumstances of war, pillage, rapine, murder, and destruction, with their attendant disease and insecurity, wholesome conditions for the fostering of a fast-growing population? 

Now, to come back to the matter of increase as revealed by modern scientific studies, we find a basis for estimates. that are perhaps fairly accurate. The authors are inclined to think that under the conditions outlined above, the Jaredites did not, could not, indeed, have increased faster than 100% every century, but for fear that this does not.

accurately represent the situation, let us put the time at seventy years, only a little over the time for those twentysix tremendously-favored nations. Surely that is low enough.

From the time of Omer, when the nation began all over again, or appears to have done so, there were 1,400 years approximately until the end. At seventy years to the doubling this would allow for twenty. Twenty doublings, beginning with a colony of one hundred, would amount to 

75,417,600. The number at the beginning might have been more than one hundred; it could as well have been less. 

The writers realize, few better, that this is highly speculative procedure. But how hardly can one arrive at any very different conclusion on the basis of the available facts? If there were other people of the Jaredites at the time of Omer than those in Heth and the household of the king, this fact would, of course, vastly affect the whole trend of our estimates. Or if there were other people in the land of whom the historians were unaware, or of whom they were aware but say nothing, this too would make all attempts at computation altogether valueless. 

The authors feel that this is a matter on which men should be humble rather than too confident. It is their conviction, however, based upon the reading of the available facts, that the Jaredites were at no time so numerous as many earnest students have been inclined to think. 

Dr. H. J. Spinden, one of the world's leading authorities on American antiquities, declares that the First Mayan Empire, which lasted from about 600 B. C. to sometime between 300 and 600 years after Christ, nine hundred to 1,200 years in all, at its height reached a population of eight million or perhaps even more. 27

II. Population as Given in the Record. 

After the calamities mentioned in connection with the reigns of Shiblom, Seth, Ahah, Ethem, Moron, and Coriantor, the last king, Coriantumr, came into prominence. 

It is in connection with his reign that the only definite figure on population is given in the record of the Jaredites. 

After some years of fighting, Coriantumr suddenly became repentant upon discovering that already two million 

27. "Population of Ancient America," Smithsonian Institute 

Annual Report, 1929, pp. 462 ff.

mighty men had been killed, and also their wives and children. It appears that the figure represents only men, though this is by no means certain. It might well have included women and children. (Ether 15:2) This is in no wise fantastic since the women and children did fight with the men a great many times. 

But assuming the dead to have been men, if we allow a wife and eight children for each, we have a key to about eighteen or twenty million people. It is likely that the enemy would have lost as many. And these were not all, because many were killed subsequently. We have here, however, a basis upon which to reconstruct a population for the Jaredites of perhaps upwards of fifty million or even sixty million people. This is in close agreement with the other estimate when we remember that seventy years was perhaps too short a time for a doubling of the number. 

How much land did they require?

While we are indulging in some speculations, may we not as well carry it a little further? But it should be remembered that this is at best a suggestion. 

The present area of Guatemala, Honduras, Costa Rica,

Nicaragua, and Mexico within which practically all students of the BOOK OF MORMON think that the Jaredites spent most of their lives-is about 834,365 square miles. A population of fifty million would make a density of about sixty to the square mile. Is this not very sparse? Is it not probable that the territory was smaller or the population larger?

One is almost inclined to think, after a careful reading of the Book of Ether, that when we attempt to build upon the foundation of the information contained therein a population of nearly half a billion people, as some have done, living in a territory of approximately 3,500,000 square miles, we introduce inconsistencies that cannot be harmonized. Where in the record do we find justification for such speculations? 

And yet, it must not be forgotten that the Book of

Ether is an exceedingly short history of a great people covering a period of sixteen centuries. Is it possible that a fuller account would influence materially the conclusions drawn from this abridgment? Who can doubt it? Perhaps, then, we should not make up our minds too firmly on any points that are not clearly and unquestionably de-

termined as matters of record or revelation.

FIRST MAYAN EMPIRE CENTRAL AMERICA 

It is within the territory indicated by the circle that Dr. H.Spinden locates the first Mayan 

Empire with a popula tion of 8000,000 or more. 

A further explanation is in order here to guard against a possible misunderstanding later. The writers do not presume to say that all the Jaredites in the western world were destroyed at Ramah, or lived in Moron and surrounding lands and cities. There may have been many in other parts of the land, to the far north and the far south. These may not have gathered to the central place at the time of the destruction. They may have had governments of their own in other localities. The present treatise deals only with those who lived and died in the permanent homeland of the nation. There might well have been many millions elsewhere.

PART TWO THE MULEKITES

CHAPTER EIGHT HISTORY AND GEOGRAPHY OF THE MULEKITES 

Elsewhere in the book, in its proper chronological setting, will be found the account of the union of the Mulekites and the Nephites, but since the Mulekites properly constitute an important part of the Nephites, whose history makes up the major part of the BOOK OF MORMON, it will be advisable to consider them for a short time outside their chronological order. The Nephites should be studied first, but a brief outline of the background of the Mulekites will be given at this point. Golonies 

Which Came to america Jerusalem 

Mulekites 589 BC. "nephites boo&C. America 

Jaredites 2200 B.C. Babel

The Mulekites represent the meeting of all three peoples. They are the link between the Jaredites and the 

Nephites. Coming as they did, shortly after the destruction of the Jaredites, they found Coriantumr and the ruins of the Jaredite civilization, and later they merged with the Nephites. Apparently all were in relatively the same land. 

I. Brief Narrative. 

Both Nephite and Mulekite records begin with events that took place during the reign of Zedekiah as king of the Jews. The dates of this reign are usually given as 597 to 

586 B. C. A slight discrepancy occurs, however, in the ac-

counts of the Bible and the BOOK OF MORMON, the latter giving the dates as 600 to 589. The error is probably in the Bible account because there is little agreement among Bible scholars as to dates in the Old Testament. Be it as it may, Zedekiah reigned eleven years. Lehi's colony left 

Jerusalem in the first year, and Mulek's, in the last. Thus they were separated by eleven years. The Bible account is contained in II Kings, chapters 24 and 25, and in chapter 

36 of II Chronicles. The version found in the BOOK OF 

MORMON is in I Nephi 1 and Omni 20 and 21.

Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, had already carried captive into Babylon the princes of the Jews and many of the people because of their rebellions against his power. 

He set up as ruler in Jerusalem a young Jew named Mattaniah and changed his name to Zedekiah. "Zedekiah was twenty and one years old when he began to reign, and he reigned eleven years in Jerusalem." (II Kings 24:18)

Zedekiah proved disloyal to his trust and rebelled against Nebuchadnezzar in the eleventh year of his reign.

When the mighty Babylonian monarch came to Jerusalem to punish him, he fled through a secret pass in the wall and attempted to escape, but he and his family were captured and brought back. "And they slew the sons of Zedekiah before his eyes, and put out the eyes of Zedekiah, and bound him with fetters of brass, and carried him to Babylon." (II Kings 25:7)

As Zedekiah began his reign at twenty-one, he was but thirty-two when he was taken to Babylon. The number and ages of his sons are not given, but there could not have been many of them, nor could they have been very old, the eldest not more than nine or ten. Helaman (6:10) says that Mulek was one of them. In Helaman 9:21 we read that all Zedekiah's sons were killed except Mulek.

There is really no contradiction here. So far as the 

Bible historian knew, all the sons of the king were killed.

But since the eldest could have been a mere child, the youngest could well have been but a babe in arms. He could easily have been preserved by a nurse or other attendant and kept from the notice of the king and his murderers.

Mulek, of course, being but an infant, could have exercised only nominal leadership over his group, but being

THE MULEKITES 

77 a king's son he would naturally have been held in high regard by his friends, and they would, of course, have honored him by giving his name to the colony. 

II. Location of the Mulekites. 

We have no information whatever as to the way and manner in which the Mulekites came to the new world. 

What little is known directly about them comes from the meager record of Omni. From the study of the Nephites, however, much can be determined with some degree of certainty.

If the city of Mulek was the one established by them, as is altogether possible, its location becomes of greatest importance. But of this we cannot be sure; it might have been farther north that the Mulekites landed. There was, however, a city of that name on the eastern coast of the land of the Nephites very near the land of the Jaredites. 

Zarahemla, of course, located somewhat inland, was the center of their civilization. "And thus he went on, taking possession of many cities, the city of Nephihah, and the city of Lehi, and the city of Morianton, and the city of Omner, and the city of Gid, and the city of Mulek, all of which were on the east borders by the seashore." (Alma 51:26)

Mulek was almost certainly a very short distance from the city of Bountiful. (Alma 52:2, 17, 20, 27-28) And that it was near the narrow neck of land, the place at which it was a day-and-a-half's journey from sea to sea, will become very evident when we come to a detailed discussion of the 

Moronite wars.

That the home of the Mulekites was in close proximity to the land of the Jaredites has already been shown, but this aspect of the matter will now claim our attention even though it brings forth material that will later be presented more fully. "And it came to pass in the days of Mosiah, there was a large stone brought unto him with engravings on it. And he did interpret the engravings by the gift and power of God. And they gave an account of one Coriantumr, and the slain of his people. And Coriantumr was discovered by the people of Zarahemla; and he dwelt with them for the space of nine moons." (Omni 20-21)

This is an extremely important statement. It helps a

great deal in locating the peoples of the BOOK OF MOR- MON. The Mulekites came to the new world in time to fulfill Ether's prophecy and find Coriantumr, the last surviving member of the ill-fated Jaredite nation. Zarahemla, the capital of the Mulekites, was, therefore, probably very near the land of the Jaredites. For, be it remembered, the 

Mulekites almost certainly found Coriantumr in his own land, among the bones of his people. (Omni 20-22)

The stone of which mention has been made was probably engraved by Coriantumr since it had the story of the death of his people and the story of his life. It was found and brought to King Mosiah of Zarahemla. Does not all this tend to show that the lands of the Jaredites and Mulekites were very close to each other?

But we need not speculate. With respect to the land of the Mulekites there is one very explicit statement in the record, explicit even though subject to some uncertainty in interpretation. 

The historian, in Alma 22:30, has this to say: "And it (Bountiful) bordered upon the land which they (the Nephites) called Desolation, it being so far northward that it came into the land which had been peopled and been destroyed, of whose bones we have spoken, which was discovered by the people of Zarahemla, it being the place of their first landing." sea.

Verse 32 of the same chapter points out that it was on the line between these lands Desolation and Bountiful that it was a day-and-a-half's journey for a Nephite from sea to Thus they were without question at some point in the western hemisphere at which the distance bewteen the oceans was negligible, provided always that sea here means the ocean, a thing of which we cannot be altogether sure. 

That was where the Jaredites landed and were destroyed; that was where the Mulekites landed and found Coriantumr, in fulfillment of Ether's prophecy; that was where the Mulekites came to possess the land of the Jaredites.

The Nephites, of course, made the land of the Mulekites their own. Therefore, all three peoples were very near each other. Does it not seem so?
