The expression, "it being the place of their first landing," may refer to the Mulekites, the Jaredites, or both of them. The cross references in the BOOK OF MORMON indicate that it means the Mulekites, and this would seem

THE MULEKITES 

79 to be the correct rendering of the above passage. But if the Mulekites landed there, where was it? It was so near the place of the Jaredites that they found Coriantumr, the last Jaredite, in the land in which his people had been destroyed. While he yet lived, the Mulekites dwelt in, or possessed, the land of the Jaredites. If, then, we know where the Jaredites landed, we know also the place of the landing of the Mulekites because they were in a very real sense the same.

Does it not appear beyond controversy that both the 

Jaredites and Mulekites landed in the western world at the point at which the land was narrowest? Is it not apparent that their lands were very near each other if not actually the same? If Coriantumr was found in the place of the destruction, and that was in the same land in which the Jaredites landed in the new world, is it not clear that Zarahemla, the capital of the Mulekites (and the Nephites) appears to have been near the place where the 

Jaredites were destroyed? If the Nephites were destroyed in the same place, does it not seem that they closed their existence near the place where they had lived for a thousand years? And does it not appear that all these things occurred at or near the narrow neck of land where the oceans were so close to each other?

It will be very interesting to see whether the stories of the BOOK OF MORMON fit into such a pattern without forcing of interpretations. Is such a set-up consistent with the facts of the records?

III. The Meeting of the Nations. 

Another very important point, already indicated, should receive some stress here. We have just seen in the foregoing developments the meeting of the three peoples of the BOOK OF MORMON. 

The Jaredites came into the new world, lived 1,600 years, and went to their fate. The Nephites came at about the time of their destruction and occupied much the same place in which they had lived though apparently somewhat farther south. The Mulekites came also about that time, actually about twelve or fifteen years later, and took up their residence very near the land of the Jaredites if not actually in it. They found Coriantumr, the last survivor of the Jaredites, (who apparently had lived alone for a number of years) evidently in the land of the destruction of his

people. Some years later the Nephites took over the land of the Mulekites and made it their homeland. Thus the homeland of the Nephites was very near the homeland of the Jaredites. Indeed, in some respects they were the same. 

Again this fact is brought to the front. 

King Limhi, a Nephite, living in Nephi, the land of the Lamanites, where the Nephites had formerly lived, sent out a company of forty-three men to find Zarahemla from which his grandfather Zeniff had come. "Now King Limhi had sent, previous to the coming of

Ammon, a small number of men to search for the land of Zarahemla; but they could not find it, and they were lost in the wilderness. "Nevertheless, they did find a land which had been peopled; yea, a land which was covered with dry bones; yea, a land which had been peopled and been destroyed; and they, having supposed it to be the land of Zarahemla, returned to the land of Nephi, having arrived in the borders of the land not many days before the coming of Ammon. "And they brought a record with them, even a record of the people whose bones they had found; and it was engraven on plates of ore." (Mosiah 21:25-27) "And the king said unto him: Being grieved for the afflictions of my people, I caused that forty and three of my people should take a journey into the wilderness, that they might find the land of Zarahemla, that we might appeal unto our brethren to deliver us out of bondage. "And they were lost in the wilderness for the space of many days, yet they were diligent, and found not the land of Zarahemla, but returned to this land, having traveled in a land among many waters, having discovered a land which was covered with bones of men, and of beasts, and was also covered with ruins of buildings of every kind, having discovered a land which had been peopled with a people who were as numerous as the hosts of Israel. "And for a testimony that the things that they had said are true they have brought twenty-four plates which are filled with engravings, and they are of pure gold." (Mosiah 8:7-9)

This journey, and discovery by the scouts of the ruins

THE MULEKITES 

81 and records of the Jaredites, is a very wonderful circumstance. It throws light on some facts that might otherwise be somewhat obscure. And does it not likewise throw into the clear some problems on which we might desire further information? 

From verses following the last quotation given above we learn that the Jaredites were exceedingly expert in the use of copper and brass. (v. 10) The instruments made of copper and brass were perfectly sound when found by Limhi's men. There were, however, swords the hilts of which were entirely gone, and the blades were cankered with rust. (v. 11)

This is remarkable! Supposing for the moment that the Prophet Joseph Smith wrote the BOOK OF MORMON, how did he know at the age of twenty-four that copper and brass are more enduring than steel or iron, for of such material the swords must have been made. Thousands of matured and well-informed men of our own age do not know that fact to this day! Yet others are perfectly willing to believe that Joseph Smith knew it in his day to avoid the necessity of accepting something less incredible, that the book came by divine means.

The above facts also show, do they not, that the lands of the Jaredites and the Lamanites were very near each other? Limhi's scouts left the land of Nephi, the southernmost of the lands of the BOOK OF MORMON, and found the place in which the Jaredites were destroyed, the north ernmost land of the BOOK OF MORMON. How long were they gone? They were lost "many days" and still returned to Nephi. We do not know the duration of "many days," but is it not a matter for grave doubt that those men traveled thousands of miles on that excursion while being lost? They were doubtless at that time in Central America or Mexico, or so most, if not all, students believe. But where did they go in "many days" of wandering to find the place where the Jaredites were destroyed? This is a very intriguing question.

Where is, or was, the land of many waters? There are other references that make mention of it. (Alma 50:29; 

Helaman 3:3-4; Mormon 6:4; Ether 15:8) If Limhi's scouts did not travel thousands of miles to find the dead civilization of the Jaredites, where did they find the land of many rivers, lakes, and fountains? Does the answer to this apparent enigma lie in still-uncharted fields of study? Ör

might it have been Mexico, so bountifully supplied with lakes?

Before leaving this phase of the subject the authors desire once more to call attention to the tremendous grasp of natural and historical information Joseph Smith would have had to possess to write the foregoing descriptions of the land of the Jaredites and the travel of the scouts to it. 

This one fragment alone would have challenged all his formal schooling to say nothing of the other thousands of details and the apparently impossible task of making them all agree. 

To conclude: Ether finished his record after the last battle of his people and hid "it in a manner that the people of Limhi did find it." This record, of course, was made in the land of the destruction, the place in which Ether followed night by night with such detail the struggle of the opposing forces. It was made while he yet lived in the cave to which he had fled in the early years of Coriantumr's reign while the people were admittedly in the land of their inheritance. And there Limhi's men found it. We cannot help wondering if they made a journey into the eastern part of the great northern continent and back in those "many days." Does this not present a problem that all sincere readers of the BOOK OF MORMON will have to answer?

Another fact calls for attention in this connection. 

When the forty-three men found the land of the Jaredites, they discovered that it was "covered with ruins of buildings of every kind." (Mosiah 8:8) This raises another interesting problem. There seems to have been a very general agreement among the men who have written in explanation of the BOOK OF MORMON that the Jaredites lived in or near their first home, probably in Central America, or

Mexico, until four years before the end of their nation. At that time they are supposed to have made a tremendous migration to a hitherto unknown country where they fought their last battle. We are faced at once with the perplexing question: How did they rear "buildings of every kind" that would endure for hundreds of years at the same time that they were either fighting or traveling? For four years is all they had for both apparently. The authors confess that they are unable to explain. Rather they find it more consistent with the rest of the record to think that the people made no such migration at all.

PART THREE THE NEPHITES

CHAPTER NINE  I. The Old Home. 

We shall now follow the Nephites to their landing place in the new world.

About 1,600 years had passed away since the Jaredites left their first home. And while the Nephites were making their departure from Jerusalem, the Jaredites were fighting their last battle at the hill Ramah in America.

The Nephites were Semites, of the same general stock as the Jaredites. They were direct descendants of Joseph who was sold into Egypt whose father Jacob was father of the Twelve Tribes of Israel. "And Aminadi was a descendant of Nephi, who was the son of Lehi, who came out of the land of Jerusalem, who was a descendant of Manasseh, who was the son of Joseph who was sold into Egypt by the hands of his brethren." (Alma 10:3)

It is generally thought that Ishmael was a descendant of Ephraim. The Nephites, then, were Israelites, of the children of Abraham.

Students of Bible history will remember that for a long period of time after the return of Israel from Egypt, the descendants of Jacob were ruled by judges of whom Moses was the first and Samuel, the last. At some time before 

1,000 B. C. the government was changed, and a kingdom was established which lasted 120 years under the reigns of Saul, David, and Solomon. Upon the death of Solomon the kingdom was divided into two, known to history as the northern kingdom, or Kingdom of Israel, and the southern kingdom, known as the Kingdom of Judah.

The northern group persisted until about 722 B. C. when the people were carried away into captivity by

Shalmaneser of the Assyrians. From that time until the present they have been called the Lost Tribes of Israel. 

The Kingdom of Judah remained until the time of Nebuchadnezzar, who, as we have already seen, conquered the Jews and took them captive to Babylon. After seventy years, under the rule of Cyrus the Great, they were permitted to return and rebuild their city. Though only part of the exiles returned to Jerusalem, the city and temple were again built. There the Jews lived until 70 or 71 A. D. when the city was destroyed by the Romans. 

Nephite history began eleven years before the Babylonian captivity. (I Nephi 10:4; 19:8; II Nephi 25:19; III Nephi 

1:1) This was 600 B. C., in the beginning of the reign of Zedekiah.

For years many prophets had been among the Jews predicting their destruction and dispersal. The Prophet 

Jeremiah was among the foremost of these.

Lehi was one of the prophets who appeared at that time. 

Because of his preachings, the Jews sought to take his life, and he was warned in a dream to take his family and depart for safety into the wilderness. (I Nephi, chapters 1-4)

II. The Family. 

When they left Jerusalem, the family included Lehi, the father, Sariah, the mother, and four sons, Laman, Lemuel, Sam, and Nephi. (I Nephi 2:5) Two sons, Jacob and 

Joseph, were later born in the wilderness, (18:7) and there were some unnamed sisters, (11 Nephi 5:6) perhaps also born in the wilderness.

This group was joined by Zoram, the servant of Laban, (I Nephi 4:35-38) and the family of Ishmael which numbered five unmarried daughters and two married sons (I Nephi 

7:5-6) besides the parents.

The company, therefore, counted somewhat more than twenty persons.

III. From Jerusalem to Bountiful. 

The Jaredites traveled north from Babel. (Ether 2:1)

The Nephites went southward from Jerusalem to the Red 

Sea. (I Nephi 2:4-5) "And he (Lehi) came down by the borders near the shore of the Red Sea.... and.... when he had traveled three days in the wilderness, he pitched

his tent in a valley by the side of a river of water." (vs.

5-6) 

Lehr Leaves Jerusalem 

There were two possible routes of travel from Jerusalem. One was over the Jordan to the east and over the country of Moab through which the Israelites had entered Canaan so long before. Along this road from Jerusalem to the nearest point on the Gulf of Akaba, an arm of the 

Red Sea, is about 180 miles in our day.

The other route would have led the company almost due south of Jerusalem along the west side of the Dead Sea, a distance of approximately 150 miles today. In either case it was a considerable distance for three days of traveling and suggests that they might have used camels as their means of transportation. This is altogether likely since 

Lehi was a wealthy man. (I Nephi 2:4, 11; 3:16) 

Here is something interesting.

Shortly after the company left Jerusalem, a short stop was made while the four sons of Lehi returned to the city for some records on plates of brass. They met with some difficulty and had to hide in a cave in a rock. (I Nephi 3:28) 

Did Joseph Smith know that there were caves near

Jerusalem in which men could hide? Is this fact well known even today? Is it generally known even to students of the Bible? It comes as a genuine surprise to read of such caves in Harry Emerson Fosdick's A Pilgrimage to Palestine. Is not this mention of caves in the BOOK OF 

MORMON an evidence of the authenticity of the book?

The travelers camped in a valley they called Lemuel. (2:14) It was from there that the sons of Lehi and Sariah returned to Jerusalem for the Brass Plates (chapters 3 and 

4) and later still for the family of Ishmael. (7:2-6) There

87. also the four sons of Lehi, and the servant Zoram, were married to the five daughters of Ishmael. (16:7) There the 

Liahona was given to Lehi. (16:10)

The Liahona was a compass or guide for use in leading the pilgrims to their destination. It is doubtful if any other modern invention has done more to help the rise of civilization than the mariner's compass. Before it was perfected, few men ventured far from land. Without it America might not have been discovered for some time.

The Lord directed Moses and the Israelites in the wilderness with a cloud of smoke by day and a pillar of fire by night, but the Nephites were provided with a compass to direct them on their journey.

The Liahona is described as a "round ball of curious workmanship; and it was of fine brass. And within the ball were two spindles; and the one pointed the way whither we should go into the wilderness." (16:10)

The spindles of the Liahona were controlled by faith instead of by magnetism. This instrument is mentioned many times in the BOOK OF MORMON. (I Nephi 16:10, 27:28; 18:12, 21; II Nephi 5:12; Mosiah 1:16; Alma 37:38-42)

The Jaredites appear to have had no serious contentions prior to their arrival in the new world. Israel under 

Moses was a nation of grumblers. The Nephites were in constant dispute. The Liahona, in addition to directing their way, acted as a daily reminder that the Lord was guiding them.

Before following the Nephites farther it will be necessary to say a few words about the country through which they traveled for eight years.

Arabia is a vast expanse of arid territory lying between 

Egypt and Persia on the west and east and Canaan and the

Indian Ocean on the north and south. It is a high plateau, sloping gently eastward from a mountain range that extends the whole length of it on the west side. This mountain range on the average is about twelve miles from the Red Sea from which it rises to an average height of five thousand feet.

The peninsula is divided naturally into three partsthe great inland center, sparsely dotted with oases and villages, the almost limitless expanse of shifting sands where few people can exist, and a narrow fringe of coastal plains.

These plains are arid, but they are more or less thickly populated and in places are cultivated. 

Owing to its aridity and southern latitude Arabia is one of the hot regions of the earth's surface. "The central desert tracts may be said to possess a healthy climate at all times of the year owing to the dryness of the atmosphere. 

It produces a feeling of invigoration where the wind is from the north. . . . The same effect is experienced along the costal fringe where an excessive humidity is coupled with a maximum summer temperature of ninety degrees on the 

Red Sea." 28

At one time there was an abundance of wild game and fruits along the Red Sea. The Nephites on the whole perhaps experienced little difficulty in providing themselves with food while traveling through the country to Bountiful.

From Jerusalem to the southern coast of Arabia is about 1,500 miles. The Nephite colony required eight years for the journey. They traveled an average of 188 miles a year or about one-half mile a day. Though nothing is said of it, they must have made long stops. 

From the valley of Lemuel they traveled four days in a south-southeast direction and came to a place they called 

Shazer. (16:13) After resting for the replenishing of their food supply they took up the journey in the same direction for "many days." (v. 15)

From this point onward the journey was eastward. (17:1) "And," says Nephi, "we did come to the land which we called Bountiful, because of its much fruit and also wild honey.... And we beheld the sea, which we called Irreantum, which, being interpreted, is many waters." (17:15)

IV. Nephi Builds the Ship. 

Bountiful was the last home of the Nephites in the old world. It must not be confused with the Bountiful which later played so important a part in the lives of the people in the new world.

At Bountiful, Nephi was commanded to build a ship for the voyage to the promised land. We can easily understand that he might build a temple after the form of the one he had seen so many times in Jerusalem, but where had he seen a ship? Nevertheless, the ship was built. (Chapters 17 and 18)

There is not the slightest information as to the size or style of this ship. Nephi disposes of this phase of the matter with a few words. "And we did work the timbers of curious workmanship. And the Lord did show me from time to time after what manner I should work the timbers of the ship. Now 

I, Nephi, did not work the timbers after the manner which was learned by men, neither did I build the ship after the manner of men: but I did build it after the manner which the Lord had shown unto me; wherefore, it was not after the manner of men." (18:1-2) 99 It was very probably a sail ship, large enough to carry the people with sufficient provisions for the trip across the ocean. The only indication there is as to the time required to build the ship lies in the following set of facts: 

The company traveled eight years in the wilderness before reaching Bountiful. (I Nephi 17:4-5) It appears that very shortly after the arrival in Bountiful the construction of the ship began. According to the footnote to I Nephi 19:23 the group landed about 589 B. C. If such was the case, the entire journey from Jerusalem to the new world required eleven years. If we take out the eight spent in traveling in the old world, we discover that three years were required for building the ship and sailing it across to the promised land.

V. From Bountiful to America. 

It is perhaps not important whether the ship was launched into the Persian Gulf or the Indian Ocean. At the beginning the colonists would have gone into the same waters from either point. 

They could have gone west around Africa and landed at any place on the eastern shores of the American continents. Steamships and sailing ships have for generations plied these waters with the aid of the winds and currents. 

The BOOK OF MORMON says nothing about directions or distances of the travels of the Nephite colony. We are left to form our own conclusions from the information available from other sources. There is no evidence that the travelers saw land along the way.

Nephi simply says, "After we had all gone down into the ship, and had taken with us our provisions and the things which had been commanded us, we did put forth into the sea and were driven before the winds towards the promised land." (18:8)

There is, however, conclusive evidence, to be presented later, that the Nephites landed on the western coast of the promised land. (Alma 22:28) They must, then, have sailed east. In this case our problem is not so easy as it was with the Jaredites who probably drifted west with the winds and currents.

In general, if the Nephites crossed the Pacific to the western coast of the new world, they would have gone counter to the prevailing winds and currents since, as has been shown already, the strongest winds move westward. 

Along the heat equator the winds rise instead of blowing west as they do to the north and south. Since there is little or no wind here, this strip is known as the "equatorial calm belt," or the "belt of low pressure." Inasmuch as there is not any wind there to determine the direction of the current, the surface water follows the pull of the earth and deeper water. The result is that at the equator, between the trade wind zones, a current runs counter to the North and South Equatorial Currents. It is known as the 

Equatorial Counter Current. In the Pacific it strikes the western coast of Central America.

The Nephites might have crossed with this current in which case they could have landed in Central America.

For that matter, they could have come with other currents and have landed in Central America. There is very good reason to believe that they did land there. The evidence for this view will be presented in proper order. Pacific Ocean North

All early writers and most recent ones held to the view that the Nephites landed in South America, far down the western coast, at about thirty to thirty-two degrees south latitude. This view was given currency by the pub lication in the Richards and Little COMPENDIUM of a purported revelation of the Prophet Joseph's that established that location as the landing place. As there is some reason to doubt that the statement in question is a revelation, this idea of the landing place of the Nephites is giving way before readers of the record of ancient America. 

To mention only one thing, many readers now find it. increasingly difficult to harmonize with the BOOK OF MORMON the traditional belief that the Nephites moved from southern South America to northern United States.

The present writers are among those who are inclined to be skeptical, and their growing skepticism was one of the moving causes of this study covering many years. 

Nevertheless, this matter of a South American landing may not so easily be disposed of. The origin of the statement is shrouded in some obscurity. Surely no Latter-day 

Saint will question a definite statement from the Prophet 

Joseph, much less a revelation.

CHAPTER TEN FROM THE LANDING TO THE SECOND ALMA 

The purpose of this chapter is to determine whether the places mentioned in the story of the Nephites and Lamanites suggest a geographical set-up with cities and lands, distances, directions, etc., that are definite. These places mentioned will be examined in relation to the lands of the Jaredites and Mulekites for purposes determining the consistency of the record.

A brief recital of the main events in the lives of the Lamanites and Nephites for the period from the landing to the wars of General Moroni will aid us in establishing the relationships between the places mentioned. 

Some time subsequent to the landing of Lehi's colony in the new world, the patriarch called the members of his family together and blessed them. (II Nephi 1-4) He admonished Laman and Lemuel and the sons of Ishmael to be obedient to Nephi, but they rejected his counsel. 

Not long after this, Lehi died, and the disgruntled sons rebelled against Nephi. (5:2) The Lord warned Nephi to leave the old home and seek safety elsewhere. (5:5) Heeding the warning, Nephi and a faithful few set out into the wilderness. (5:7) (5:7) They journeyed many days and then established a new home that, owing to the insistence of the people, was called Nephi. (5:8)

This journey by Nephi and his followers from the first home (Ishmael beyond any reasonable doubt, as will appear later) to Nephi was the only movement of the main body of the Nephites until the time of Mosiah I who led a few north to Zarahemla. From that time forth there appears to have been no rooting up of Nephite civilization. It is true that near the end there was some maneuvering, but this did not involve any large-scale migration seemingly. 

This matter will be discussed fully in connection with the last years of the people.

FROM THE LANDING TO THE SECOND ALMA 93 

This new land abounded in wood, steel, gold, silver, and precious ores. (5:15) (5:15) The people became very proficient in working these raw materials into improvements and weapons of defense.

Nothing whatever is told about the place Nephi beyond the fact already given, but the abundance of raw materials suggests a mountainous region. 

How far Nephi was from the first home at no time appears certain. By indirection we may arrive at a fairly good idea of the distance. Nephi himself gives a good suggestion. In II Nephi 5:34 we read: "And it sufficeth me to say that forty years had passed away, and we had already had wars and contentions with our brethren." 

Another reference throws very great light upon this problem of the distance from the first home, the landing place, to the second, Nephi. Special attention is called to 

Alma 25:13. "And it came to pass that when the Lamanites saw that they could not overpower the Nephites they returned again to their own land; and many of them came over to dwell in the land of Ishmael and the land of Nephi, and did join themselves to the people of God, who were the people of Anti-Nephi-Lehi."

Early writers, and some recent ones, had the Nephites arriving far down the coast of South America. It is almost beyond dispute that the landing place was known as Ishmael. This matter is discussed at some length in chapter 13. 

After the landing and the subsequent dispute between Nephi and Laman and Lemuel, Nephi and his few followers, as we have just seen, left the first home and went into the wilderness where they established a new home, Nephi. One well-known map shows the course of that flight, from about thirty or thirty-two degrees south latitude to northern 

South America, more than one thousand miles!

Yet, Alma explicitly declares that when the Lamanite warriors returned from an invasion against the Nephites in Zarahemla, part of them went to Ishmael and part to Nephi. How could this have happened if the places were one thousand miles of mountainous country apart?

Had the two places been widely separated, there would have been neither opportunity nor necessity for wars and

contentions. 

Another incident is very convincing. (Alma 18:9)

On one occasion, it will be recalled, King Lamoni was invited to a feast in Nephi, a feast given by his father. 

Lamoni lived in Ishmael, the landing place. He gave orders for his horses to be hitched to the chariot for the journey. 

Is it at all likely that Lamoni contemplated a trip across half the length of South America?

During the time of Nephi, Jacob, Enos, Amaron, Jarom, Omni, Chemish, Abinadom, and Amaleki the Nephites remained at Nephi, but the Lamanites had come among them. 

It was Amaleki, adding to the brief record of Omni, who wrote that one Mosiah, living at Nephi, was told by the Lord to take such as would go with him and depart into the wilderness for safety. (Omni 12)

Neither the date of this occurrence nor the number of people involved in the migration is given. 

However, the exiles fled from the tyranny of the Lamanites. They traveled in the wilderness for a time and discovered Zarahemla. (Omni 13-14) Zarahemla was the capital of the Mulekites whose progenitors left Jerusalem during the last year of the reign of King Zedekiah as king of the Jews, eleven years after the departure of the Nephites from Jerusalem. 

CV-HANSEN 

Zenitt bargains with Lamanites

Thus for nearly four hundred years the Mulekites and Nephites existed in the new world, side by side, almost within a stone's throw of each other, so to speak, as we

FROM THE LANDING TO THE SECOND ALMA 95 

shall see in a later chapter, and had no contacts or communication with each other, and no knowledge of each other, so far as we are able to determine from the record. 

Naturally Mosiah and his people left many friends and relatives in Nephi about whom, and their homes and lands, they would cherish tender memories and entertain some concern.

It is not surprising, therefore, that a group later returned to Nephi from Zarahemla to enquire into the welfare of those friends and relatives. Zeniff was the leader of this company. (Mosiah 9:1-4) This occurred during the reign of Mosiah I who had become king in Zarahemla. 

Zeniff and his company were given the cities of Nephi and Shilom in which to live. (9:6-8) This was subterfuge on the part of the Lamanite king for the purpose of bringing the Nephites into bondage. (v. 10) 

Zeniff lived a number of years and was successded as king of the Nephites in the land of the Lamanites by his son Noah, a wicked and selfish ruler who caused almost infinite trouble. (Mosiah 11:1) One of the worst of his sins was the slaying of Abinadi, a noble prophet. (17:20)

Alma, one of Noah's priests, was sympathetic toward Abinadi and incurred the displeasure of the king. He was forced to leave the city of Nephi, and he took refuge in the forest at the waters of Mormon, a fountain in the borders of Nephi. (Mosiah 20:4-5) By day he remained hidden, but at night he returned to Nephi and preached to his friends privately. (20:1-3) So many people believed and followed Alma that his activities came to the notice of Noah, and he sent an army to bring the group to punishment. (Mosiah 18:32-33) Alma was informed of the approach of the army and fled with his converts into the wilderness, a flight that took eight days. (23:3) They stopped at a fertile place they called Helam. (23:19)

Back in Nephi, King Noah, abetted by his unprincipled priests, indulged in all manner of wickedness. When one day a Lamanite army was seen approaching, Noah commanded the men to leave their families and save themselves. (Mosiah 19:11) Some obeyed this order and fled with the king. (v. 12)

It later appears that those who fled with the king were 

In the main his priests.

In the wilderness the king was burned to death by his associates. (vs. 20-23) His priests caused untold sorrow for the Nephites by stealing twenty-four Lamanite girls who were wont to sing and dance in the land of Shemlon. (20:1-5) 

When Noah failed to return to the city, his son Limhi was made king in his place. (vs. 26-27) He was a righteous ruler, but he and his people endured the results of wickedness. They came into bondage to the Lamanites; they were driven like beasts; they were taxed almost beyond endurance. (Mosiah 21)

During this time of suffering Limhi sent out a small company of men with the hope of finding Zarahemla and obtaining help. The men became lost in the wilderness and did not find Zarahemla. They did, however, find the ruins and records of the civilization of the Jaredites. The records they brought back to Nephi with them. (Mosiah 21:25-28)

These records, the twenty-four gold plates having the history of the Jaredites, were discussed in connection with the story of the Jaredites in America.

At this juncture the despairing Nephites received help. 

There came to them one day sixteen men from Zarahemla with one Ammon as leader. (21:23-24) With the support of Ammon and his brethren, Limhi and the Nephites succeeded after a time in escaping into the wilderness one night while their Lamanite guards were intoxicated. (22:110) Under the direction of Ammon they reached Zarahemla. (v. 13)

During this time, a period of some years, Alma and his small colony had lived peacefully at Helam. (23:19-20) ..

When the Lamanites from whom Limhi and his people escaped had become roused from their intoxication, they set out in pursuit of the Nephites. ..They followed the trail for two days and then lost it. (22:16) Then the Lamanites became lost themselves and could not find their way home. (v. 16)

After wandering many days in the wilderness, unable to find their way, the Lamanites came suddenly upon the priests of Noah and the young Lamanite girls, who by now had become their wives. (23:31) The warriors were about to slay the priests, but their wives pleaded for them so

FROM THE LANDING TO THE SECOND ALMA 97 

earnestly that their lives were spared, and they joined the 

Lamanites. (vs. 33-34)

Together they were still wandering when they came upon Helam where Alma and his followers were living. (v. 35)

They promised Alma and his friends that they would grant liberty unto them in return for showing the way back to Nephi, (v. 36) but they broke their promise and put the colony into bondage. (v. 37) 

Later, after much persecution and suffering, Alma and his friends made good their escape and reached Zarahemla after a journey of thirteen days in the wilderness. (Mosiah 

24:20, 25)

In the meantime, at Zarahemla King Mosiah I had died. 

His son, the worthy Benjamin, had reigned many years and passed away, and Mosiah II, King Benjamin's son, had become king. It was he who had sent Ammon and his brethren to Nephi in search of Zeniff and his company. (Mosiah 7:1)

CHAPTER ELEVEN  

In the story of the Nephites and Lamanites, Nephi and Zarahemla are the centers of interest and activity. Nephi was the capital of the Lamanite territory, and Zarahemla was the chief city of the Nephites. Both places are mentioned early in the history, and both are important to the end.

I. Nephi-Lands and City. 

Nephi was the chief city of the land of Nephi. (Alma 47:20)

But this needs some explanation. The expression, "the land of Nephi," has two meanings in the BOOK OF MORMON. In some instances it refers to the whole land of the Lamanites, comprising all their cities and smaller lands. Here are a few examples. "And the land of Nephi did run in a straight course from the east sea to the west." (Alma 50:8)

Obviously this means the whole land of Nephi from coast to coast. "And it came to pass that as Ammon and Lamoni were journeying thither, they met the father of Lamoni, who was king over all the land (of Nephi)." (Alma 29:8)

Ammon, the missionary to the Lamanites, had just converted Lamoni, king of Ishmael, a subdivision of the land of Nephi, who was the son of the king of all the land. 

Each subdivision of the land had its own king. 

Years later the people converted by Ammon and his companions "came into the wilderness which divided the land of Nephi from the land of Zarahemla, and came over near the borders of the land." (Alma 27:14)

This, of course, refers to the whole land of Nephi and

the whole land of Zarahemla. "For, behold, he (Amalickiah) had taken those who went with him, and went up in the land of Nephi among the Lamanites, and did stir up the Lamanites to anger against the people of Nephi, insomuch that the king of the Lamanites sent a proclamation throughout all his land, among all his people, that they should gather themselves together again to go to battle against the Nephites." (Alma 47:1) "Behold, I would tell you somewhat concerning the justice of God, and the sword of his almighty wrath, which doth hang over you except ye repent and withdraw your armies into your own lands, or the land of your possessions, which is the land of Nephi." (Alma 54:6)

These passages are self-explanatory. There can be no question whatever that the Lamanites occupied all the land of Nephi. Many more such citations might be given to show that often the phrase, "the land of Nephi," meant the whole land. But these will be sufficient. 

We shall now examine a few that show unmistakably that another unit is meant, the city of Nephi, or the city and the land immediately adjacent to it, as a subdivision of the whole land. "And we did take our tents and whatsoever things were possible for us, and did journey in the wilderness for the space of many days. And after we had journeyed for the space of many days we did pitch our tents. And my people would that we should call the name of the place Nephi; wherefore, we did call it Nephi" (II Nephi 5:8)

This was the beginning of the city of Nephi. But the place these people had just left, the place of their landing or inheritance, was also part of the whole land of Nephi. 

It was later known as Ishmael and was the home and dominion of Lamoni in the time of Ammon. "And Ammon took three of his brethren, and their names were Amaleki, Helem, and Hem, and they went down into the land of Nephi and behold, they met the king of the people who were in the land of Nephi, and in the land of Shilom; and they were surrounded by the king's guard, and were taken, and were bound, and were committed to prison." (Mosiah 7:6-7)

This was the incident already mentioned in which Am-

mon and his companions came from Zarahemla to the rescue of Limhi and his people in the lands of Nephi and Shilom. The other lands of the whole land of Nephi were inhabited by the Lamanites. The king of the Lamanites had given Ženiff the cities of Nephi and Shilom "and the land round about." (Mosiah 7:21) This "land round about" was the land of Nephi in the narrow sense as a subdivision of the whole land of Nephi.

In Mosiah 9:14 Zeniff makes mention of his reign in the land of Nephi.

But Zeniff reigned only in the small land of Nephi, as a tributary king to the king of the Lamanites who reigned in the whole land of Nephi.

It is perhaps not advisable to spend more time on this phase of the matter. Nevertheless, it is important that every reader understand that there were two lands of Nephi, one within the other. The small land was sometimes called the land of Lehi-Nephi. It was probably much like Utah 

County in Utah State.

There is, however, one very pertinent reference that may be given at this point. "Now the king (Lamoni) had commanded his servants, previous to the time of the watering of their flocks, that they should prepare his horses and chariots, and conduct him forth to the land of Nephi; for there had been a great feast appointed at the land of Nephi, by the father of Lamoni, who was king over all the land (of Nephi)." 

Here both meanings of the expression are evident. 

Lamoni, king of Ishmael (the landing place), a subdivision of the land of Nephi, had been invited to the land of Nephi (in the narrow sense) where his father, king of all the land, had his capital.

Nor is this all. It is beyond all question that other cities were likewise centers of the small lands immediately surrounding them and called by their names. This seems to be the clear intent of the writers of the BOOK OF MOR MON. It makes clear some things that on any other basis are very confusing. 

There is a very good passage in Alma (53:3) that illustrates this point. 66 • · 

Teancum, by the orders of Moroni, caused that

they should commence laboring in digging a ditch round about the land, or the city, Bountiful." 

Just how much land was enclosed within this ditch is not clear, but it certainly was not the whole land of Bountiful from ocean to ocean.

In the war on the western front the Lamanites obtained possession of certain important places, among them the "land of Manti, or the city of Manti." (Alma 56:14)

Again land and city are synonymous. And there are other such instances. There is a reference to the land of 

Lehi-Nephi or the city of Lehi-Nephi. (Mosiah 7:1) Attention has already been called to the citation in which reference is made to the "city of Lehi-Nephi, and the city of Shilom; and the land round about." (Mosiah 7:21) The 

Nephites built a city between Aaron and Moroni, "and they called the name of the city, or the land, Nephihah." (Alma 50:14)

In many instances it is difficult, if not impossible, to determine whether the writer had in mind the large land or the small one, and perhaps often it makes no difference. 

It is only in connection with the four landing countries and two cities that such uncertainty can arise. There were only four large lands to be confused with small ones, Nephi, Zarahemla, Bountiful, and Desolation. 

While it is not certain, it appears that the farms and vineyards were very close to the cities but outside of them. 

The people must have lived within the city walls for protection and worked the surrounding land to a distance that did not take them too far from the security of the cities. 

Upon a cry of alarm the men without would fly to the walls for security from their foes. Hence, there would be many small cities very close to each other. And, indeed, there were.

This is almost an exact parallel of the early Latter-day Saint communities in the West. It is also a counterpart of the ideal Latter-day Saint community with the farms grouped closely about the city with the temple as its center. 

Let us pause here a moment for an explanation before leaving the discussion of Nephi.

It has been suggested that there may have been two cities named Nephi, one far down the coast of South America where we have always supposed the Nephites landed,

and one farther north, corresponding to the city of Nephi discussed here. The exact reason for this view is not altogether clear. It has probably arisen from the necessity of transferring the Nephites from South America to Central America. Two Nephis would fit into this pattern. Nor in logic is this objectionable. The transfer of names is universal. If the Nephites spread over most of the western world, as we may safely assume they did, we should not be surprised to find in their outlying districts names very common to the original homeland. 

The present authors have no particular fault to find with this idea of two Nephis except to urge that they do not find it in the BOOK OF MORMON as they read it. 

They feel that it arises from expediency rather than from the record.

II. Zarahemla. 

Zarahemla was the city of the Mulekites whose forebears migrated to the new world from Jerusalem about 

589 B. C. (II Kings 24; Omni 9, 12, 27-30) It was the capital of the land of Zarahemla, the homeland of the 

Mulekites in the new world. (Helaman 1:27)

There is but one statement as to the location of the city, and it is somewhat general. During the war under 

General Moroni the governor of the land, Pahoran, failed to send supplies and men, and Moroni wrote a letter to him from the east coast. Among other things he wrote: "Or is it that you have neglected us because ye are in the heart of our country and ye are surrounded by security, that ye do not cause food to be sent unto us, and also men to strengthen our armies?"

Zarahemla was "in the heart of the country." Beyond that, nothing explicit is given. But it is possible to locate it with some degree of exactness in spite of that. 

It is very interesting to observe that during the period of four hundred years or more that Nephi and Zarahemla flourished, no contacts took place between them, or at least none is reported, before Mosiah. This phase of the subject is treated in the next chapter. Was this owing to the tremendous distance between them? Or was it the result of some peculiarity in the country that separated them? 

The text (Omni 17) suggests that the Mulekites, or Zarahemlaites, were fairly numerous. At least there were

factions, contentions, and struggles among them. They had been in the new world many years. Beyond that, little is known about them.

When the Nephites under Mosiah I fled to Zarahemla, they literally took it over though by peaceful means. 

Mosiah was made king, and from that time forth the Mulekites were known as Nephites, and the city Zarahemla was the capital of the Nephite civilization.

CHAPTER TWELVE RELATIONSHIPS BETWEEN  

As the story proceeds, a number of relationships are made clear between the two cities. Three will now claim our attention. They are distance, direction, and elevation. 

I. Distance.

The matter of distance between Nephi and Zarahelma will first be presented since it comes close to the heart of the investigation.

A. Distance Indicated by Alma's Activities. 

Alma's work and travels give us a good indication of the distance from Nephi to Zarahemla. 

It will be remembered that Alma, the priest of King Noah, took refuge at the waters of Mormon when he came under the displeasure of his monarch. The first question that naturally arises is this: How far was it from Nephi to the waters of Mormon? 

1. From Nephi to Mormon.

Mormon was in the borders of Nephi. (Alma 5:3) 

This could mean either the little land or the big land of Nephi and is not very helpful. But it could have been in the borders of the big land of Nephi and still be very near the city. When Ammon, Amaleki, Helem, and Hem, after their wandering in the wilderness for forty days, first came to occupied territory, they came to the land of Shilom. (Mosiah 7:7) Evidently Shilom was very near the wilder- 

And Nephi was near Shilom since these two places had been given to the Nephites. Furthermore, Noah had built at Nephi a tower from the top of which he could see the land of Shilom. (Mosiah 11:12) Both must have been near the northern boundary line of the country Nephi. ness.

Mormon was near, or associated with, Jerusalem. (Alma 21:1)

This suggests that Mormon was north and west of Nephi for reasons which will be given in the discussion of the 

Lamanite Mission. 

The citation that is most helpful in locating Mormon with respect to Nephi is that which tells that Alma hid by the fountain by day. (Mosiah 18:5) It follows that by night he went into the city of Nephi and preached to the people who would listen to him. (18:1) The two places were, therefore, so near each other that Alma had quick and easy access from one to the other. They were probably only a few miles apart.

One other bit of information relative to Mormon merits a brief discussion.

Mormon was near the land that was at times infested by wild beasts. (Mosiah 18:4)

The wilderness of Hermounts was filled with wild beasts, (Alma 2:37) but it was in the northern part of 

Zarahemla. And south of the narrow neck of land, during the days of the Jaredites, there was a wilderness filled with wild animals. These two might well have been the same. 

Both were far north of Mormon. ZARAHEMLA 

WEST SER Directions and elevations are quite 

Clear. It is always up" from Zarahem la to nephi and down from nephi to Zarahemla arrow shoo εi- 47ma 24:25 

EAST SEA Strip of wilderness JOURNEY OF ALMAI 

Helam almass Mormon NEPHI 

Again relationships of cities are Suggestive, though distance here is quite definite, measured in days traveled. lays If the conclusions the authors have drawn are correct, Mormon was near the strip of wilderness between Nephi and Zarahemla, and here wild beasts would have been very plentiful. But, for that matter, wild animals were plentiful everywhere. (I Nephi 18:25; Enos 3; Alma 16:10)

A careful reading of the text will almost certainly con-

vince anyone that Mormon was near Nephi, probably a very few miles from the city. 

2. From Mormon to Zarahemla. 

When Alma and his converts were forced to leave Mormon for fear of Noah's army, they made a journey that is, to the student of the BOOK OF MORMON, in importance out of all proportion to its length. Here the time element is given very definitely, and it is one of the few instances in the record in which the time is specified. Hence, from it we are able to get a very important key to the matter of distances.

Alma at that time had about 450 converts. Whether there were small children among them is not certain, but there in all probability were. At any rate they gathered their flocks, their grain, (Mosiah 23:1) and their tents. (v. 5)

That was about everything they could have possessed. The record says: "And they fled eight days' journey into the wilderness." (Mosiah 23:2) 

How far did they travel in those eight days? Was it a great many miles?

When later they were driven by presecution and hardship from Helam where they had been so long, they traveled thirteen days, as we have seen. (Mosiah 24:20, 25) How many miles did they traverse this time? How far was it from Mormon, where Alma actually began his journeying, to Zarahemla where he stopped? What distance could he and his people, burdened as they were, cover in twentyone days?

This is a tremendously important question, one that justifies all the attention that can be given to it. 

A comparable incident may help in answering this question. "In 1636," writes a historian, "the Little River settlements were re-inforced by the coming of the entire church congregation from Dorchester, Winterton, and Newton, which settled separately at Windsor, Weatherfield, and 

Hartford. The Newton (or Cambridge) congregation was under the leadership of its ministers Thomas Hooker and

Samuel Stone. They marched through the wilderness, driving their hogs and cattle along in true pioneer fashion. 

Arriving at the Connecticut, after having spent a fortnight

in going the one hundred miles, they settled around the 

Dutch fort." 20

These people made about seven miles a day, and Alma and his people could have done no better. Indeed, it is altogether likely that the Nephite colony made the slower progress in view of the nature of the country through which it had to move. From this it appears that the distance between the two capital cities could probably not have exceeded one hundred to three hundred miles. This was not the era of roads. The "Mormon” Pioneers averaged about eleven miles a day. Yet, even here the authors desire that these figures be taken only as a suggestion. Obviously many factors that should be taken into consideration are missing. According to the information at hand the foregoing estimate seems sound. 

B. Distance as Indicated by the Journey of Ammon and His Friends.

Ammon, the Zarahemlaite, and his fifteen friends traversed the same distance in the opposite direction. Their journey from Zarahemla to Nephi required forty days. But they were wandering all the time, and they were traveling uphill whereas Alma had gone down. How far did they travel in those forty days? And how far did they go out of their way in their wandering?

Does it not, in view of these incidents in which the time is given, seem to be beyond doubt that Nephi and Zarahemla were fairly close together? Another aspect of this matter will now be presented that will tend to confirm the idea of proximity.

II. Direction. 

There are numerous citations that show Zarahemla to have been north of Nephi. But it is settled definitely by 

Alma in chapter 22, verses 27 to 32. This fundamental relationship of direction with Nephi on the south and Zarahelma on the north is one of the important facts upon which any detailed study of the BOOK OF MORMON is based.

III. Elevation. 

Equally as evident as the matter of direction is one other fact relating to these two important centers. Nephi 

29. Chitwood, History of Colonial America

was in elevation higher than Zarahemla though south of it. 

In nearly every instance in which travel is mentioned from Nephi to Zarahemla, it was down, and from Zarahemla to 

Nephi it was up. (Omni 12, 27-28; Mosiah 28:1, 5-7, 9; Alma 

17:8; 28:5; 30:15; Helaman 6:4) Though Nephi was higher than Zarahemla, there was between the two places, near Nephi, a hill still higher than either, the hill north of Shilom. (Mosiah 7:5) This may account for the fact that in a few instances people are spoken of as having come over from Nephi to Zarahemla, meaning, perhaps, over the higher elevation. (Alma 27:14; 47:29)

This matter of the elevation is closely related to another phase of the same problem that demands discussion. 

This is the nature of the land. This seems to be a logical place in which to present some ideas regarding it. 

IV. The Nature of the Country. 

A strong intimation has already been given that there was something remarkable about the land between Nephi and Zarahemla. For some cause, or perhaps causes, travel was restricted to actual need. The two cities lay fairly near each other for hundreds of years without any contacts. 

Why was this? There is plenty of evidence to show that no one ventured very far from his walls without good reason.

The reason in all probability was in the nature of the country. It was a rough and mountainous land. 

Alma and his colony fled eight days into the wilderness to a place they called Helam. It is significant that they called it Helam. It was not so named before. It was unsettled, perhaps unknown, yet it was within eight or nine days of the old city of Nephi. At Helam they lived for years in perfect security, a very few miles from the homes of the Lamanites. That is very strange. 

When Limhi and his Nephite friends succeeded in escaping from the Lamanite guards in Nephi, they took to the wilderness. When the guards became sober, a matter of a few hours later, they set out in pursuit. They followed the trail of the fleeing Nephites for two days and then lost it. (Mosiah 22:16) 

But a much more surprising thing happened! These

Lamanites became lost and could not find their way home. (Mosiah 22:15-16)

This is an astonishing, an almost incredible situation! 

How could these men have lost themselves hopelessly within two days of the place where they had lived all their lives?

They continued to wander for a time, and one day they came suddenly upon the priests of Noah and their stolen Lamanite wives. These too had been living in the same country in obscurity. The combined group then happened upon Helam where Alma and his friends had been safe for years. How could all this be?

Let us recall for a moment other facts already presented. Limhi sent out from Nephi some men to find 

Zarahemla. They did not find Zarahemla; they passed it twice, on the way to Desolation and back. 

Ammon and his fifteen companions started from Zarahemla to find Nephi. They wandered in the wilderness forty days. 

What is the explanation of all this?

It seems doubtful whether it can still be urged that these people traversed vast stretches of country. In almost every case the travels were limited to days. It was not distance that dominated the people of the BOOK OF MOR- MON; it was rough, forbidding, and dangerous territory. 

Nor are we left to mere speculation on this matter. The record itself gives the answer.

It will be necessary to anticipate for a short time a small portion of the Lamanite king's all-important proclamation. In Alma 22:27 appears the statement that the land of Nephi was separated from the land of Zarahemla by a narrow strip of wilderness that extended across the country from sea to sea.

The word mountain is rarely used in the BOOK OF MORMON. It frequently appears that wilderness is used in the place of it. A rough and mountainous territory would have had the water, timber, ores, and valleys with feed and arable land that would be necessary for a sustained civilization. Rocky country would account for the many failures to follow tracks and trails. Precipitous and animalinfested land would explain the total lack of travel between Nephi and Zarahemla for so long a time. And the tropical precipitation and heat would have brought about the wilderness aspect to the country and the dense timber that

almost certainly figured so prominently in the lives of the people of the BOOK OF MORMON. 

V. Rate of Travel in Central America. 

Closely related to the problem of relationships between Nephi and Zarahemla is the question as to the rate at which it is possible to travel in the land under discussion. A little information will now be offered on this question. 

Is it not altogether likely that the limited distances of Central America have given us who are unacquainted with the country a wrong idea of the time required to traverse it? Should we be surprised to learn that it is no small matter to cross the Isthmus of Panama? In times past many have found to their dismay that a few miles could easily constitute a journey of no mean proportions. "The Isthmus of Darien," writes an American historian, "is not above sixty miles in breadth, but this narrow neck of land which binds together the continents of North and 

South America is fringed by a lofty chain of mountains stretching through its whole extent. "The mountains are covered with forests almost inaccessible. The valleys in that moist climate, where it rains during two-thirds of the year, are marshy, and frequently over-flowed so that the inhabitants find it necessary in many places to build their houses on trees." 30 

It might be objected that there is no mention in the BOOK OF MORMON of such rains as are mentioned by Mr. Robertson. It is true that there is perhaps not a reference to it. But neither is there any mention of snow. In fact, while climate is fairly evident, the manifestations of the weather are nowhere indicated. But is not this about what would be expected? What reason is there to call attention to rain, or snow, or wind when it is the usual thing? 

Writing of this same country another famous historian says, in connection with the Spaniards: "It is a difficult trip of 45 miles through the tropical forests, those dark forests of high trees festooned so thickly with vines and creepers. Even with the ax the Spaniards can hardly break through." "1 31 Another illustration from Robertson is to the point. 

30. Robertson, William, History of America, pp. 201-2 

31. Rugg, Harold, A History of American Civilization, p. 47

This author tells (p. 203) that in 1513 Balboa landed on the east coast of Darien. He had 190 men and one thousand 

Indians to carry his provisions. But he found that his progress was impeded by many obstacles. There were, of course, human enemies. But whereas the guide had represented to Balboa that the isthmus could be crossed in six days, the company spent more than twenty-five. Thus they made little more than two miles a day. 

If such country separated Zarahemla and Nephi, it is not surprising that no contacts took place between them. 

Nor is it so difficult to understand how the Lamanite warriors could get lost within two days of their homes. But we should not forget that these descriptions are of comparatively recent times. It might not be wise to take them as representative of more ancient epochs.

Let us pause a moment for a further examination of this matter of distances. 

What is meant by a "day-and-a-half's journey"? This question cannot be answered with any degree of certainty, but a comment is in order.

Is it not significant that the expression is used two times, with a slight variation, and in connection with different places? Is not the explanation that it was a unit of measurement? A story recently read by one of the authors makes mention of so many "lengths" and "breadths." Surveyors speak in terms of "chains." Some people measure in yards and others in meters. But in the Bible itself we find an almost exact parallel. 

We read there of the "sabbath day's journey." What does it mean? It was the distance from the tents of Israel to the Ark, the distance an Israelite might under the Law travel on the Sabbath. It was a definite unit of distance, about two thousand yards. Is it not altogether likely, then, that the "day's journey for a Nephite" was a standard unit of distance? Such being true, the narrow strip of land could well have been more than a Nephite could traverse in a day, or a day and a half. Its total distance might have been such as a Nephite could cover on level ground and with favorable conditions for traveling.

3447 

COLLE 

Lost Sheep

In passing, it should be said that the coming of the Mulekites and Nephites (and for that matter, the Jaredites) to the new world was an essential part of the plan of God for the scattering of Israel. Those who were taken into far countries are known as the lost sheep of the House of Israel.

CHAPTER THIRTEEN LAMANITE LANDS AND CITIES 

Consideration will now be given to other locations of interest and importance associated with the central cities, Nephi and Zarahemla. Some of those now to be mentioned will assume considerable significance in later phases of the discussion. In fact much of the value of presenting these facts at this time lies in their relation to matters yet to be developed. 

I. The Lamanite Mission. 

In 92 B. C. the four sons of Mosiah II, Ammon, Aaron,

Omner, and Himni, and a few friends, left Zarahemla, where Mosiah was king, and went "up to the land of Nephi" to preach the gospel to their brethren, the Lamanites. (Mosiah 28:1) Arrived at the land of Nephi, they separated and went their several ways.

Ammon went to the land of Ishmael. (Alma 17:19) 

Lamoni was king of the land. He was a direct descendant of the Ishmael who came over from Jerusalem with Lehi. (v. 21) He was the son of the king who ruled over all the land of the Lamanites.

Two references say that the great king was ruler over all the land. (Alma 18:9; 20:8) But one points out that he was king over all but Ishmael. 

There is no contradiction here. Lamoni was freed from all accountability to his father as a result of his associations with Ammon. The great king made him independent. (Alma 20:26-27; 21:21)

A note will be helpful here. In all cases except Nephi, Zarahemla, Bountiful, Desolation, and Lehi and Mulek (see chapter 21) the term "land of" refers to the small land adjacent to the city. As indicated earlier, in connection with these principal lands it often means as well the whole land.

The following citations show the relationship of land to city: Mosiah 7:1, 21; Alma 16:8; 25:1; 43:25; 50:14; 

53:3; 56:14; 61:8.

LAMANITE CITIES 

There were doubtless Lamanites in the east, but there is no definite in- 

Jormation about them. 

Again,locations are but suggestive. no coasts are, here Indicated.

Ishmael is not located with any great detail, but its relative position is fairly definite. Moreover, much can be learned about it with respect to other places. 

Ishmael was one of the lands of the Lamanites that came under the influence of the Nephite missionaries. (Alma 

17:19-20) It was named for the sons of that Ishmael who came over from Jerusalem with Lehi. (v. 19) It was probably near Middoni and was almost certainly in close association with it. (Alma 20)

Two other statements about Ishmael are of first importance. "And it came to pass that Ammon and Lamoni returned from the land of Middoni to the land of Ishmael, which was the land of their inheritance." (Alma 21:18)

If "the land of their inheritance" means the place where they landed in the new world, as it must surely do, we have here a good indication of the landing place of the Nephites. The next citation locates it even more definitely. "Now, the more idle part of the Lamanites lived in the wilderness on the west in the land of Nephi, in the place of their fathers' first inheritance, and thus bordering along by the seashore." (Alma 22:28)

LAMANITE LANDS AND CITIES 

115 Thus Ishmael was on or near the seacoast, almost beyond question the western coast, and it seems to have been the place where the Nephites came to the promised land. This is reasonable. The sons of Lehi had had places named in their honor; it was only natural that the sons of 

Ishmael should likewise have places named for them. (Alma 8:7; 17:19)

Ishmael was in the land of Nephi-the large land-on the western shore, some distance from the city and small land of Nephi, and it seemed to be relatively near Zarahemla.

Ishmael was the "land of their fathers' first inheritance." 

So also was Nephi. (Mosiah 9:1) The land of Ishmael, as a subdivision of the land of Nephi, was evidently the landing place and, therefore, the land of the first inheritance, but, since Ishmael was a part of Nephi, since Nephi comprised Ishmael, the land of the first inheritance, it was also the land of the first inheritance. The whole includes all its parts, and one of the parts of Nephi was the land of the first inheritance. In the same way the Pioneers settled 

Salt Lake City, a subdivision of Utah. In so doing they settled Utah. 

A question might be asked in passing: How would 

Joseph Smith keep all these details, and countless others, straight in his mind? He could not have done it. Is this not positive evidence of the divinity of the record? B. Middoni.

Only two things are told about Middoni. 

It was part of the Lamanite Mission, (Alma 21) and it was near, or held close connection with, Ishmael. (Alma 

22 and 23) 

C. Ani-Anti. 

Ani-Anti was a villiage. (Alma 21:11) 

It was probably near Middoni. (v. 12)

D. Jerusalem. 

Jerusalem was an important city in the mission district.

(Alma 22:1-2) 

As already noted, Jerusalem was near the waters of 

Mormon. (Alma 21:1) It is for this reason that the authors feel that Mormon was north and west of Nephi. The 

Lamanite cities, at least those in the mission, seem to have

been in the west in the neighborhood of Ishmael. No eastern city is named though there were perhaps some in the eastern section. (Alma 22:27)

Another reason for believing that the bulk of the Lamanite population was in the west is the frequency with which the armies appeared in the western part of Zarahemla in comparison with the number of times in which they went directly to the east.

Mormon was also near the wilderness, or "in the borders of the land." (Mosiah 18:21) If this was the narrow strip of wilderness, discussed in the following charter, it was in the very nature of things north of Nephi. This appears to be the right of it. If, therefore, Mormon was near Jerusalem, and Mormon was in the northwest, Jerusalem was in the northwest also. 

E. Nephi.

Nephi was, of course, in the Lamanite Mission. It was there that the great king of all the Lamanites was converted by Aaron. (Alma 23:16-18; 24:1-4)

No further discussion of the city Nephi is needed at this time. 

F. Shilom.

Shilom was very closely related to the city of Nephi. 

When Zeniff and his people went from Zarahemla to Nephi to enquire into the welfare of the friends their people had left there at the exodus under Mosiah, the Lamanites gave them the lands of Nephi and Shilom in which to live. (Mosiah 7:21)

Here the city and land of Nephi are called Lehi-Nephi, perhaps to distinguish the small land from the large land of the same name.

From the time of Zeniff to the time of his grandson Limhi, who led the Nephites out of the land, the people dwelt in Nephi and Shilom. 

When Ammon, the Zarahemlaite, and fifteen companions, going also from Zarahemla to enquire into the welfare of Zeniff and his colony, had finished their forty days of wandering in the wilderness, they came out upon a hill that was north of Shilom. (Mosiah 7:5)

This seems to make clear the fact that Shilom was

LAMANITE LANDS AND CITIES 

117 north of Nephi, nearer the wilderness. The hill north of 

Shilom is mentioned again in Mosiah 7:16. It was very near 

Shilom and Nephi. In Nephi, King Noah had built a tower from the top of which he could see the lands of Shilom and Shemlon. (Mosiah 11:12) They were evidently all in a compact setting. 

G. Shemlon.

As already pointed out, Shemlon was near enough to 

Nephi that Noah could see it from his tower.

The direction of Shemlon from Nephi is not given, but it is practically determinable from related facts. 

It was from the land of Shemlon that the priests of Noah stole the twenty-four Lamanite maidens who were dancing in the forest. (Mosiah 20:1-5) But have we not seen that the 

Lamanites were probably to the west as a general thing?

Then, the maidens would have been in the wilderness to the west. Furthermore, the priests of Noah, who later fled into the wilderness, were somewhere in the neighborhood of Alma at Helam, in the north and west. Hence, Shemlon was probably west of Nephi and Shilom. 

H. Lemuel.

Lemuel was in the territory of the Lamanite Mission. (Alma 23:12) Beyond that fact nothing is known about it. 

J. Helam. 

Helam was in the missionary territory. (Alma 24:1)

Helam, we remember, was the place in the wilderness, eight days distant from Mormon, in which Alma and his converts sought security when pursued by the army of King Noah. Alma and his friends would have had to leave the settled section of the land. Hence, Helam was in the wilderness. Thus it was north of Nephi. But if Mormon was a little to the west, near Jerusalem, Helam would in all likelihood have been northwest of Nephi. And that it was somewhat to the west is almost certain from the fact that Mormon could not have been directly north of Nephi since that was the position occupied by Shilom, and Shilom extended clear to the strip of wilderness. 

K. Amulon.

Amulon was named for the leader of the priests of 

Noah who stole the Lamanite girls and fled to the refuge of the wilderness. (Mosiah 24:31) It was a mission city.

(Alma 24:1) 

It appears very likely that Amulon too was in the wilderness to the north and west. The Lamanite army hunting Limhi and his vanished Nephites discovered Amulon and his associates at Amulon. (Mosiah 23:30-31) Together they later found Alma and his people at Helam. (v. 35) Thus they were all together, practically speaking. Moans Priests Steal Lamanite girls Much of the foregoing argument is based upon the assumption that the Lamanites were thickest in the western portion of their land. The evidences appear conclusive, but if that was not the case, much that has just been reported would have to be modified. 

II. Non-Mission Places. 

To be strictly accurate, there were few, if any, Lamanite cities of the period of the mission that were not included in the territory covered by the missionaries. It is likely that the missionaries easily traversed, perhaps many times, the seemingly limited territory of the Lamanites in their fourteen years. Yet, there may have been cities into which they did not go.

But aside from the reference in the Lamanite Proclamation to probable cities in the east, there is another citation that might be interpreted to mean that there were people in the east. If there were, and it is likely, and those cities we have seen were in the west, there may have been cities other than those included in the mission. 

The other reference that seems to suggest people in the east is Alma 25:5-8 which tells of two different groups of

LAMANITE LANDS AND CITIES 

119 Lamanites who were driven into the east wilderness, one of which "usurped power and authority over the Lamanites." (v. 5) 

The only uncertainty arises in connection with the place from which the wilderness was east. It could have been some of the cities named above and still have the wilderness east of them in the western part of the land, or it might mean the wilderness to the east of Nephi and Shilom.

In closing this section the authors consider it wise to call attention to Alma 25:13: "And it came to pass that when the Lamanites saw that they could not overpower the Nephites they returned again to their own land; and many of them came over to dwell in the land of Ishmael and the land of Nephi." 

According to the pattern established in this chapter the Lamanite cities were mostly in the west with Ishmael on the extreme west, on the coast, and Nephi somewhat inland with all the rest in a general way between them. Yet, the Lamanites returning from an invasion of Zarahemla entered those two cities.

A question naturally arises. Did the group split up and separate widely? This is doubtful. Rather it seems that the two cities were after all fairly near each other. 

Does all this mean that the land was compact and small?

III. The Ammonites. 

The missionaries who spent fourteen years among the Lamanites did not labor in vain. They converted many people. The number is not given, but it was certainly many hundred, and the probability is that there were thousands. 

They found it impossible to remain after their conversion among their erstwhile associates. They had taken an oath that they would never again bear arms against anyone. They buried all their weapons in the earth. (Alma 

24:16) Thereafter they had to move.

They were persuaded by the missionaries to go to Zarahemla and ask protection of the Nephites. This design was followed out. The converted Lamanites were given the land of Jershon for their home. Thereafter those wonderful people remained with the Nephites and were known as the Ammonites or the Anti-Nephi-Lehis.

CHAPTER FOURTEEN  

We come now to one of the most puzzling, interesting, and significant parts of the whole study. It is the heart of the whole matter of geography of the BOOK OF MOR- 

MON.

But important as it is, it is not sufficient alone as a guide to the study. It is in fact unintelligible except in the light of other citations. No single passage, however long or comprehensive, can supply, taken alone, an adequate basis for determining distances, directions, lands, and landing places. The whole record must be considered. 

After Aaron had converted the great king of all the land of the Lamanites, as told in the foregoing chapter, the happy monarch sent out to all his people a proclamation granting free access to the missionaries in all homes, cities, churches, and lands of the Lamanites. 

I. The Proclamation Considered. 

Alma, chapter 22, verses 27-32, gives the following information about the proclamation. It was sent to all the 

Lamanites "throughout all the land, amongst all his people who were in all his land, who were in all the regions round about, which was bordering even to the sea, on the east and on the west and which was divided from the land of Zarahemla by a narrow strip of wilderness, which ran from the east sea even to the sea west, and round about on the borders of the seashore, and the borders of the wilderness which was on the north by the land of Zarahemla, through the borders of Manti, by the head of the river Sidon, running from the east towards the west-and thus were the 

Lamanites and Nephites divided. "Now the more idle part of the Lamanites lived in the wilderness, and dwelt in tents; and they were spread through the wilderness on the west, in the land of Nephi;

yea, and also on the west of the land of Zarahemla, in the borders by the seashore, and on the west in the land of Nephi, in the place of their fathers' first inheritance, and thus bordering along by the seashore.
