Mephites were nearly Lamanites surrounded by the alma 22:29 "And also there were many Lamanites on the east by the seashore, whither the Nephites had driven them. And thus the Nephites were nearly surrounded by the Lamanites; nevertheless the Nephites had taken possession of all the northern parts of the land bordering on the wilderness, at the head of the river Sidon, from the east to the west, round about the wilderness side; on the north, even until they came to the land which they called Bountiful. "And it bordered upon the land which they called Desolation, it being so far northward that it came into the land which had been peopled and destroyed, of whose bones we have spoken, which was discovered by the people of Zarahemla, it being the place of their first landing."

The antecedent of "their" here is probably the Mulekites as already shown. "And they came from there up into the south wilderness. Thus the land on the northward was called Desolation, and the land on the southward was called Bountiful, it being the wilderness which is filled with all manner of wild animals of every kind, a part of which had come from the land northward for food. "And now it was only the distance of a day and a half's journey for a Nephite, on the line Bountiful and the land Desolation, from the east to the west sea; and thus the land

of Nephi and the land of Zarahemla were nearly surrounded by water, there being a small neck of land between the land northward and the land southward.”

This will now be discussed in the light of all the facts having any bearing upon it. 

It must be borne in mind that these paragraphs are Mormon's abridgment of the writing of Alma, or, perhaps, even the words of Mormon himself, and not a detailed original. It is very interesting to observe that whereas everyone is aware that the Book of Ether is an abridgment, many people seem not to remember that the story of the Nephites is also an abridgment. With the exception of a number of quotations and they are not always indicated as such everything from the end of the Book of Omni to the Books of Mormon and Moroni is a condensed account. It becomes clear immediately that most details are lacking. The BOOK OF MORMON as we have it is not a treatise on geography. It is concerned with other things, and geography is only incidental.

Mormon certainly knew all the facts himself, and his explanations, in the light of his own knowledge, were complete and comprehensive. But with our limited information we frequently find them confusing and uncertain. 

Let us remember these few important things in interpreting the proclamation.

The land of Nephi, says the record, bordered "even to the sea, on the east and on the west." That is, it extended from sea to sea across the whole country, whatever country it was or is. In this connection it is instructive to see also 

Alma 50:8, wherein we read: "And the land of Nephi did run in a straight course from the east sea to the west."

Thus it was bounded on the east and west by the seas or oceans. There is no indication at all as to the southern boundary.

This land of Nephi, or the land of the Lamanites, was separated from the land of the Nephites, Zarahemla, by a narrow strip of wilderness" which ran from the east sea even to the sea west." This strip of wilderness extended across the land, not up and down or north and south. Comments already given indicate that this wilderness was a very difficult and forbidding piece of territory that was probably very heavily wooded.

The wilderness was near the river Sidon. The river

almost certainly headed in the strip at some point, probably toward the center. The river flowed in general north. 

The Lamanites had pushed over the strip of wilderness on both the east and west into the extremities of the land of the Nephites with the result that the Nephites were almost surrounded by Lamanites. Actually the Lamanites were on three sides of them-the east, the west, and the south, in their own land.

The Nephites had possession of the northern part of the land from the narrow strip of wilderness, beyond Bountiful even to and including the land of Desolation. In the wars of Moroni, to be considered later, the Nephites drove the Lamanites back across the wilderness strip on the east and west into their own land.

north and south they were as here indicated.

The land Bountiful was north of Zarahemla, and Desolation was north of Bountiful. This is perhaps as good a place as any in which to give a few additional facts about Bountiful, or observations that appear to be facts. On the line between Bountiful and Desolation the distance from sea to sea was a day-and-a-half's journey for a Nephite. 

On the southern boundary of Bountiful, the Zarahemla line, the distance was a day's journey. (Helaman 4:7) It thus becomes clear that Bountiful must have been a tiny land of little importance as to size. Indeed, its very smallness was the reason for its importance. It seems to be beyond question that Bountiful was the NARROW NECK OF LAND of which a great deal will yet be said, and thus easily fortified because of its smallness. 

Northern Zarahemla, Bountiful, or southern Desolation

was the landing place of either the Mulekites or Jaredites, or both. It was probably the Mulekites who came there whereas the Jaredites seem to have reached the new world farther north. When we learn later that there was a city 

Mulek right at the point under discussion, the line of Zarahemla and Bountiful, we see the extreme likelihood that it was the landing place of the second Jerusalem colony. 

For the present at least it is reasonable to assume that the Jaredites landed on the eastern coast of Central America or Mexico, probably the latter, the Mulekites, on the eastern coast of Central America, and the Nephites, on the western coast of Central America somewhat farther south. 

Desolation, whether part or all the land of the Jaredites, was the northernmost land of the Nephites, according to the pattern given by Alma. Tiny Bountiful was immediately to the south. Bounding Bountiful on the south was Zarahemla, and south of Zarahemla, over the narrow strip of wilderness, was Nephi.

It will be interesting to see whether this set-up is consistent throughout.

A. A Guess at Relationships. 

A few somewhat elusive connections have throughout the study hovered, as it were, on the periphery of definite fact, defying a complete understanding. Yet these relationships are important, and an attempt will now be made to marshal them into some semblance of order. However, readers should bear in mind that this is at best but speculation. Indeed, it comes as near to a forcing of interpretations as any explanation in the book. Yet, granting even this, it may not be so far from the truth. Certainly there seems to be in the material comprising the sourcebook of this study nothing contrary to it. And as certainly no harm can come from it.

Obviously the difficulty arises out of the incompleteness of the history. We may too easily forget that in a few pages we often read the story of many years. The following explanation is based upon a universally-known truth that boundary lines are unstable in the extreme among shifting and warring people as in Europe in 1939. For once at least it would be a very helpful thing to establish a boun dary line in the BOOK OF MORMON. 

The problem grows out of the uncertainty surround-

ing the relationship of the lands to each other. If we might take the pattern given in Alma 22 as representing the setup of lands throughout the entire record, the difficulty would probably be eliminated at once, but it is not nearly so simple as that. Alma-or Mormon, injecting his explanations into the record of Alma-gives the following arrangement as we have just seen. To the south was Nephi, the land of the Lamanites. It was separated from Zarahemla, the land of the Mulekites and Nephites, by a narrow strip of wilderness. So far the relationship appears to have been maintained all through the history of the BOOK OF MOR- MON. To complete the arrangement given in the Book of 

Alma, we find Bountiful immediately to the north of Zarahemla, and Desolation again to the north of that. 

Here we get into difficulty. Does this convenient disposition of countries obtain throughout the history? Apparently this is far from the case. During the hundreds of years covered by the narrative the dividing lines seem to have changed again and again, particularly in the case of the land of Bountiful.

Let us see if we can isolate the specific problem and then attempt a solution of it.

The Jaredites seem to have landed on the eastern coast and moved inland to a site that was satisfactory for a permanent seat of government. The capital was known as 

Moron, and it was probably some distance from the coast. 

From it the people must have spread in every direction though it appears that the main body of their population was to the south.

Was Moron the capital of Desolation, or were Moron and Desolation both parts of the land of the Jaredites? In other words, was Desolation the land of the Jaredites or only the southern part of it? Was all the land of the Jaredites laid waste, or was just the southern portion near the hill Ramah so denuded of timber? 

The uncertainty comes from the following quotation. "Now the land of Moron, where the king dwelt, was near the land which is called Desolation by the Nephites." (Ether 7:6)

This may be variously interpreted. The land of Desolation mentioned here might have been the small land and city. In that case the situation becomes clear. Moron, the

capital of the Jaredites, was toward the northern end of the land of the Jaredites, and Desolation was at the southern end. Yet, they could have been near each other. 

But if the land of Desolation in this paragraph has reference to the large land of Desolation, it suggests that the northern part of the land of the Jaredites, that around Moron, was not laid waste, that only the southern part was called Desolation. 

There is no way of knowing for a certainty, but there is good reason to think that the city of Desolotion was the great city built by the Jaredites and mentioned in Ether 10:20.

Such a city was surely of great importance in the last wars of the Nephites. Says Mormon: "And it came to pass that I did cause my people that they should gather themselves together at the land of Desolation, to a city which was in the borders, by the narrow pass which led into the land southward." (Mormon 3:5)

The early years of the war passed with the fighting right at the city of Desolation. (Mormon 2-4) At one time by common consent the Lamanites held the land south of the narrow pass, and the Nephites held the land north of it. 

There can be but little question that their headquarters at this time was the city of Desolation, at the narrow pass. 

A whole chapter is devoted later to the problems of the narrow pass, but we may anticipate at this time to say that the narrow pass was geographically the same as the narrow neck of land though in feature they might have been different.

It was unquestionably at that point that it was a dayand-a-half's journey from sea to sea. This was, of course, the boundary between Desolation and Bountiful. (Alma 22:30-32)

But now another element of uncertainty enters into the situation. "And there came forth poisonous serpents also upon the face of the land, and did poison many people. And it came to pass that their flocks began to flee before the poisonous serpents, towards the land southward, which was called by the Nephites Zarahemla." (Ether 9:31)

Here again we have the land southward, the land just

over the narrow pass or neck of land from Desolation, the southern end of the land of the Jaredites. This land southward was called Zarahemla by the Nephites. 

But according to Alma 22 the land just south of the narrow neck of land was Bountiful. The writers have failed to find any mention of Bountiful following the visit of Jesus to the western world. Does this mean that the people moved away from Bountiful? Not at all. It seems to mean rather that Bountiful, in Alma's time a fairly well-defined land in itself, at a later date became properly part of Zarahemla. That Bountiful was considered part of Zarahemla is clear from III Nephi 3:23. As a matter of fact Bountiful was probably at all times an integral part of either Zarahemla or Desolation, usually the former. 

Here is where the straining of interpretation comes in. 

It seems that Bountiful at one time or another must have been part of Desolation. Here is the reason for the statement. The city of Mulek was south of Bountiful, the city, but very near it. (See chapter 19) Did Mulek mark the landing place of the Mulekites? It is probable, since Zarahemla was the capital of the Mulekites, and it was but a short distance from Mulek, but inland as was Moron. But 

Zarahemla, the city, was in Zarahemla, the land. Was, then, Mulek likewise in the land of Zarahemla? It would seem so but for one thing. 

The Mulekites discovered the land of the Jaredites and lived in it. Hence, if Mulek was the landing place of the 

Mulekites, the land of the Jaredites must have extended clear to the land of Zarahemla. But what of Bountiful, supposedly between?

We may suggest, though the suggestion must not be taken for fact, that the Jaredites did inhabit the country as far south as Zarahemla proper though in the main their country was a little distance farther north. 

The Mulekites landed near the southern extremity of the country inhabited by the Jaredites and pushed a short distance to the north, into Desolation proper, and to the south, into Zarahemla proper. Thus, they lived in the land of the Jaredites, and they also peopled a new land. They lived for hundreds of years before they were found by the Nephites, and they could easily have spread over considerable territory. In Desolation they would have found Coriantumr.

If these speculations are sound, the city of Mulek was at one time in Desolation, or at least the land of the Jaredites, and at other times in Zarahemla. Or, for that matter, some of the Mulekites could have pushed at once into the land to the north. Or Mulek might not have marked the landing place of the Mulekites.

CHAPTER FIFTEEN THE REBELLIONS OF NEHOR AND AMLICI 

Alma, the son of that Alma who led his people in two successive journeys from Mormon to Zarahemla, was an ambitious but wayward youth. With the four sons of King

Mosiah II he went about destroying the church and bringing sorrow to his noble father. (Mosiah 27:8-9) These youths were miraculously converted and thereafter spent their entire lives and energies in the cause of righteousness. 

About 92 B. C. the government of the people was changed from a monarchy to a system of judges. The four sons of Mosiah had recently gone on a mission to the Lamanites, (Mosiah 28:1-8) and the youthful Alma had recently been appointed to the high office of high priest over the church. (29:42) Then, upon the death of Mosiah, he was elected chief judge as well. (v. 44) 

I. Nehor.

During Alma's first year as chief judge a cunning and influential man, named Nehor, drew away many followers. (Alma 1:2-15) Much trouble and persecution followed, so much, indeed, that the church was in danger. (vs. 16-33)

Nehor was tried, condemned, and executed, but this did not put an end to the troubles. 

II. Amlici. 

About 87 B. C. another very powerful man, Amlici by name, aspired to be king. (Alma 2:2) An election was called to settle the issue, and the vote went against Amlici. (2:7) 

This did not settle the matter, for Amlici immediately got together his adherents and led them against Alma and the rest of the people to try the question with the sword. (vs. 8-14) Amlici stationed his forces on the hill Amnihu, "which was east of the river Sidon, which ran by the land of Zarahemla.” (v. 15)

The "land of Zarahemla" can only be interpreted here to mean the small land around the city of Zarahemla. The river flowed past the city and down to the sea, almost certainly on the east. The hill Amnihu is nowhere else mentioned.

Alma and his soldiers went against the rebels and routed them in a bloody combat (vs. 16-19) until they fled.

Alma pursued one day, and at night he rested his men in the valley of Gideon. (v. 20)

As night came on, Alma sent four spies to follow the Amlicites, and the next day the spies rushed back with a most astonishing report. "Behold, we followed the camp of the Amlicites," they declared, “and to our astonishment, in the land of Minon, above the land of Zarahemla, in the course of the land of Nephi, we saw a numerous host of the Lamanites; and behold the Amlicites have joined them." 

Hence, Minon was south of Gideon where Alma was camped, and both were south of Zarahemla. "In the course of the land of Nephi" can only mean on the way toward the land of Nephi because the Lamanites were in Minon, not in Nephi. They were on their way from Nephi to Zarahemla. 

According to the scouts the people of "that land" (Minon) were fleeing with their "flocks, and their wives, and their children" toward Zarahemla. (v. 25) 

How many people would be thus fleeing? Would it be millions, hundreds of thousands, thousands, or hundreds? 

How big were the lands involved here? Were they tremendous stretches of country? Or were they small territories surrounding relatively small cities?

Upon hearing the disturbing news brought by the scouts, Alma took his soldiers and left the valley of Gideon to return to Zarahemla. (v. 26) 

It is perfectly clear that Gideon was between Minon and Zarahemla.

As they were crossing the Sidon to go to Zarahemla, the Nephites were set upon by the enemy on the west bank of the Sidon. (v. 34) Amlici was slain by Alma, (v. 31) and the numberless Lamanites were defeated. The bodies of the dead were thrown into the river to float down to the

THE REBELLIONS OF NEHOR AND AMLICI 131 

ocean; (v. 34) the living fled toward the wilderness north and west. Fighting as they fled, the survivors reached the comparative safety of the wilderness of Hermounts. (vs. 36-38) Here we pause. There is much to be said, but little evidence for any of it. 

Where was the wilderness west and north?

It is a foregone conclusion that there was forest and jungle all through the country of the ancient Americans, but the location of this wilderness of Hermounts is hard to determine. There is no cross reference to aid us, owing, of course, to the fact that there are no statements that clearly connect up with the one in question. Perhaps we can in a round-about way come to some conclusion regarding it.

There was wilderness adjoining many of the cities. To mention only one example, on one occasion Moroni marched his men by night into the wilderness just west of the city of Mulek. It is doubtful, however, that these wildernesses in the settled country were large. 

Still, the case is not altogether hopeless.

The Amlicites marched into the wilderness west and north. But west and north of what? Was it west and north of Minon? This is unlikely since Minon was in a thicklysettled part of the country, and it is to be doubted that there were forests large enough there to offer security to the enemy. In fleeing west and north the Amlicites and 

Lamanites would necessarily have passed some cities that offered little place of refuge.

There is, however, an answer that appears to the writers as being the logical one.

We must remember that in the days of Lib, the renowned Jaredite hunter, the wilderness south of the narrow neck of land was preserved for game. (Ether 10:21)

This wilderness corresponded very closely with Bountiful and northern Zarahemla. When the Lamanites and Amlicites reached the wilderness, many of the wounded were killed by the wild beasts. (Alma 2:38) Is it not unlikely that fierce beasts in any numbers would have been living in too close proximity to the cities? In the unsettled wilderness there would naturally have been many of them.

The wilderness thus designated would have been west. and north of the city of Zarahemla and the rest of the cities of the land of Zarahemla.

It is interesting, if this suggested set-up is true, to note that the defeated invaders fled across almost the entire length of the land of Zarahemla, from south to north, and away from the direction of their own land.

There is one other point with reference to this matter that is pertinent. Alma 22:31 contains a statement that tends to confirm the idea set forth above. "And the land on the southward was called Bountiful, it being the wilderness which is filled with all manner of wild animals of every kind." This does confuse matters also, but it shows that 

Desolation, Bountiful, northern Zarahemla, and the wilderness were close and compact. Bountiful, as already shown, could well have been northern Zarahemla.

It is readily admitted by the authors that this is not a very full nor convincing statement of the location of the wilderness of Hermounts. So far as they are aware, however, there is no evidence contrary to that given. After all the place is of relatively little importance.

CHAPTER SIXTEEN THE SECOND ALMA 

By the ninth year of the rule of the judges, about 83 B. C., the wickedness in the church had increased to alarming proportions. (Alma 4) Alma was naturally very deeply concerned about the situation. He studied this new crisis in his people's affairs carefully and humbly and then came to a very characteristic decision.

I. Alma's First Mission. 

Alma resigned the judgment seat to a righteous man named Nephihah, retaining for himself the office of high priest over the people of the church. (4:17) He then began a reform movement by preaching to the people in Zarahemla and adding officers to the church organization. (Alma 5; 6:1-7) 

When he had completed his work in Zarahemla, he suddenly left the city and, crossing to the east side of the 

Sidon, went to Gideon. (v. 7)

The geography here agrees in detail with that given heretofore. The Sidon flowed past Zarahemla on the east. 

Its direction was, of course, north since Zarahemla was down; Gideon was east of the river. We have already seen that Alma's army met the Amlicites on the west bank after crossing the river from Gideon.

In the end of the ninth year Alma returned to Zarahemla to rest. (Alma 8:1-2) But in the beginning of the tenth year he went over to Melek, on the west of the Sidon, by the borders of the wilderness. (Alma 8:3) 

This calls for some explanation. 

That Melek was in general west of Gideon is clear since the river Sidon flowed between them. It was probably the dividing line. It is not possible to determine whether Melek was due west or to the north or south some distance from

Gideon.

That Melek was west by the borders of the wilderness does not necessarily mean that it was by the west wilderness. This might easily have been true though it is to be doubted that there was at that point a west wilderness. We have already seen that the Amilicites probably fled way past Melek and the other cities in order to reach the wilderness of Hermounts in the northwest part of the land of Zarahemla.

The stronger likelihood is that Melek was near the narrow strip of wilderness that lay between Nephi and Zarahemla of which Alma tells us in the twenty-second chapter. That is, it was in the west (of the Sidon) bordering upon the south wilderness. This appears to be in perfect agreement with all the known facts. Alma and Amulek leave Ammonihah 

CVHANSEN 

Many people came to Alma from the borders round about and were baptized. (Alma 8:5) He preached in 

Melek for a time and then traveled three days to the north and came to Ammonihah. (v. 6)

The manner of his traveling is not known. Hence, it is not possible to estimate the distance which he traveled in those three days, the distance from Melek to Ammonihah.

Ammonihah might be truly called the Sodom of ancient 

America. It was indeed a wicked city. (v. 9)

Much glorious doctrine and useful information come to us from Alma's visit to Ammonihah, but these do not immediately concern us here. The reader should read carefully chapters 9 to 14 of the Book of Alma.

THE SECOND ALMA 

135 In Ammonihah, Alma met a splendid man whose name was Amulek. Together they exhorted the people to repentance, and converted a few, among them an influential lawyer, a man called Zeezrom. The converts were persecuted terribly. Many women and children were burned to death. (Alma 14)

From Ammonihah, Alma and Amulek went to Sidom. 

Sidom was apparently thus near Ammonihah. The direction is not given, nor the distance, but it was probably not west as we shall see shortly.

Following many marvelous experiences in Sidom, Alma and Amulek went to Zarahemla. The mission had taken just a year.

II. A Cry of War. 

Though safely resting at home after his trying labors in the west mission field, Alma was by no means idle. He was shortly called upon to use his magnificent powers in the capacity of prophet and seer. 

On the fifth day of the second month of the eleventh year of the rule of the judges over the people of Nephi, there was heard a sudden cry of war throughout the land. (Alma 16:1) The Lamanites "had come in upon the wilderness side, into the borders of the land, even unto the city of Ammonihah, and began to slay the people and destroy the city." (v. 2)

We could wish for much more detail here. "Upon the wilderness side." This wilderness might have been the south, the narrow strip between the lands of Nephi and Zarahemla, or it might have been the wilderness of Hermounts, or it might have been both and probably was. 

In other words the Lamanites would have had to cross the narrow strip of wilderness to get into the land of Zarahemla at all, unless, indeed, they also occupied at that time the western wilderness as indicated in Alma 22:28. They probably came upon Ammonihah from the west, the southern part of the wilderness of Hermounts if our conclusions are sound. Yet, the wilderness of Hermounts could have extended south some distance and furnished a screen for the enemy most or all of the way from the narrow strip of wilderness. "Into the borders of the land." This would have been

the (west?) border of the land of Zarahemla. But did not 

Zarahemla border on the west sea as well as on the east?

The wilderness of Hermounts must have extended south into the land of Zarahemla. There the Lamanites would have had an opportunity to remain in the forest and come upon Ammonihah unknown to the inhabitants. 

Before the Nephites could gather for resistance, the city was utterly destroyed, in one day. The city of Noah was likewise attacked. Some of the people of Noah were taken captive into the wilderness. (v. 16)

It appears thus that Noah was near Ammonihah. It was also near enough to the wilderness that the Lamanites felt safe in attacking it. It must, therefore, have been north or south of Ammonihah, not east, and certainly not west toward the wilderness. Inasmuch as Sidom, also apparently near Ammonihah, was not attacked, we would naturally suppose that it was somewhat to the east in the general direction of Zarahemla.

The bodies of the dead of Ammonihah were mangled and eaten by the wild beasts. Does this not further suggest that the city was north by a great wilderness, perhaps the wilderness of Hermounts?

Zoram, the leader of the Nephite forces, with his sons, Lehi and Aha, finally got his army together, but he was uncertain how to proceed against the invaders. He, therefore, enquired of Alma, and the prophet outlined for him a plan of action. (Alma 16:5-6) 

Zoram and his sons followed the plan.

They crossed the Sidon (from Zarahemla) and marched beyond Manti into the south wilderness. (v. 7) 

This south wilderness was in all probability the narrow strip of wilderness of which Alma writes as being between 

Nephi and Zarahemla.

There they came upon the armies of the Lamanites. (v. 7)

The Lamanites were evidently on their way back to their homes in Nephi, having destroyed Ammonihah and attacked Noah. Zoram encountered or intercepted them as Alma had said he would.

The Nephites defeated and scattered the enemy. They

THE SECOND ALMA 

137 drove the Lamanites into the wilderness. The captive citizens of Noah were released and returned to their homes. (v. 8) This campaign of the Lamanites into Nephite territory lasted about ten months. (Alma 16:1, 9) It was the one that took place while the sons of Mosiah were still among the Lamanites on their mission while the converted Anti-Nephi-Lehis, or Ammonites, were deciding what course to adopt for their future welfare. (Alma 25:2)

The foregoing facts fit perfectly into the pattern already suggested. There are apparently no contradictions. 

This mention of Manti here raises a very timely and interesting question. It appears to be beyond doubt that 

Manti was south of Zarahemla the city, in the border of the narrow strip of wilderness between the lands of Nephi and Zarahemla. It was near the head of the river Sidon that flowed northward and past the city of Zarahemla on the east.

One wonders in passing what relation this land of Manti had to that mentioned by the Prophet Joseph Smith as having been in what is now Missouri. Is this another example of the transfer of names?

CHAPTER SEVENTEEN THE MORONITE WAR IN THE WEST No other group movement of the BOOK OF MORMON 

is more extensive nor more enlightening than the campaigns directed by General Moroni. Because of the nature and extent of the territory and the movements involved in them, they furnish an excellent basis for the study of Nephite history and geography. While the analysis must of necessity be brief, the purpose of this chapter will be to show the relation of the movements to locations already given and others yet to be considered.

The prolonged war between the Nephites and their hereditary enemies, the Lamanites, covered substantially the whole land of Zarahemla in two sections or fronts, the west and the east. It came about as the result of a conspiracy on the part of a wicked yet engaging man named Amalickiah. He was another of those Nephites of tremendous mental and physical stature who made the Nephite history so bloody and disastrous. The events that led up to the struggle briefly are these. 

Nephihah was the chief judge. (Alma 4:16-17)

Amalickiah, an influential man, desired to become king and destroy the government of the judges. He succeeded in drawing away many people unto him, including many of the lower judges. (Alma 46:1-5)

Moroni, a young Nephite, was commander-in-chief of the armies of the Nephites. (Alma 46:11) Aroused by the treachery of Amalickiah, he raised a standard of freedom, made the first American flag, and called for volunteers to defend it. (Alma 46:12-22) Those who rallied to the call of Moroni took this oath: "We covenant with our God, that we shall be destroyed, even as our brethren in the land northward, if we shall fall into transgression." (v. 22)

A few comments by way of explanation and review are

THE MORONITE WAR IN THE WEST 

139 made at this time. These points should be held clearly in mind. மாமம FB 17 

First American Flag-Standard of Liberty 

The land northward, spoken of in the above covenant, was the land of the Jaredites, Desolation. It is mentioned in connection with the associated lands in Alma 22:30-31. 

It was the land discovered by the men of Limhi who were sent to find Zarahemla. They found it covered with the bones of men and animals. It was there that they also found the twenty-four gold plates bearing the history of the Jaredites. (Mosiah 8:5-11) It was the place where the

Jaredites were destroyed, and because of its desolate and wasted condition it was called Desolation by the Nephites. 

We have seen already that Zarahemla was near Desolation because the latter land was discovered by the people of Mulek. It was there they found Coriantumr, the last of the Jaredites. (Omni 21)

It was at the southern end of Desolation that it was a day-and-a-half's journey for a Nephite from sea to sea. 

Moroni was at Zarahemla, the capital of the Nephites. 

When Amalickiah saw that he could not make himself king, he gathered all he could of his followers and marched southward to join the Lamanites. Fearing the result of this maneuver, Moroni went out and intercepted this band of renegades. In a battle that was fought Moroni was victorious and succeeded in bringing back to Zarahemla most of Amalickiah's men, but the traitor escaped to the Lamanites with a few followers. (Alma 46:29-33)

Through continued wickedness and intrigue he succeeded in making himself king of the Lamanites.

At this point a place Onidah is mentioned as being in all probability in the land of Nephi. We never hear of it again. A hill Onidah is located in Alma's mission to the 

Zoramites. This would doubtless mean that there were two places by that name. Yet, they could have been the same. 

Neither location is very important.

In the twenty-ninth verse of Alma 47 we read that when Amalickiah's henchmen killed the Lamanite king, the king's own servants were frightened and fled to the land of Zarahemla to join the converts from the Lamanite Mission. 

Here again we have evidence of the proximity of the two lands, Nephi and Zarahemla. 

Toward the end of the year 72 B. C. Amalickiah sent a large army from the land of Nephi northward against the 

Nephites in Zarahemla. Moroni had not been idle. The

Lamanites found the Nephite cities fortified. They marched first against Ammonihah and finding it too strong for successful attack, they went on to Noah. (Alma 48-49)

As indicated already the city of Ammonihah must have been west of Zarahemla near the borders of the wilderness into which the Lamanites had pushed. It was easily accessible from the wilderness.

Some years earlier Alma and Amulek, in their mission to Ammonihah, had found the city so wicked that they predicted its complete destruction. (Alma 8:12) Alma 16:3-9 records the fulfillment of this prediction. At that time, as in the case now under discussion, the Lamanites invaded the land and attacked the city of Ammonihah. Then they moved on to Noah. It is interesting to note that though these movements were separated by many years, they agree in every detail in matters of relationships involved. 

When the invading Lamanites found that all the Nephite cities on the west were too strongly fortified to be attacked successfully, they returned to Amalickiah at Nephi, after some costly attempts to storm the fortresses, and reported their failure. Amalickiah was so violently angry that he swore to drink the blood of Moroni. (Alma 49:23-37)

Thus ended the first campaign and the year 72 B. C. The entire invasion had taken less than two months. (49:1, 29)

There followed then a period of six years during which Amalickiah laid plans for his next invasion of Zarahemla. 

He caused that the men should be provided with swords and shields and heavy clothing.

THE MORONITE WAR IN THE WEST 

141 When the war was resumed, it was in the east. 

Amalickiah himself came down to lead the armies. He was a formidable opponent and forced the fighting against Moroni. In the eastern campaign he was killed by Teancum, and his brother Ammoron continued the contest. 

It was Ammoron who returned the fighting to the west. 

He went back to Nephi to report the death of Amalickiah to the queen of the Lamanites. (Alma 52:12) Then he gathered another army-having left the first to maintain the cities taken on the east-and at its head marched to the western cities of the Nephites under the impression that the Nephites were not strong enough to maintain two armies. 

Moroni himself had gone into the west long enough to help fortify the cities there and encourage the men after which he returned to the north and east, leaving the western campaign under the direction of Antipus and Helaman. 

The information we have of the ensuing contest in the west and south comes from a letter written by Helaman from that quarter of the land to Moroni. (Alma 56-58)

He reported that affairs in the west had gone badly with the Nephites owing to the vastly superior numbers of the enemy. They were so sorely pressed that the Ammonites were on the point of breaking their oath never to fight, (Alma 56:7-8) but they were persuaded not to do this.

Instead they sent to the front two thousand of their sons who had been too young to be parties to the covenant. (v. 3) 

More than a year had passed since Moroni had left the west, (Alma 52:15; 56:1) and in the meantime the Lamanites had taken many cities. Among those that had fallen were Manti, Zeezrom, Cumeni, and Antiparah. (56:13-14)

Helaman, with his young Ammonite boys, joined Antipus at Judea. (v. 15)

It is to be doubted that any of these cities was directly on the western coast. There was, however, at least one city on the coast. (56:31) There were some to the north, (v. 22) probably Ammonihah, Noah, Sidom, etc. Alma 

52:12 appears to give the impression that the cities were on the west coast. There were probably some there, but we must not forget that there was a place (wilderness) on the west seashore that sheltered the Lamanites. (Alma 22:29)

Throughout the movements on the west there was

evidence that the western cities were not far from Zarahemla. (56:25, 28) 

Verse 25 is very puzzling. "Neither durst they march down against the city of 

Zarahemla; neither durst they cross the head of Sidon, over to the city of Nephihah.” 

Nephihah was on the east. (51:24) Why, then, was it named as if it were on the west? The only answer seems to be that the cities on both east and west were so near each other that they could have been considered together. There is always the possibility, of course, that there was a pass or other natural feature that rendered movement easy from 

Manti to Nephihah. See also Alma 59:5-6.

After receiving re-enforcements from Zarahemla, Antipus and Helaman planned a clever bit of strategy and took Antiparah. Helaman, with his two thousand young 

Ammonites, marched near but past Antiparah as if they were "going to the city beyond, in the borders by the seashore." (Alma 56:31)

It follows, of course, that Antiparah was at least some distance inland.

According to expectations the Lamanite army followed. 

For three days and nights Helaman fled, and the Lamanites pursued, camping by night in the wilderness. (vs. 36-42)

They were not traveling west all the time. At some point they turned northward. (v. 36) 

On the morning of the third day the Lamanites were not following. Helaman turned back and found them in battle. Antipus was dead, and his men were in confusion. 

Helaman and his young men saved the day, captured many prisoners, and sent them to Zarahemla. (Alma 56:51-54)

Antiparah was taken with little difficulty by Helaman. 

The army in the west received supplies and men from Zarahemla, among them sixty more young Ammonites. The 

Nephites captured a Lamanite supply train. The provisions were sent to Judea and the prisoners, to Zarahemla. On the way they tried to break away and were nearly all killed. 

Their guards returned to the war just in time to help the army retake Cumeni. (Alma 57:6-17)

This was a critical time for Helaman. He received more men, but his force was yet much too small to maintain all

THE MORONITE WAR IN THE WEST 

143 the cities he had retaken and still prosecute the war. 

Helaman wrote to the chief judge at Zarahemla. Months passed, and there was no word and no help. His men were on the verge of starvation. (58:1-7) Still, they recaptured most of their cities.

The Lamanites at that time concentrated on Manti at the headwaters of the Sidon. Helaman determined to attack them. Taking a position "on the wilderness side," he divided his forces. Gid with one division was placed in the wilderness on the right; Teomner with another was placed on the left.

When the confident Lamanites came out to fight, Helaman fled into the wilderness, and the Lamanites followed. During the night he turned back and joined Teomner and Gid who by now had taken Manti. (58:15-29)

Manti was the last Lamanite stronghold in the western part of the Nephite lands. When the Lamanites saw that it was taken from them, they returned to Nephi for another army, and the war in the west had come to an end. (vs. 29-33) Again attention is called to the consistency of the whole pattern. If the book be permitted to tell its own story, there seems to be little need for important differences of opinion.

CHAPTER EIGHTEEN THE ZORAMITE MISSION 

During the seventeenth year of the judges, Korihor, the Anti-Christ, came among the people of Zarahemla and stirred them up to trouble. (Alma 30:6)

His outbreak was probably but an indication of the underlying current of unrest that seems to have been present most of the time among the people. When this uprising was quelled by the trial and death of Korihor, the Zoramites next were the cause of sorrow and trouble to Alma. 

CV-HANSEN 

Korihor the Anti-Christ

The Zoramites, followers of one Zoram, (Alma 30:59) were apostate Nephites who banded together and established themselves in the land of Antionum. 

The land of Antionum was east and south of the land and city of Zarahemla. It extended almost to the east sea, leaving very little room for the eastern cities if they existed at the same time as Antionum. It touched Jershon on the north, the land occupied by the converted Lamanites known as the Ammonites. Its southern boundary was the wilder-

ness line. (Alma 31:3) Siron, the home of the harlot Isabel, was there.

Thus, Antionum was east of Melek and Zarahemla, probably with Minon, Gideon, and Manti between. It was also bounded by Jershon on the north, by the seaboard cities on the east, and by the narrow strip of wilderness on the south. Since Jershon touched the land of Bountiful, (Alma 27:22) it is clear that the two lands of Jershon and

Antionum spanned the entire land of Zarahemla north and south.

Alma took with him his former companions, Amulek and Zeezrom, the converts from Ammonihah; Ammon, Aaron, and Omner, three of the sons of Mosiah and missionaries to the Lamanites, and two of his three sons, Shiblon and Corianton, and set out to preach to the Zoramites. (Alma 31:6-7)

Corianton deserted his mission in his wild infatuation for the harlot Isabel in the land of Siron. (Alma 39:3) 

At any point in the BOOK OF MORMON an apparently simple statement can introduce difficulty and confusion. 

All this is proof of the everpresent necessity for remembering that its geography is not easily explained and that we should proceed with caution. 

Here is a case in point. Corianton went "over into the land of Siron, among the borders of the Lamanites." Was 

Siron a Nephite land and city (if there was a city) on the southern border of Antionum in the land of Zarahemla? 

Or was it a Lamanite land on the northern border of the land of Nephi? Who can say?

A hill Onidah is mentioned in connection with this mission. (Alma 32:4) One finds it difficult to connect it with the place Onidah named in connection with the deviltries of Amalickiah unless both refer to a place in or near the narrow strip of wilderness to the south of Zarahemla and the north of Nephi. (Alma 47:5) Nor is there any particular reason for connecting them. One could have been in 

Zarahemla and the other, in Nephi.

It is in connection with the Zoramites that we learn of the rameumptom, that strange tower used for a stranger worship.

When the missionaries had sought out all the Zoramites who would hear them, they departed out of Antionum and

THE ZORAMITE MISSION 

147 went across the line into Jershon. (Alma 35:1-2) The converted Zoramites were so unpopular with their fellows that they too took the liberty of joining the Ammonites in 

Jershon. (v. 6). JONA 

AV ANN Rumeumptom 

The leader of the Zoramites, a very wicked man, was enraged by this desertion on the part of his subjects and sent to the Ammonites asking them to refuse admittance to the Zoramites who had appealed to them. (v. 28) 

The hospitable Ammonites, of course, turned deaf ears to this proposal, whereupon the Zoramites swore out threats against them. (vs. 9-10) To make good their threats and complete their apostasy they got into touch with the Lamanites who filled the wilderness to the south and made a league with them. (v. 11)

When this development took place, the Ammonites, who, of course, years before had sworn never again to take up weapons of war against anyone, became exceedingly fearful. Their enemies had free and full access to their southern border which rendered the position of Jershon extremely critical.

Therefore, the Ammonites, or Anti-Nephi-Lehis, did a very wise thing-they departed out of the land of Jershon and went to Melek. (v. 13) This made a place for the 

Nephite armies to prepare for the invasion, and the war began. (v. 13)

The missionaries having done all they could returned to Zarahemla. (v. 14)

Let us turn for a moment for a glance at some additional points of geography.

We have already seen that Melek almost certainly touched the narrow strip of wilderness on the south. There would, therefore, be, lying between it and Antionum, beginning at the south, Manti, perhaps all of Minon, and possibly part of Gideon. The river Sidon would have been between as well.

These views offer much food for speculation, but there is a dearth of definite facts. Why should the people of 

Jershon, the Ammonites, have gone to Melek instead of to 

Zarahemla? There was perhaps good reason, but there is no answering this question now. There may have been some feature of the country that made it hazardous or difficult to cross over to Zarahemla. There may have been a particular bond between the peoples of Melek and of Jershon. Or might it have been a result of the fact that 

Melek was nearer to Jershon?

Here is another point of interest. How many people were there in Jershon? How many could so quickly and easily have moved to a place of safety? Would there have been more than five or ten thousand? These people later sent 2,060 young men to the war with Helaman. If they were all there were of fighting age who had been too young to take the oath of peace, does this number offer a hint as to the number of the total population? When Johnston's army entered Salt Lake City, the population was slightly less than twenty thousand. They quickly and effectively evacuated their homes. Many reached Provo, fifty miles away.

The authors add here a comment that shows the unity of the record.

Many people have wondered what became of Corianton, the youngest son of Alma. Did he ever repent and return to the work of the mission? 

In Alma 35:15 we read that Corianton was with the other missionaries when they returned to Zarahemla after the close of their labors. He is numbered with those who brought many Zoramites to repentance. He went home apparently in good standing. 

Corianton seems to have remained in good standing.

Years after the mission he went northward with a party

THE ZORAMITE MISSION 

149 of emigrants and was not heard of again. (Alma 63:10)

This apparently insignificant fact gives rise to two important observations. The first is that many people, like the Ten Tribes in the Bible, passed entirely out of the record and became lost to the history to begin in another place a new history. 

Missions to Zoramtes

CV.HANSEN 

The second fact that merits mention is that many details, apparently unworthy a passing notice, appear from time to time, but almost never are they isolated or apart from the oneness of the history.

CHAPTER NINETEEN  

The people enjoyed a period of four years of comparative peace after the Lamanites' unsuccessful invasion against Ammonihah and Noah in the west. The days of peace were brought to a sudden end, however, not by the enemies from the south, but by civil strife within. 

In the year 68 B. C. the people of two cities, Morianton and Lehi, had a disagreement about boundary lines. (Alma 

50:25) The upshot of the matter was that the people of Lehi fled to the camp of Moroni for protection, and the citizens of Morianton began a concerted movement toward the north with the purpose of taking possession of the land which was covered with large bodies of water. (Alma 50:29)

Whether this means the land of Desolation is not altogether sure. They probably had in mind striking out into new territory inasmuch as the Nephites were already settled in the land of the Jaredites, or at least part of it. 

Morianton, the leader of the Moriantonites, was a man of violent temper, and in a fit of anger he beat one of his maid-servants. (v. 30) She fled to the camp of Moroni and informed him of the intention of the Moriantonites to go into the land northward. (v. 31) 

An interesting question arises here.

How big were the populations of the cities of Lehi and 

Morianton? Could many thousands or hundreds of thousands have picked up so hurriedly and moved as the people of Lehi and Morianton did? Does it not seem much more likely that there were but a few thousand at most in those places?

Moroni was determined that Morianton should not lead his followers into a land north to become enemies of his people. Had they done so, there would have been enemies

on the north and south, and the Nephites would have been constantly in a dangerous position. Moroni struck with his characteristic force and promptness.

He sent an army to head off the rebellious Moriantonites. The army did head them off, at the narrow pass that led into the land of Desolation, and turned back such as would give up the wild design. Morianton and the others were killed by Teancum and his men. (vs. 34-35)

A year later, in 67 B. C., another rebellion broke out. 

This was much more grievous than the one under Morianton. 

Certain men among the Nephites were desirous of making some changes in the law. The chief judge, Pahoran, would not consent to the changes, and the discontented group tried to have him taken from the judgment seat in order that a king might be chosen. (Alma 51:2-5) election was called.

An It was at that critical time that the Lamanites chose to come again to war. Amalickiah himself led a great army into the borders of the land. (vs. 9-12)

In the election in Zarahemla the king-men were defeated, and they took the invasion of the Lamanites as an opportunity of evening matters. They refused to go to war against the enemy. (v. 16) Thereupon Moroni asked

Pahoran for permission to force the rebels into subjection. 

The authority was given, and Moroni marched his army against the king-men. A battle ensued in which thousands were killed before the government was once again established. (vs. 74-21)

In the meantime Amalickiah had come into the land with his Lamanite army and captured the city of Moroni. 

This was a very natural move. Moroni was in the extreme southeast corner of the land of Zarahemla nearest the narrow strip of wilderness and by the east sea. (Alma 50:13) 

Following the capture of the city of Moroni, Amalickiah pushed along the eastern coast, taking all the cities as he came to them, until Moroni, Nephihah, Lehi, Morianton, Omner, Gid, and Mulek had fallen into his hands. In each case he left enough men to hold the place and marched on. (Alma 51:23-27) The Lamanites continued their ruthless conquest to the northward with the firm intention of taking Bountiful, but they were met by Teancum, the man who had

intercepted the Moriantonites, and they too were headed off, and in the same place. Lamanites and Nephites fought a determined fight, but when night came, the Nephites still held the strategic city of Bountiful and the narrow pass. 

The surviving Lamanites pitched their camp on the seashore to the south of Bountiful. (vs. 30:31)

The geographical set-up appears perfectly clear. The following cities were on the eastern coast. 

1. Moroni.

Moroni was in the southeast corner of the land of Zarahemla near the narrow strip of wilderness that separated the lands of Nephi and Zarahemla. (Alma 50:13; 51:22-24; 

62:32, 34) In later years, at the time of the crucifixion,

Moroni was sunk into the sea and destroyed. (III Nephi 

8:9; 9:4) 

2. Nephihah.

Nephihah was just north of Moroni but somewhat inland from the sea. (Alma 50:14; 51:24-25; 62:21)

This last citation above indicates that Nephihah was some distance from the seashore though it could not have been far. This may have been one of the reasons for its being mentioned as associated with Manti in the south and west. 

3. Aaron.

The city of Aaron presents something of a problem. 

Alma, in 82 B. C., was preaching in Ammonihah, a city presumably away off in the opposite corner of the land from Aaron, when, despairing of accomplishing anything in that wicked place, he departed and took his journey toward 

Aaron. (Alma 8:13)

The problem is obvious at once. It is this: Does Alma's going from Ammonihah to Aaron suggest that these cities were near each other? There might, of course, have been some peculiarity in the land that rendered access from Ammonihah to Aaron easy even though they were widely separated.

The difficulty is not great, however. There might even have been two cities called Aaron. The stronger likelihood is that the prophet had a definite object in mind in crossing from one section of the nation to the other. If, as is sug-

gested here, the distance at most could not have been far, this view becomes more reasonable. 

4. Lehi.

Lehi was in the north by the borders of the seashore. (Alma 50:15, 25; 51:26) The city with which it seems to have been most nearly associated was Morianton. (50:25; 

51:1, 26) It was also near Nephihah.

Lehi was probably next north of Nephihah on the seashore with Morianton in turn next to it. The idea that 

Morianton was north of Lehi arises from two considerations. 

The first is the order in which the eastern cities were taken by Amalickiah. Beginning at the south, with Moroni, the invader captured Lehi, Morianton, Omner, Gid, and Mulek. 

This is in keeping with other citations. 

The second reason for believing that Morianton was north of Lehi is the fact that in the dispute between the citizens of both places, the Moriantonites started northward whereas the people of Lehi appealed to Moroni who was inland.

There is one other very interesting and important matter in connection with Lehi that will be discussed in chapter 21.

Still another comment is in order. As indicated above, Lehi was in the north. But it was also in the east. How shall we understand this? Is not the most satisfactory explanation to be found in the thought that while the city was on the eastern coast it was situated at a point at which the coast line turned to the west? This would place Lehi on the promontory to the north of other cities and much of the land of Zarahemla. 

5. Morianton.

Morianton was one of the east coast cities of the 

Nephites. (Alma 60:5)

As seen heretofore, Morianton and Lehi had a common boundary. 

6. Omner.

Alma 51:26 is the only place in which Omner is mentioned.

It seems to have been between Morianton and Gid. It

is enumerated in that order, and information now to be given shows that it was south of Gid. It might well have been one of the other cities to which reference is made in 

Alma 50:15. 

7. Gid.

In the invasion of Amalickiah, Gid fell to the Lamanites. 

(Alma 51:26) 

It is named as though it was north of Omner and south of Mulek. This appears to have been the correct position although there is one citation that places it north of Mulek, or that can be interpreted so to place it. Bountiful. Jershon Mulek Gid.

In the account of the ministry of the two brothers, Lehi and Nephi, (Helaman 5:14-16) we read that they went from

Bountiful to Gid and from Gid to Mulek. This would seem to give the impression that Gid was between Mulek and Bountiful. This need not, however, have been the case. The missionaries could have missed Mulek in favor of Gid. There is other evidence, and it will now be given. 

8. Mulek.

Mulek was one of the most important of the seacoast cities. That it was built in the days of Mulek is by no means certain though there is a strong probability that such was the case. It could have been the landing place of the 

Mulekites as it seems to have been in about the right location to agree with Alma's (or Mormon's) arrangement as given in Alma 22.

Mulek and Bountiful were closely associated. (Alma 

52:2, 17, 20, 27-28) The plains between the two cities were the site of one of the battles of the war in the east. (Alma 52:20)

This seems effectively to preclude the possibility that Gid could have been between Mulek and Bountiful. 9. Bountiful.

It is now clear that Bountiful was on the eastern seashore in the land of Bountiful. It was one of the northernmost of the Nephite cities in the days of Moroni and because of its location in or at the narrow neck of land was perhaps the most important.

It is also clear that the city of Bountiful was near the east sea and north of Mulek. This appears to be in full agreement with all the requirements of the text. 

For the sake of completeness we shall continue briefly with the story of the eastern campaign. 

During the night, while the Lamanites were yet camped on the seashore near Bountiful, Teancum and his servant stole into their camp and killed Amalickiah. (Alma 51:34)

When the Lamanites discovered that Amalickiah was dead, they abandoned their design of marching against Bountiful. They retreated into Mulek and sought protection in their fortifications. (52:2)

Ammoron, the brother of Amalickiah, was appointed king of the Lamanites. (v. 3) He determined to maintain all the cities the Lamanites had taken. In the meantime 

Moroni sent word to Teancum to keep all Lamanite prisoners who fell into his hands and also to fortify the narrow pass that led into the land northward. (vs. 8-9) 

The situation is perfectly clear in the main. Teancum was located at Bountiful, the city. The land of Bountiful, as we have repeatedly seen, was in the narrow part of the land where it was so short a distance from sea to sea. That would have been a good place to fortify because it was narrow. Furthermore, it appears that there was some natural feature of the land, a valley, a mountain or lake, that lent itself to fortification. Had the Lamanites succeeded in getting past Bountiful to Desolation, they would have completely surrounded the Nephites and would have had them at their mercy.

Ammoron returned to Nephi to report the death of Amalickiah to the Lamanite queen. He had left in Zarahemla most of his army. (v. 12). In Nephi he gathered another army and returned to the war, but in the west, hoping to weaken the Nephite forces by division. (v. 13) 

In the twenty-seventh year of the judges, B. C. 65, Moroni sent orders to Teancum to attack Mulek and take it.

Mulek was too strong, and Teancum returned to Bountiful to await the coming of Moroni who was on his way back from the west where he had been fortifying some cities. (vs. 15-18) 

When Moroni arrived on the east front, the officers held a consultation as to the best way to recapture Mulek. (v. 19) They sent a challenge to Jacob, commander of the

Lamanites in the east, to come out into the plains between 

Mulek and Bountiful to battle. (v. 20) Jacob refused.

Then the Nephites worked a fine bit of strategy. Moroni sent Teancum with a small force past Mulek toward the seashore on the east. At night Moroni marched another division into the wilderness west of Mulek. (v. 22) When the Lamanites saw Teancum's small army, they supposed it to be the entire force of the defenders, and they rushed out to take it. Moroni immediately entered the city with his men and took possession. (vs. 23-24) 

When Teancum and his men saw the Lamanites after them, they fled northward with the enemy in pursuit.

Moroni, leaving enough to maintain the city of Mulek, set out in pursuit of the pursuers.

The Lamanites in the meantime had approached Bountiful only to be met by Lehi with a division of fresh soldiers. 

They turned and fled toward Mulek and came face to face with Moroni and his army. In the battle that followed, the 

Lamanites were routed. Many were killed, and still more either gave up their weapons in token of peace or were marched to Bountiful as prisoners. (vs. 26-40)

The only indication of distance here is the statement (v. 31) that the Lamanites had had a long march, from Mulek to Bountiful and back to Mulek. What was meant by a long march it is hard to determine. The text reads, however, as if the whole affair occurred in one day.

The Lamanite prisoners were compelled to bury the dead of both armies, then they were put to work digging a ditch around the land or city of Bountiful. (Alma 53:3)

This is interesting. The land Bountiful around which the prisoners were digging the ditch must have been the small land around the city, for no matter how small the whole land was, it is hardly possible that they could have dug a ditch around it. Does not this suggest very strongly that even Bountiful, important as it was, was a comparatively small place as ancient Jericho has been proved to have been around which the armies of Israel marched? 

Should we not modify our traditional views of nations and cities of vast and limitless proportions?

At this point occurs a statement that is very difficult to explain. "And it came to pass that Moroni had thus gained a victory over one of the greatest of the armies of the Lamanites, and had obtained possession over the city of Mulek, which was one of the strongest holds of the Lamanites in the land of Nephi." (Alma 53:6) 

What is meant here by the land of Nephi? There is only one answer that does not altogether confuse the whole situation and prove utterly contradictory to other citations. 

Does it not mean "the land of the Nephites?" This would be no mere flourish. The Nephites were the people of 

Nephi; why should not the land be called the land of Nephi?

Surely there is no intention of confusing it with the land to the south, the land of the Lamanites. It is the only citation that raises a definite conflict. If Joseph Smith had written the book, he would have avoided such discrepancies. 

For two years there was an armistice in the east while Ammoron prosecuted the war in the west. In 63 B. C. he proposed to Moroni that they exchange prisoners. Moroni did not like the tone of Ammoron's proposal, and he wrote the Lamanite leader a letter. There followed other communications. The result of the correspondence was that no prisoners were exchanged. (Alma 54; 55:1-3) Instead, Moroni won them back. (55:4-17) It was in Gid that this happened. 

Is this not as we should expect? Was not Gid the next city south of Mulek?

It was at that time Moroni received the letter from Helaman telling of the progress of the war in the west. (Alma 56-58) Helaman was in need of men and supplies and had asked Moroni to procure them for him, since Governor Pahoran had failed to do so. Moroni himself

wrote to Pahoran two exceedingly warm and forceful letters, reproaching him for his laxness when the cause of national safety was at stake. (Alma 59:1-4; 60) Pahoran answered after a long time to say that he had been busy preserving his life and government. The king-men had risen again and driven him from Zarahemla. (Alma 61) 

Pahoran had fled south to Gideon while the leader of the king-men had made himself king in Zarahemla and had already made a league with Ammoron. (Alma 61:5-8)

Pahoran pleaded with Moroni to go immediately to his assistance. Moroni complied. He gathered what men he could and still leave some in the east and marched to Pahoran's aid. Together they were strong enough to overthrow the temporary kingdom of Pachus and destroy the rebellion. (Alma 62:1-11) 

Meanwhile other important developments had been taking place.

The city Nephihah was attacked by the Lamanites. 

Nephihah at that time was inhabited by a mixed citizenry from Nephihah, Lehi, Moroni, and Morianton. They were attacked by a Lamanite army augmented by refugees from 

Manti. (Alma 59:5-7) 

We have already observed that Moroni, Nephihah, Lehi, and Morianton were very near each other in the east, and attention has already been called to the apparent proximity between Manti, in the south, last stronghold of the west campaign, and Nephihah, in the east. That they were easily accessible to each other is beyond question. Does this suggest a small and compact land?

In the siege mentioned above, the Lamanites succeeded in taking Nephihah, and it appears that they held it for at least a year, during all the time that Moroni was engaged with the uprising of the king-men at least. When, however, that trouble was settled, Moroni determined to retake 

Nephihah. (Alma 62:14) On the way from Zarahemla to

Nephihah, Moroni captured a large number of Lamanites with supplies. All but four thousand were killed, and the survivors were sent to live with the Ammonites in Jershon. (v. 17)

Moroni camped his army in the plains of Nephihah and desired the enemy to come out to battle, but the Lamanites were too well acquainted with the courage and wisdom of Moroni and his men and held fast to their defenses. True

to form, Moroni employed a device. He climbed to the top of the wall to spy out the land, and discovered that the Lamanite force was camped in the east part of the city by the entrance. He immediately gave orders for his men to let themselves down from the walls by ropes in the west part, away from the enemy. When morning came, the 

Nephites were inside the city, and the Lamanites awoke in dismay and fled through the convenient entrance. 20-27) (vs. 

After the successful attack on Nephihah, Moroni marched against the city of Lehi. When the Lamanites saw him coming, they fled from the city and started northward to avoid Moroni. Here they met Lehi and Teancum in the north and turned back. This time they fled south until they came to the seashore near the city of Moroni. All the 

Lamanites in the land of Zarahemla were thus gathered in one place, and Ammoron was at their head. (vs. 31-33) 

General Moroni CV-HANSEN 

The combined forces of Moroni, Teancum, and Lehi now surrounded the Lamanites in the borders around Moroni, the last bulwark of the enemy in Zarahemla. No attempt was made to attack that night. While waiting for morning the Nephites camped. During the night Teancum stole into the city and killed Ammoron as he had before killed his brother Amalickiah, but Teancum lost his life this time. (v. 36)

Feeling the loss of Teancum keenly, the Nephites next day fought with unusual courage and determination, and the Lamanites were easily overpowered. Those who were not killed were glad to depart out of the land into Nephi.

(v. 38) 

Thus ended the Moronite wars. As Manti, the last outpost in the west and south, had fallen before the determination of the Nephites, so Moroni, the last in the east, also fell. 

The Lamanites, now fully defeated on both fronts, fled precipitately to their own lands, and the war was over. 

A whole book could be written, and doubtless some day will be, on the unity of the BOOK OF MORMON. In the material of the record covered by the three chapters immediately foregoing there is much evidence of it. All movements are altogether natural and consistent. They are not, however, continuous. That is, one movement, or series of movements, is started only to give way to another, but the first is invariably completed. The completion may in the book be out of its natural order, but in the chronology each event finds its proper place.

This matter of temporarily incompleted developments merits a brief discussion as evidence of the very obvious fact that no one man wrote the BOOK OF MORMON.

The words of Moroni, who lived four hundred years A. 

D., appear in the history of the Jaredites that covered the period 2,200 B. C. to 600 B. C. (Ether 1:1-5; 2:7-12; 5:16; 8:2026; 12:6-41) The words of Mormon, who lived nearly to 400

A. D., are found between the books of Omni and Mosiah, about 130 B. C. Ammon, in Nephi, heard from King Limhi, the story of the forty-three scouts and the discovery of Desolation. Here is an unexpected development-a story within a story. (Mosiah 8) The beginning of that story, that goes back to about 200 B. C., is found in Mosiah 8, the date for which is 121 B. C. Suddenly, in the midst of that absorbing narrative it is cut short that another might begin, this, the lovely tale of Alma. (Mosiah 23-24) In Mosiah 

20:1-5 a new element is abruptly injected into the story with the stealing by the priests of Noah of twenty-four Lamanite girls. Apparently the incident is closed. It is almost certainly forgotten when all at once it is brought again to the front (Mosiah 23:30-34) to constitute the tie by which three scattered elements of the original history are brought together, the stories of Zeniff, Alma, and the priests. (Mosiah 22-24) Here is another. We find that when Amalickiah had the Lamanite king put to death (Alma 47:22-29) and laid the blame upon the king's servants, the servants fled to the

land of Jershon and joined the Ammonites.

During the war in the east, Moroni had a pressing need of a friendly Lamanite to aid in his capture of Gid. He found one, and who is not surprised and pleased to learn that he was one of these greatly-wronged servants? (Alma 55) In keeping with the authors' desires and expressed purpose of remaining unbiased in their judgments, they here call attention to the great lapses of time that occurred during the wars under Moroni. For example, months were required to receive an answer from Pahoran. Was it the result of the fact that a great territory was to be covered, or was it because of other conditions? These are important questions.

There are numerous such incidents, but nowhere is there any miscalculation. An interesting detail occurs in 

Mosiah 22:15-16 in which we are told that an army of Lamanite warriors became hopelessly lost in the country in which the men were born and had lived all their lives! 

Surely any man with sufficient intelligence to write the least chapter in the book would avoid such a blunder. And yet, strangely enough, that incident fits perfectly the whole tenor of the record!

Again, it is interesting to speculate as to the size of that army of Moroni's that descended from the wall of Nephihah while the Lamanites slept. How many soldiers could thus practicably and silently have acted? Would it have been many thousands? This, however, was but a division of the entire army.

CHAPTER TWENTY A BRIDGE LESSON 

We must now account for the period from 52 B. C. to 

23 B. C., to give the approximate dates. Many apparently unrelated events were crowded into this short space of time. 

A number of points are brought out in their unfolding. 

I. The First War Under Moronihah. 

Moronihah, son of the wonderful Moroni, was appointed by his father to be leader of the armies. (Alma 62:43)

He was not immediately called upon to give evidence of his ability. Five or six years passed before serious trouble threatened.

Chief Judge Pahoran died, and three of his sons contended for the judgment seat. (Helaman 1:3) The matter was temporarily adjusted, but not peaceably. Secret combinations arose that introduced a reign of terror. Murders were of frequent occurrence, and much trouble followed. (1:8-13) 

It was, as usual, at a critical time that the Lamanites chose to attack. (1:14-17) The leader, a man named Coriantumr, took the Nephites so completely by surprise that he easily captured Zarahemla. (1:22) Striking fiercely,

Coriantumr marched from place to place toward Bountiful with the intention of taking the strategic city by the narrow pass and gaining possession of the land northward. (1:23)

He naturally supposed that the strongest place would be in the center of the nation. (v. 24)

Moronihah, on the other hand, had supposed that the enemy would attack the outside, and he had carefully fortified the outlying cities, leaving Zarahemla in a relatively defenseless condition. (v. 26)

When General Moronihah saw the Lamanites marching boldly toward Bountiful, he sent Lehi to check them. (v. 28) 

Lehi did check and turn them back. To their great dismay they came face to face with Moronihah and his army. Com-

A BRIDGE LESSON 

163 pletely surrounded, the Lamanites fought doggedly, but they were overwhelmed. (vs. 31:32) The Nephites easily took Zarahemla again.

In these movements the nearness of Zarahemla to Bountiful is made apparent, and the importance of Bountiful, owing to its position at the narrow pass into the land northward, is unmistakable. It appears that he who held the narrow pass had control of the northward lands. 

One can hardly fail to note the suddenness with which the Lamanites could come into the heart of the Nephite country. Surely the lands could not have been large! 

II. Two Unholy Men. 

The authors here call attention to two men whose stature in wickedness was enormous and whose influence upon the history of the Nephites cannot be overestimated. 

Seldom has it been the lot of a given people to be afflicted at once with two such terribly bad characters. Gadianton was the hand, and Kishkumen was the tool. They were together a formidable combination. They planned and carried out their murders with all the audacity and finesse of modern gangsters. (Helaman 2)

They are important to us in this study in that they and their kind were almost constantly, except for two periods, harassing the people. In no small degree did they influence the numbers of the Nephites by their murders and wars and the many who deserted from the Nephites to join their hereditary enemies. The path of the Nephites was not a rosy one. One might easily over-estimate their population. 

III. Northward Migrations Again. 

Here is some information that many will find of very great interest. "And it came to pass in the forty and sixth (year), yea, there was much contention and many dissensions; in the which there were an exceeding great many who departed out of the land of Zarahemla, and went forth unto the land northward to inherit the land. (Helaman 3:3) "And they did travel to an exceeding great distance, insomuch that they came to large bodies of water and many rivers." (v. 4)

That there were many who left the lands of the Nephites' inheritance is perfectly clear. On many occasions

groups moved away from the center of their civilization. 

In the instance given above the people went north, possibly beyond even Desolation, the northernmost land of the Nephites. "Yea, and even they did spread forth into all parts of the land, into whatever parts it had not been rendered desolate and without timber, because of the many inhabitants who had before inherited the land." (v. 5)

It is clear that they went at least beyond Desolation because they occupied the land that had not been rendered desolate by the Jaredites. How much farther they went is not told.

Does this mean that the entire Nephite civilization migrated northward, leaving the land of its earlier years?

This is obviously not the case. The story of the main body of the people continues with the people still in their own country. The foregoing simply means that some moved away and left the majority of the people where they had always been.

The description of that Jaredite land is so good that the authors quote liberally from it here. "And now no part of the land was desolate, save it were for timber; but because of the greatness of the destruction of the people who had before inhabited the land it was called desolate." (v. 6) 

Is it any wonder that the Nephites called it Desolation? "And there being but little timber upon the face of the land, nevertheless the people who went forth became exceeding expert in the working of cement; therefore they did build houses of cement, in which they did dwell." (v. 7) 

This is a remarkable statement! Where further need one look for proof of the authenticity of the BOOK OF 

MORMON? How could Joseph Smith know of the existence of cement houses in this hemisphere when the scholars of his day were openly contemptuous and skeptical about it? 

President Heber J. Grant's fervent testimony regarding the BOOK OF MORMON is very much to the point in this connection. Upon a number of occasions in his early years he heard intelligent and scholarly men ridicule or make light of certain claims of the divine record. Many called absurd some of the statements contained therein, but Presi-

A BRIDGE LESSON 

165 dent Grant has lived to see the complete and convincing vindication of the record. He bears witness that those things the scholarly men said were foolish or impossible have been unquestionably demonstrated to be true through scientific research and the revelations of time. Among these developments has been the descovery of great cities of cement. Mexico 

Gulf of Mexico 

Sea East 

Note the many directions of Seas in these countries

Pacific Ocean South
