Sea North 

South America "And it came to pass that they did multiply and spread, and did go forth from the land southward to the land northward, and did spread insomuch that they did cover the face of the whole earth, from the sea south to the sea north, from the sea west to the sea east." (v. 8)

How much territory did they cover? This is an important question. They covered "the face of the whole earth." That does indeed seem to justify the vast expanse of the territory demanded by many students, but "from the sea south to the sea north, from the sea west to the sea east" suddenly limits it again, unless, to be sure, we interpret it to mean from the Atlantic to the Pacific and from the Great Lakes to the Gulf of Mexico. This matter is discussed fully in chapter 26.

The chart given herewith shows how admirably the set-up of the four seas fits into the geography of Central America. Does it not appear that verse by verse, point by point, step by step, the record gives proof of a consistent pattern for the peoples of ancient America? 

Again one is led to marvel that Joseph Smith, an unschooled lad of twenty-four, more than one hundred years ago, could have known so much about the land he had

never seen. How could he know about the four seas and the countless other details that have come to light in the 

BOOK OF MORMON? 

As the authors have tried to make clear long before this, this book is in no sense a presentation of external evidences of the BOOK OF MORMON. Indeed, this phase of the matter has been almost studiously avoided lest in some way some finding might unduly influence the interpretation of the record. Yet, the writers feel justified in calling attention to the fact that the cement houses have been found only in Central America and Mexico. Numerous authoritative and scholarly works have been devoted to this matter of cement houses that tend to confirm the locations established in this book. 

IV. Moronihah Again to the Rescue. 

The reader of the BOOK OF MORMON is faced con tinuously with the spectacle of countless numbers of Lamanites marching upon the less-numerous Nephites. In whatever figure we number the Lamanites, we must agree that they were for the most part vastly more numerous than the Nephites. At first glance this is difficult to explain, but a second thought shows it to be very easily understood. 

Only on two or three occasions did any considerable number of Lamanites join the Nephites, but we read from beginning to end of dissenters going over from the Nephites to the Lamanites. Whenever any man or group of men became dissatisfied with things in Zarahemla, it was but the beginning of a movement that eventuated in an increase in the numbers of the Lamanites, for almost invariably the discontented Nephites went over to the enemies. 

It is not at all surprising, therefore, that we read that a group of dissenters persuaded the Lamanites (who seldom needed much persuasion) to go against the Nephites. As a matter of fact this was one instance in which the Lamanites did need persuasion. (Helaman 4:3)

In the fifty-sixth year of the reign of the judges a combined force of Lamanites and deserters did go to war against the people of Zarahemla. (4:5) They came in such numbers that they overwhelmed the Nephites and drove them out of their own land, Zarahemla, (v. 5) forcing them to take refuge northward in Bountiful.

At Bountiful the Nephites, under the direction of

A BRIDGE LESSON 

167 Moronihah, fortified the narrow pass where it was a day's journey for a Nephite from sea to sea. (v. 7) They later regained one half their possessions. (4:16)

V. Two Holy Men. 

If there were many wicked men among the Nephites, there were also many noble ones. For every Laman there was a Nephi; for every Korihor, an Alma; for every Gadianton, a Helaman.

Helaman, the son of Helaman, who was the son of Alma, who was the son of Alma, was a righteous and just man and a successful chief judge. (Helaman 3:20) He was the father of two splendid men who will next claim our attention. (v. 21)

Nephi, the elder, was made chief judge to succeed his father Helaman. (Helaman 3:37) Lehi was with him a preacher of righteousness. (4:14) When war and misery took hold of the people to such an extent that they were threatened with destruction, Nephi yielded up the judgment seat to Cezoram that he, like Alma, might devote his entire time to the work of the church. (5:1, 4) Taking his brother with him, he set out on a mission to his own people. (v. 14) 

Beginning at Bountiful, they traveled to Gid and Mulek. (vs. 14-15) They preached to all the Nephites who were in the land southward, and then they went into Zarahemla among the Lamanites.

Let us pause to see where we are. 

It will be remembered that when the Lamanites and their too-earnest allies, the Nephites dissenters, came down to battle, they succeeded in capturing all the Nephite territory, including Zarahemla, the city, (Helaman 4:5) clear to Bountiful (v. 5) where the Nephites took advantage of a natural feature of the country to defend themselves. At that time the invaders held all the country south of Bountiful, at the narrow pass, and the defenders held Bountiful and Desolation.

Later, however, Moronihah succeeded in retaking onehalf his people's lands. (4:16) Of course, he would have taken that portion to the north, nearest his own position. 

This gave him possession of the northern half of Zarahemla, meaning Zarahemla in the large sense, the homeland of the Nephites.

This would place Zarahemla, the city, near the middle of the land of Zarahemla and agrees perfectly with Moroni's statement that it was in the "heart of the country." (Alma 60:19)

This whole scheme fits admirably into the set-up of the territory covered by Nephi and Lehi in their mission. 

They started preaching at Bountiful, the headquarters during the time Zarahemla was in the hands of the enemy; they went from there to Gid or Mulek (see the explanation about Gid and Mulek in chapter 19), thence to the other northern towns and cities of the land of Zarahemla. At that time they were among the "people of Nephi who were in the land southward." (v. 16) That is, they were among their own people, their own towns and cities south of the narrow pass, (hence, the land southward) that "one-half" of the Nephite possessions retaken by Moronihah. Then they went into Zarahemla into the midst of the Lamanites themselves. (v. 16)

That marked the end of the war. The dissenters repented, and the Lamanites gave ear to the missionaries. (v. 17) "And it came to pass that Nephi and Lehi did proceed from thence to go to the land of Nephi." (v. 20) 

Does this sound as if they made a long journey?

Here we have them taking the same route so frequently traveled after the discovery of Zarahemla, up to the land of the Lamanites to continue the work they had so bravely begun among the Lamanite invaders of Zarahemla.

CHAPTER TWENTY-ONE LANDS NORTH AND SOUTH 

We come now to a very interesting and somewhat puzzling set of circumstances. Surely no mortal man, of whatever degree of intelligence, could write so many details, so many important and so many apparently insignificant details, so much of crossing and recrossing of trails, so many stories within a story, so many definite dates, so many references to the Bible and other information, and all in so short a time, without tripping himself many times. 

Yet each point found in the record, when understood, adds to the totality of the history. It could not be the work of 

Joseph Smith, the man. 

No single statement should be read independently of the rest. No private interpretations will add to the understanding of the story. Only as a unit will the record tell a complete, consistent, and convincing tale. 

Of fundamental importance to the whole record is the relation of lands north to lands south. Is this relationship maintained throughout?

We have already seen the extreme probability that whenever reference is made to the land northward, it means the land north of the narrow neck of land, and when reference is made to the land southward, it means that south of the narrow neck, Bountiful, or Zarahemla, or Bountiful and Zarahemla, respectively. This is true, however, only when no definite point of location is given. Of course, Zarahemla would be northward from Nephi, and Nephi would be southward from Zarahemla. 

Let us examine a few such passages. "Therefore, Morianton put it into their hearts that they should flee to the land which was northward, which was covered with large bodies of water, and take possession of the land which was northward." (Alma 50:29)

To what land did Morianton intend going? What does the word "northward" mean here? The answer is not far to seek. Moroni, it will be recalled, sent an army to stop the flight of the Moriantonites lest they should become established on the north and become a perpetual menace to the safety of the people in the homelands. "And it came to pass that they did not head them until they had come to the borders of the land of Desolation; and there they did head them, by the narrow pass which led by the sea into the land northward, yea, by the sea, on the west and on the east." (Alma 50:34)

The authors have admitted elsewhere in this book that they have no unimpeachable explanation of this narrow pass by the sea west and the sea east. That it has reference to the narrow neck of land, or of some aspect of it, there can be no question. It was on the "border" of Desolation that it was a day-and-a-half's journey for a Nephite from sea to sea. "And now, it was only the distance of a day and a half's journey for a Nephite, on the line Bountiful and the land 

Desolation, from the east to the west sea." (Alma 22:32)

This was unquestionably the narrow neck of land. "And they (the Jaredites) built a great city by the narrow neck of land, by the place where the sea divides the land. And they did preserve the land southward for a wilderness to get game. And the whole face of the land northward was covered with inhabitants." (Ether 10:20-21)

This was, of course, before the days of the Nephites and Mulekites. The land southward referred to here was later 

Bountiful and perhaps part or all of Zarahemla. The land northward was Desolation.

The problems arising in connection with the narrow neck of land are discussed later in a separate chapter. It was not the intention of the authors to present it here. They desire only to show that the land northward means Desolation, and the land southward, Bountiful, or Bountiful and Zarahemla. 

One might ask incidentally what is meant by the "large bodies of water." Does it indisputably refer to the Great 

Lakes? Are there no large bodies of water south of the region of the Great Lakes? Is not the Great Salt Lake large? Was not Utah Lake probably large in those times?

LANDS NORTH AND SOUTH 

171 

Might this not as logically have reference to them? Are there no lakes and large bodies of water in Mexico or Central America? Even in this enlightened age of rapid transportation, radio, and telephones the people in one section of our country know relatively little of the lives of those in others. One wonders how the Jaredites or the Nephites could have known of large bodies of water more than four thousand miles distant from their homes, and it is perhaps more remarkable still that they should have known that they were separate bodies of water and not the ocean. 

Were they also acquainted with Canada? Did they know that the lakes are lakes? Had they been around them? 

These are not pointless nor idle questions. 

But we wander from the point.

If it be admitted that Desolation was the land northward, and Bountiful and Zarahemla, the land southward, how shall we explain this abruptly startling and apparently inconsistent statement in Helaman? "Now the land south was called Lehi, and the land north was called Mulek, which was after the son of Zedekiah; for the Lord did bring Mulek into the land north, and Lehi into the land south." (6:10) 

Is this a contradiction of the other pattern? 

It might be that "north" and "northward" and "south" and "southward" do not mean the same, but this does not help, for we are still facing the same confusion. There must be some other explanation.

We do not know from the record exactly where Lehi landed, but we do know approximately where Mulek landed. Alma (22:30) makes it clear beyond question that the 

Mulekites came to the new world at or near the narrow neck of land. If they did not come to the land of the

Jaredites, they came very near it, for they found the ruins and the bones in that land. Lehi landed south of there. 

What is the significance of all this? 

Does the term "south" in Helaman mean the lands of 

Bountiful, Zarahemla, and Nephi? This seems the right construction. Does the word "north" mean Desolation? It seems so. How, then, shall we harmonize this with the other equally obvious explanation of the lands northward and southward? If "north" and "south" have reference to the lands farther north and south than those mentioned here,

* 

as some writers have assumed in the past, how shall we make this agree with the certain fact that the Mulekites landed near the narrow neck of land, at Bountiful and Desolation? (Alma 22:30)

The question is: Desolation seems to have been known for years and years as the land northward; Bountiful and 

Zarahemla-and at times Nephi-as the land southward; how shall we explain this sudden change of designation from those names to Mulek and Lehi?

The authors see it in this wise. During all the years that the Nephites and Lamanites had lived in the new world they had been at war. There were periods of armistice, but there was little that could have been called peace. 

For hundreds of years a state of almost perpetual war had existed. 

Suddenly something very strange occurred. Note the words of the text. "And it came to pass that when the sixty and second year of the reign of the judges had ended, all these things had happened and the Lamanites had become, the more part of them, a righteous people, insomuch that their righteousness did exceed that of the Nephites, because of their firmness and their steadiness in the faith... "Nevertheless, the people of the church did have great joy because of the conversion of the Lamanites, yea, because of the church of God, which had been established among them. And they did fellowship one with another, and did rejoice one with another, and did have great joy. "And it came to pass that many of the Lamanites did come down into the land of Zarahemla, and did declare unto the people of the Nephites the manner of their conversion, and did exhort them to faith and repentance . . . "And behold, there was peace in all the land, insomuch that the Nephites did go into whatsoever part of the land they would, whether among the Nephites or Lamanites. "And it came to pass that the Lamanites did also go whither-soever they would, whether it were among the Lamanites or among the Nephites; and thus they did have free intercourse one with another, to buy and to sell, and to get gain, according to their desire. "And it came to pass that they became exceeding rich, the Lamanites and the Nephites; and they did have an ex-

LANDS NORTH AND SOUTH 

173 ceeding plenty of gold, and of silver, and of all manner of precious metals, both in the land south and in the land north." (Helaman 6:1-9)

It must not be supposed that the Nephites and Lamanites were one people as they were following the visit of the Savior to this continent. (IV Nephi) Nevertheless, the situation was without parallel in their long history to that time. Enmities were in the main forgotten. The people lived at peace with each other. "Now the land south was called Lehi, and the land north was called Mulek, which was after the son of Zedekiah; for the Lord did bring Mulek into the land north, and 

Lehi into the land south." (v. 10)

The cross reference indicates that the lands north and south were those north and south of the narrow neck of land as shown heretofore in this chapter. Now they were suddenly known by different names. Why?

The times had changed. Why should old landmarks and old terminologies be kept to remind the people of their age-long-bitternesses? Why should new-yet old-names not be given to the lands to indicate the new state of affairs? 

What could be more natural than that the names of their founders should be given to the lands? Lehi settled south of the narrow neck of land, and the land was called Lehi. 

Mulek came to the narrow neck of land, or perhaps just north of it, to the land of the Jaredites, and that was now honored with his name.

Does this not seem to be in keeping with all the strict demands of the record? How often have we found the people in the same lands century after century! But how can we escape the conclusion that just as soon as we begin to expand the territory, we likewise begin to introduce inconsistencies? Is it not now clear that the troubles grow almost in proportion to the size of the land?

It is not likely that anyone will confuse these lands north and south with the city of Mulek and the city of Lehi south of it. Yet, it is perhaps well to point out that there was apparently no connection except an accidental one between the city of Lehi, south of Mulek, and the land of Lehi to the south, and the city of Mulek, north of Lehi, and the land of Mulek to the north. Surely Lehi did not land anywhere near the city of Lehi on the east coast! Nevertheless, this very confusion has appeared in some of the writing on this subject!

CHAPTER TWENTY-TWO FROM 23 B. C. TO 36 A. D. 

Little of present interest happened for some time after the opening year of this period, about 23 B. C. It was a time of great wickedness and activity on the part of the secret combinations. (Helaman 11:25-27) A terrible famine was required to humble the people and keep them from utter destruction.

In the year 6 B. C. Samuel the Lamanite came into Zarahemla and called upon the people to repent, (Helaman 13-16) but they did not repent. By 15 or 16 A. D. the wicked combinations had introduced a reign of terror. (III Nephi 3 fol.) 

It is in connection with this bitter struggle that we find one of the most helpful and significant passages in the entire record. The robber gangs had grown so in numbers and strength that they actually threatened the extinction of the nation.

So confident were they of their ability to conquer the civilization of the Nephites that the leader, Giddiahni, a very boastful man, wrote a letter to Governor Lachoneus telling what he and his followers were intending to do. 

(III Nephi 4:3-10) 

It was evident something had to be done; consequently, Lachoneus sent out a proclamation to all his people. "Yea, he sent a proclamation among all the people, that they should gather together their women, and their children, and their flocks and their herds, and all their substance, save it were their land, into one place." (III Nephi 3:13)

The proclamation brought gratifying response; all the people went to the appointed place. (v. 22) "And the land which was appointed was the land of Zarahemla and the land Bountiful, yea, to the line which was between the land Bountiful and the land Desolation."

This is a tremendously important paragraph! Let us discuss it from three angles. 

1. Population. Have we been thinking in terms of hundreds of millions of people? If we have, how can we harmonize this with the idea of having all the people come to one place? Does the context here suggest a teeming population of many millions? 

2. Size of Land. It must not be forgotten that we are apparently dealing with an exceedingly small land. Bountiful was the distance of a day and a half across on the north and a day on the south. The people were gathered for a prolonged siege with their belongings, and they were probably not crowded together. 

3. Location of the Land. How can we harmonize the notion of a tremendous population with the fact that this whole occurrence took place near the narrow neck of land. 

That this is the case is beyond dispute. Has it not become abundantly clear that Bountiful was the narrow neck of land? Thus the land was that in which the people had been living all the time. Where in the western world is such a place? There seems to be only one. "And they did fortify themselves against their enemies; and they did dwell in one land, and in one body." (III Nephi 3:25) "Therefore, there was no chance for the robbers to plunder and to obtain food, save it were to come up in open battle against the Nephites; and the Nephites being in one body, and having so great a number, and having reserved for themselves provisions, and horses and cattle, and flocks of every kind, that they might subsist for the space of seven years." (4:4)

The Nephites "had so great a number." Was it a great number in terms of present populations, or of populations of the BOOK OF MORMON? Were there millions for whom thousands of square miles would have been required? 

Did they meet in one body over thousands of square miles?

The robbers came out of their strongholds in the mountains and wildernesses and took possession of the deserted lands, (4:1) but the lands were valueless to them. 

They had no intention of farming them, and the Nephites had left nothing of value on them. The robbers took the land both north and south.

FROM B. C. TO 36 A. D. 

177 This probably means that they took Desolation on the north and southern Zarahemla and perhaps even part of Nephi on the south. The people were met at Bountiful and probably northern Zarahemla.

The robbers were in danger of starvation, and they had to fight or starve. They chose to fight, and there followed the greatest slaughter in the history of the people of Lehi. 

When the leader of the robbers saw that he could not overcome the people, he gave orders for his forces to march into the northernmost part of the land. (v. 23) 

This probably means that he intended going into extreme northern Desolation or even farther to the north. 

In 26 A. D. the people returned to their homes both in the north and south. (III Nephi 6:1)

There followed then a very short period of peace in which some cities were built and others rebuilt. (vs. 6-9) 

In the year 29 the church was broken up because of wickedness. (v. 14)

At that time another dissenter, one Jacob, took a group of followers to build up a kingdom in the northernmost part of the land. (7:9-13) 

Then, in the "thirty and fourth year, in the first month, on the fourth day of the month" there arose such a storm as had never before been known in all the land. (8:5)

Zarahemla was destroyed by fire; (v. 7) Moroni was sunk into the sea; (v. 9) Moronihah was covered with land; (v. 10) highways were broken up; (v. 13) many cities were burned; some fell to the ground, and many sank into the sea and the earth. (v. 14) "And thus the face of the whole earth became deformed." (v. 17)

The extent of the transformation cannot be determined. 

Whether the coast lines were changed materially, as the result of the sinking of coastal cities, we cannot tell. Just how much the surface of the land was altered is likewise left to speculation, but the changes must have been considerable and far-reaching.

Yet, does it not seem that the language should not be taken too seriously? Surely it can hardly be interpreted literally. "The face of the whole earth" became deformed.

Does this actually mean the whole earth? That can hardly be true. It does mean, however, the whole earth so far as the historian was aware. In other words, it probably referred to the earth with which he was acquainted. 

It is true that the land of the Nephites was altered sufficiently that one should be very careful in trying to fit the cities of the BOOK OF MORMON into modern settings. 

Furthermore, we should keep in mind that though the land was altered, the seat of civilization was not changed. The 

Nephites were still in their old home. 

A list of those cities that were destroyed in that tremendous cataclysm reads like a page from an epic of the giants of old. They were Zarahemla, (8:8) Moroni, (9:9) 

Moronihah, (8:10) Gilgal, (9:6) Onihah, (9:7) Mocum, (9:7) 

Jerusalem, (9:7) Gadianhi, Gadiomnah, Jacob, Gimgimno, (9:8) Jacobugath, (9:9) and Laman, Josh, Gad and Kishkumen. (9:10)

CHAPTER TWENTY-THREE THE  I. The Nephites in Their Glory. 

We shall here see whether the later Nephite civilization flourished in the same land or lands as that of the earlier years, and whether the landmarks remained the same in relation to human improvements. To make the views of most students tenable it is necessary to show that a very extensive northward migration took place. Was there such a movement?

As late as the visit of the Savior to the western world there had been no rooting up of the nation because the 

Savior came to Bountiful. (III Nephi 11) Let us take up the story after the crucifixion. 

Within two years after the visit of Jesus the Nephites had established churches in all the lands claimed by them. (IV Nephi 1) Peace and prosperity settled upon the nation. (IV Nephi) Many new cities were built, and many were rebuilt, among them Zarahemla. (vs. 7-8) There were no contentions, and the people had all things in common. (vs. 

18, 3) It was an age of miracles. (v. 13) Everyone was wonderfully blessed. (v. 18)

This period of love and fellowship continued for about 167 years, until approximately 200 A. D. The Nephites spread over all the land. (v. 23)

By 200 many had become puffed up in their hearts. (v. 

24) By 210 some divisions had come among them. (v. 26)

Despite these things the people remained peaceful. There were neither Nephites nor Lamanites. "There were no robbers, nor murderers, neither were there Lamanites, nor any manner of Ites; but they were in one, the children of Christ, and heirs to the kingdom of God." (v. 17)

During the last many years, though peace continued, there was much wickedness, and there began, about 200, to

be Lamanites again. (v. 20)

A. The Heyday of Ancient America. 

What were the people doing all these years? For nearly 

290 years, from 33 A. D. to 322 A. D., there was no fighting.

Here was an era of uninterrupted peace and prosperity. 

Yet, the record of it is contained in one chapter of forty nine verses, the Book of IV Nephi. 

The people filled the whole land. (v. 23) A tremendous growth in population must have taken place during that long period of time. The population could have doubled three or four times. It was an era of great activity. 

Doubtless many roads were made. Buildings of many kinds. were constructed.

Then might the Nephites have spread over a great territory. They could well have pushed into both North and 

South America. A mighty and highly-developed civilization flourished. That was the glorious era of the Nephites and 

Lamanites. It was the zenith of their career. It could have been at that time that they produced many of the magnificent works that so delight and astound the world today. 

There was time; they had the wealth; the total absence of war made it possible to turn their energies to works of an enduring character. When the truth is at last known, we may find that the noblest monuments of ancient American culture belong to the period between 30 A. D. and 330 A. 

D., to give approximate dates.

Does all this, then justify the contention that the Nephites became concentrated in what is now the United States?

In the year 320 A. D. Ammaron, the grandson of the fourth Nephi, hid all the sacred records in the hill Shim, in the land of Antum. (IV Nephi, verse 48; Mormon 1:3)

The significance of this must not be overlooked. The hill Shim now becomes a location of first importance. 

By turning again to Ether 9:3 we shall find that King

Omer, the fourth ruler of the Jaredites, in his flight from his rebellious son Jared passed this hill Shim on his way to the eastern coast of the country. Where was the hill? It was in the land to which the Jaredites came in the new world. It was in the land of their first inheritance, and it must not be forgotten that in that same flight Omer also passed the hill Ramah or Cumorah, where both Jaredites

and Nephites were destroyed. 

It is worthy of note in passing that the Nephite historian told the story of this incident, not the Jaredite historian. That is, our account of it comes from Moroni directly, from Ether only indirectly. What the Jaredites called the hill, if other than Shim, and the land in which it was situated, we have no idea. The important thing is that from the days of the Jaredites to the end of the Nephites it seems to have been a prominent place. 

Where, then, were the Nephites at the time of Ammaron and Mormon? Is it not clear that they were in the land of 

Desolation, the home of the Jaredites, and does it not seem to have been the place where the Nephites were destroyed? 

It is certain that as late as 345 A. D., or thereabouts, the 

Nephites were still in their old home, for at that time Mormon, the leader of the Nephite armies, went to the hill Shim and removed the records. (Mormon 2:17)

The final long struggle began about 322. (Mormon 1:8) 

Interestingly and logically enough the war began at the southern boundary of Zarahemla, at the head of the river 

Sidon. (Mormon 1:10) In the first encounters the Laman ites were beaten. (vs. 11-12) This was a time of wickedness so great that the three Nephite disciples were taken away. (v. 13) 

Mormon, though only sixteen years of age, was made commander-in-chief of the Nephite armies. (Mormon 2:1)

In the year 327 the Lamanites came in such numbers that the Nephites would not try to face them but started to fly northward. (Mormon 2:3)

The Lamanites, of course, came from their own land, the land of Nephi. The previous battles had been fought, naturally enough, on the border between the two lands. 

Had the Nephites stood their ground, this next conflict too would have taken place near the narrow strip of wilderness. But instead of fighting they fled toward the north. 

Does this mean to the United States? Does it not rather signify that they started northward through the land of Zarahemla toward the narrow pass and the natural bulwark behind which so many harassed Nephite armies had taken refuge?

As an interesting sidelight the authors call attention to

the fact that after the visit of the Savior the name Bountiful is not mentioned. Bountiful appears to have become a regular part of Zarahemla.

At this point a new name appears. The Nephites entrenched themselves in the city of Angola. (Mormon 2:4)

Was this a new country or a new name in an old country?

The Lamanites drove the Nephites, not from Angola alone but from David as well. (2:4-5) They fled to the city of Joshua, on the west seashore. (v. 6) In the year 

345 they fled again, this time to Jashon. (v. 17)

This mention of Joshua is interesting as almost the first time the west coast has been connected with the fighting of the Nephites. There was one other time, during the wars of Moroni, when reference was made to the cities on the west.

There can be little doubt that the Nephites were all this time moving northward. But how far? Was it hundreds of miles, or thousands of miles? Was it into a new country?

They fled through the extent of Zarahemla, through what had once been Bountiful, and into the land of Desolation. 

How can we know this? Mormon 2:17 has this to say: "And now, the city of Jashon was near the land where

Ammaron had deposited the records unto the Lord." The records, of course, had been deposited in the hill Shim. The hill Shim was in the land through which Omer passed while traveling eastward from Moron to the seashore in the land of the Jaredites' first inheritance, the country where they landed. (Ether 9:3) Was this country not one with the site of the hill Ramah or Cumorah where the Jaredites and 

Nephites were destroyed? (Ether 15:11; Mormon 6:6) Thus, the armies were apparently still in the original home of the Jaredites.

The Nephites were driven again. This time they stopped at Shem, a place still farther north. (Mormon 2:20)

How far northward was Shem? Was it in territory now constituting part of the United States?

Another battle was fought in which the Lamanites were beaten. The armies of Mormon pursued them until they had regained the lands of their inheritance. (Mormon 2:27)

Therefore, the armies did not get very far from their permanent home after all. The fighting all took place in familiar territory. 

Which were the lands of the Nephites' inheritance?

They were, of course, Bountiful and Zarahemla. The fighting that had taken place just previous to the turn in the affairs of the Nephites had been done in Desolation, as the hill Shim makes plain, and the city of Jashon.

And now we come to a statement that should commend itself to every sincere searcher for the truths of the divine record of ancient America.

II. The Nephites in Final Conflict. 

In the year 350 the Nephite lands were divided again. 

The Nephites took the northern portions, and the Lamanites took the southern portions. What was the dividing line? 

It was the narrow neck of land! (Mormon 2:20)

And so we are right back where we were at the very beginning at the place where the east was so close to the west sea. Does it not appear beyond controversy that the people were in the same place all the time? 

For ten years, from 350 to 360, no further fighting took place. During those years the opposing forces maintained their relative positions. The Nephites were still north of the narrow neck of land; the Lamanites were still south of it. (Mormon 3:5) In 361 the Lamanites came down against the city of Desolation. (v. 7)

It now becomes clear that the city of Desolation was near, and north of, the narrow pass or narrow neck of land. 

It was also near the seashore, so near in fact that the bodies of those killed in the battle were thrown into the sea. (Mormon 3:7-9)

The next year the Lamanites came down to Desolation again to battle. (v. 8) The Nephites were victorious and boasted in their strength. Because of this, Mormon refused to lead them any longer. . In 363 the Nephites took the offensive and went up, out of the land of Desolation, against their enemies.

For perhaps the first time Desolation is spoken of as down. Whether or not this is significant, who can tell? A very simple explanation is that the country where the fighting was taking place was quite unlevel. One army would,

therefore, be higher than the other. If the city Desolation was near enough to the sea that the bodies of the dead could be thrown into the ocean, would it not have been near sealevel? That being the case, an army camped near it, but inland, would have been up from it. 

It is, of course, not unthinkable that the changes in the land had caused a difference in the relative elevation of the various cities and localities.

The Nephites were defeated and fled, and the invaders captured Desolation. (Morom 4:2) Those Nephites who escaped death and capture took refuge in the city of Teancum, a city on the shore very near to Desolation. (v. 3) 

There they were attacked, but they succeeded in defending themselves. (v. 8) Then in anger they went against the 

Lamanites and recaptured Desolation. (v. 8) Their success, however, was turned to defeat when sometime in 366 the invaders took it back again. (v. 13) 

Then, in 367, the Nephites drove the enemy entirely out of their lands. (v. 15)

The relative positions of the peoples were thus still unchanged! The Lamanites were still in Nephi, and the 

Nephites were still in possession of their own lands. There had been no movement, no migration. For practical purposes they were back at the beginning of their history! 

In 375 the Lamanites came down in such numbers that they could not be counted. (Mormon 4:18)

The word "down" obviously means that they came, as always before, from Nephi, for, of course, the contest was beginning anew with the Lamanites once more pushing out from their own land.

In the struggles that followed, the Lamanites captured Desolation and Boaz, and the Nephites fled before them. (Mormon 4:19-22) Mormon went to the hill Shim and took from it all the records to keep them from falling into the hands of the Lamanites. (v. 23)

The Lamanites had at last taken possession of at least part of the homeland of the Jaredites. This was the farthest north they had ever been.

Mormon repented of his oath not to lead the armies any more and at once assumed command. (Mormon 5:1)

Nevertheless, he was driven to the city of Jordan. (v. 3)

By 380 A. D. the Lamanites were so numerous that the defenders could not stand against them, and they sought respite in flight. (vs. 6-7) 

Where did they go this time? They fled to the hill

Cumorah, and there Mormon hid the records. 

This seems to be the only place in the entire BOOK OF MORMON into which one can read the idea of any rooting up of the Nephite civilization. If there is here sufficient to justify that conclusion, and the conclusion is in harmony with the rest of the record and does violence to none of it, there can not be the slightest objection. The authors' only hope has been to show that the BOOK OF

MORMON is consistent, and when that harmony is established, upon whatever grounds, that hope is satisfied.

Let us go to the record for a few paragraphs. "But it came to pass that whatsoever lands we had passed by, and the inhabitants thereof were not gathered in, were destroyed by the Lamanites, and their towns, and villages, and their cities were burned with fire; and thus three hundred and seventy and nine years passed away. (Mormon 5:5) "And it came to pass that in the three hundred and eightieth year the Lamanites did come again against us to battle, and we did stand against them boldly; but it was all in vain, for so great were their numbers that they did tread the people of the Nephites under their feet. (v. 6) "And it came to pass that we did again take to flight, and those whose flight was swifter than the Lamanites' did escape and those whose flight did not exceed the Lamanites' were swept down and destroyed." (v. 7) 66 and it came to pass that we did march forth before the Lamanites. "And I, Mormon, wrote an epistle unto the king of the Lamanites, and desired of him that he would grant unto us that we might gather together our people unto the land of Cumorah, by a hill which was called Cumorah, and there we would give them battle." (Mormon 6:2) "And it came to pass that we did march forth to the land of Cumorah, and we did pitch our tents round about the hill Cumorah; and it was in a land of many waters, rivers, and fountains; and here we had hope to gain advan-

tage over the Lamanites." (v. 4) 

It is significant that the place was already known as Cumorah. Evidently it was not a new country. 

From 380 to 384 the Nephites gathered their forces, and in the 385th year the final battles took place. (v. 6 fol.) 

There can be no denying that these movements covered considerable ground and required some time. There is not a suggestion, however, as to the extent of either. To say that from these few citations one can justify a flight of thousands of miles can hardly be true, for no such thing is said. 

That this is of first importance to every reader of the BOOK OF MORMON none will deny, and until such time as the question shall be answered with finality, each will have to determine in his own mind from such information as he has available just how far the Nephites moved in those unforgettable and dramatic times. 

Where was the hill Cumorah?

Did the Nephites and their enemies make a journey of thousands of miles from the neighborhood of the hill Shim, gather their people, and fight their final battles within the last eight or nine years? If that is the clear message of the

BOOK OF MORMON, there should be none to question it. 

There is, however, one reasonable requirement: That it be in harmony with such other facts as are known. 

Let us review briefly a few details relative to the hill Cumorah.

The hill Cumorah was the hill Ramah of the Jaredites. 

There seems not to be a line in the story of the Jaredites to give the impression of any northward migration at all from the Moron of the Jaredites to the Ramah of the Jaredites. 

When King Omer, the fourth king of the Jaredites, fled from the menace of Jared, he went eastward and in his flight passed both the hill Shim, where Ammaron later hid the Nephite records, and the hill Cumorah, where Mormon later hid part of those records and where the Nephites were destroyed. The only directions mentioned are east and south. If there was a flight of thousands of miles to the north, there is no mention of it here. Does it not seem that the Jaredites were destroyed in their own homeland? If they were, where were the Nephites destroyed?

Put succinctly, the question is: Taken in its entirety, does the BOOK OF MORMON say that the civilizations were destroyed in the homelands of the peoples or in a land far distant?

That the hill Cumorah was in a land of many waters, rivers, and fountains is not sufficiently explicit to determine much from it. The Great Lakes region has all the requirements. Likewise the beautiful valley of Mexico and the country to the south and east of it fit the description perfectly. 

This land of rivers, lakes, and fountains was doubtless: the place to which Morianton intended going. (Alma 50:29)

It was in all probability the place in which the forty-three scouts found the records of Ether. (Mosiah 8:8) It must have been this same country to which many people migrated in 46 B. C. (Helaman 3:4) 

One cannot help questioning seriously whether Morianton, the forty-three scouts, and the restless Nephite emigrants were acquainted with the Great Lakes region. 

Where in the BOOK OF MORMON can one find reason to believe that the peoples of ancient America traversed vast distances? A close reading inevitably tends, it seems to the present authors, to the conclusion that those peoples lived and died in the same place, a relatively restricted land. They probably scattered to great distances, but did the whole nation move? This seems very doubtful.

CHAPTER TWENTY-FOUR SIZE OF NEPHITE POPULATION 

The time has now come to devote some consideration to the matter of the size of the Nephite population. The company at the beginning was very small, but the Nephites were augmented by the Mulekites of whom we have no information at all. Again, they received the large number of converts from the Lamanites, and throughout their history they were continually being cut into by the dissenters to say nothing of the wars, fevers, wild animals, and frequent destructions.

However, we are not left altogether without evidence. 

I. Population Indicated in the Record. 

A careful reading of Alma 2:5; 16:21; 22:27; 45:22; 46:28; 

50:6; Helaman 4:5; 6:38; 11:21; IV Nephi 23; and Ether 7:11, to give only a few of them, will show that the people constantly covered the face of the whole land. These citations cover a period of approximately 2,200 years, from 2,000 B. 

C. to about 200 A. D. (Ether 11:7; IV Nephi 23)

Periodically throughout that long era the land was covered with inhabitants. What land was it? Was it North and South America? Whatever land it was, does it not appear beyond dispute that it was the same all the time? 

Let us select, more or less at random, one reference from those given above. "And it came to pass on the other hand, that the Nephites did build them (the Gadiantons) up and support them, beginning at the more wicked part of them, until they had overspread all the land of the Nephites." (Helaman 6:38) 

Did the Gadiantons too cover the whole land? Were there, then, hundreds of millions of them as well? What

SIZE OF NEPHITE POPULATION 

189 land was it over which they spread? Is there a single line of evidence to show that it was other than the lands of the people, Zarahemla, Bountiful, and Desolation?

Where were those lands? It seems that Bountiful was the narrow neck of land, that Zarahemla was immediately to the south, and Desolation, immediately to the north. 

Zarahemla and Bountiful were the "one place" to which the people had been gathered in order to fight the robbers. Did the Nephites extend from Uruguay to the Great Lakes?

A. Evidence of High Death Rate. 

"And it came to pass that the Nephites who were not slain by the weapons of war-now the number of the slain were not numbered, because of the greatness of their numbers after they had finished burying their dead they all returned to their lands, and to their houses, and their wives, and their children. "Now many women and children had been slain with the sword." (Alma 3:1-2)

Is not this a key to the total extinction of two ancient American peoples? The women and children fought with 

This fact is almost ever-present throughout the record. "And in one year were thousands and tens of thousands of souls sent to the eternal world" (Alma 3:26)

This was in the same year as the other instance given above, 87 B. C., and might have included some of those killed in the other struggle. "But behold, in one day it (Ammonihah) was left desolate; and the carcases were mangled by dogs and wild beasts of the wilderness." (Alma 16:10) "And from the first year to the fifteenth has brought to pass the destruction of many thousands of lives; yea, it has brought to pass an awful scene of bloodshed." (Alma 28:10) 

The latter date in this instance was 76 B. C. "Now the dead were not numbered because of the greatness of their numbers; neither were the dead of the 

Nephites numbered." (Alma 30:2) 

This was in 75 B. C.

"Now the number of the dead were not numbered because of the greatness of the numbers; yea, the number of the dead was exceeding great, both on the Nephites and on the Lamanites." (Alma 45:21) 

This was the year 73 B. C. 

The dead mentioned in the foregoing quotations apparently did not include the thousands killed in the Moronite wars. It is doubtful if they take into account other numerous dead who fell in the uprisings under Nehor and Amlici. They are quite apart from the toll of lives taken in the struggles between Pahoran and the king-men and the second greatest slaughter in the history of the nation, the strife between Lachoneus and the robbers. They do not include most of the dead in the age-old conflict with the secret combinations nor the large number who migrated northward beyond the limits of the record. 

How, under such conditions, could the Nephites have increased to hundreds of millions of people? 

Does it not appear to be beyond argument that the land was relatively small, a land the whole face of which could be covered with people numbered in millions? How could hundreds of millions of men, women, and children get into "one place" in the sense in which the Nephites did?

Is it likely that the Nephite population was ever large in terms of modern populations at least until after the death of the Savior? During the period of nearly three hundred years of peace that elapsed after His visit to America the people could have become truly numerous. Yet, those who remained within the confines of the same lands in which they had always lived probably did not grow into a vast empire. 

B. Population as Revealed by the Closing Struggles.

About 322 A. D. the closing struggle began. (Mormon 

2:1) By 331 or 332 Mormon was trying to get the people into one body. (2:7) In that same year there was blood and carnage in all the land. (v. 8) Aaron, the Lamanite king, came against Mormon with 44,000. Mormon met him with 42,000. (v. 9)

These 42,000 were presumably all men. It is hardly likely that so early in the conflict the women and children were taking part.

SIZE OF NEPHITE POPULATION 

191 In 344, thousands were slain. (v. 15) In 346 Mormon met fifty thousand with thirty thousand. (v. 25) There was a truce from 350 to 360. (Mormon 3:1, 4) Almost continual bloodshed followed. In 366 occurred a horrible slaughter. (3:11) 

What percentage of the population were those 42,000 men? Would it be one man in two or one in ten? If two, there is a key to about 600,000 people; if ten, there were 

8,000,000 to 10,000,000. Is it not extremely unlikely that only one man in ten would be in arms under such trying conditions? It was a period of national peril. Yet, at that estimate could there have been more than twelve million in the nation? It must be remembered that that was at the close of nearly three hundred years of continual peace. It was just past the zenith of the career of the Nephites.

Dr. H. J. Spinden, one of the leading authorities on ancient American civilizations, offers some interesting information on this subject. He estimates the population of the 

First Mayan Empire at eight million or even more. " Moreover, it is very significant that Dr. Spinden suggests for that empire the period from about 600 B. C. to between 300 to 600 

A. D. Notice how closely these facts agree with those given for the Nephites. Still more remarkable is the additional fact that he puts that civilization in Central America almost at the exact place that the material of the BOOK OF MOR- 

MON seems to suggest.

In the year 380 the Nephites were trodden under foot. (Mormon 5:6)

One wonders if there were millions or hundreds of millions thus destroyed. 

About 380 A. D. Mormon wrote to the leader of the 

Lamanites for permission to gather his people. (6:2) The permission was given, and for four years there was little or no fighting while the people gathered at Cumorah. (v. 5) 

In the last battle 230,000 were killed on the Nephite side. (Mormon 6:11-15) That was the last war.

They were probably all men. If each had a wife and family an extremely unlikely thing-there would have been about twenty million killed in that contest counting losses on both sides. 

32. "Population of Ancient America," Smithsonian Institute 

Annual Report, 1929, pp. 462 ff.

The writers believe there could easily have been that many. If we suppose that another twenty million had fallen previously, we find that there might have been at the height of their glory about thirty to fifty million Nephites. 

II. Estimates Greatly Dissimilar. 

It would run the present volume far beyond its necessary limits to enquire into all statements and estimates that have been made regarding the possible populations of the ancient inhabitants of this hemisphere. Indeed, it may not be advisable to devote more space to the subject. Still, it is interesting to note the great differences in estimates that have been made. Some place the number of Nephites, for instance, in the hundreds of millions. 

It is difficult for the present writers to conceive of such numbers for the Nephites. There seems to be no mathematical basis on which to justify it or, for that matter, to compute a population at all. The Nephites, though few in number in the beginning, must have increased rather rapidly. Their numbers were swelled by the Mulekites after about four hundred years in the new world; they were further increased by some few thousands of converted Lamanites. On the other hand they were constantly being diminished by apostasy, and war and disease were everpresent among them.

Nevertheless, a little speculation may not be wholly out of order. It is to be doubted seriously that the Nephites doubled oftener than every one hundred years under the conditions under which they lived. If we say for the sake of a beginning that there were at the height of their civilization, about 330 A. D., 200,000,000 Nephites, they could not have counted more than half that, or 100,000,000, in 230 A. D. 

Allowing one hundred years, we would compute a population of fifty million for the year 130 A. D., and 25,000,000 for 30 A. D. In 70 B. C. there would have been 12,500,000; in 170 B. C., 6,250,000; in 270 B. C., 3,125,000.

In 370 B. C. there would have been, on this basis, 1,562,- 

500; in 470 B. C., 781,250, and in 570, about the time of the landing, 390,625. There were, however, only about twenty

Nephites. There must have been, therefore, in the original colony of the Mulekites about 390,605 persons!

If one hundred years be considered too long a time for a doubling of the population, set it down to seventy. In 600 

B. C. there would still have had to be about 24,000 Mulekites,

SIZE OF NEPHITE POPULATION 

193 and if the doubling be established at fifty years, a rate of increase probably never equaled in human history before the nineteenth century, there would still have been more than seven hundred in the Mulekite colony. 

Apply this same kind of computation to the Jaredites, and we shall find that at the time of Omer, when the nation was apparently almost destroyed, there must have been, not thirty and a few more, but 24,000. At seventy years for a one-hundred-percent increase there must have been more than three hundred Jaredites.

Now, it must be granted that there is an element of absurdity in all this, owing simply to the fact that populations do not increase on any such fanciful mathematical lines; (and that is the point to the discussion) still, there is also an inexorable logic behind it. If the descendants of the eight persons surviving the Flood had doubled in numbers each century, that is, if the number of births over deaths had been sufficient to double the number in each one hundred years, there would have been in the world today the staggering and inconceivable number of 102,368,744,177,632 people, unless the writers' figures are badly handled! As a matter of fact the world has a meager two billion! 

Does this not show that people do not increase so fast as figures?

It seems to the authors that the only way in which hundreds of millions of people could have accumulated in the BOOK OF MORMON lands would have been by a constant influx of immigrants. 

Still, despite all these improbabilities, there is a very simple way in which such vastly dissimilar estimates can be harmonized. If these figures be intended to represent the population of the Nephites in their homelands AND ALL 

THEIR OFFSHOOTS IN ALL OTHER LANDS TO WHICH 

THEY SPREAD, there can be not the slightest objection. 

If, however, they be applied only to those who remained within the limits of the history, the story of the BOOK OF MORMON, it is exceedingly doubtful whether justification can be found for such precedure.

CHAPTER TWENTY-FIVE THE KEY LOCATION 

Though much uncertainty exists regarding many details of the subject, the location of the lands of the BOOK OF MORMON appears to be established beyond any question relatively speaking. Within a few hundred miles one might be wrong in suggesting locations, but there is little possibility that one can miss the whole land.

In making the foregoing statements the authors have in mind the seeming certainty of location established by the narrow neck of land.

Yet, despite this assurance that this place offers a key to geography of the BOOK OF MORMON, we are in fairness and logic bound to confess that this is not actually the case. Does the narrow neck of land refer unquestionably to a location in Central America?

In the past the writers on this subject have been accustomed to think in terms of Central or South America as the early lands of the Jaredites and Nephites. For the purpose of getting both peoples into the United States for their destruction they have assumed large-scale migrations to the northward. The writers here use the word "assumed" in view of the apparent lack of justification in the record for such migrations. 

Recently, however, narrow necks of land other than those in Central America have been proposed. Only within the past few months the authors have had brought to their attention the suggestion that the narrow neck is between the southern extremities of Lake Michigan and Lake Erie.

The authors have no particular objection to this location though they question seriously the procedure that leads to it. Is not this the type of thinking that has led to so much unnecessary controversy and difference of opinion on this subject? Is it not the reason that the whole matter is ap-

THE KEY LOCATION 

195 proached by many with such fear and trembling? Let us note again a caution made previously: It is not enough to find a narrow neck of land; we must find a narrow neck of land near north, south, east, and west seas, in a mountainous and forbidding country of tropical or semi-tropical nature, with gold, silver, and iron in abundance. We must find a narrow neck of land that is a day's-journey wide, in a land very apparently limited in extent, that is almost surrounded by water. We must find a narrow neck of land within quick and easy access of the east and west oceans across which the colonists sailed to their landing places. We must find a narrow neck of land that could easily be fortified.

Thus our task may not be so easy as it at first appears. 

The authors believe, however, that a thorough analysis of the BOOK OF MORMON will give much help in solving this problem. Still, they here make little attempt to locate cities and lands in terms of present-day topography. Rather their purpose is to locate cities, lands, and places with respect to each other wherever they were. 

I. The Narrow Neck of Land. 

This fundamental point of location, the narrow neck of land, is given many times in the period convering the lives of three nations in the new world. Throughout the whole time it remained the same so far as the writers tell. As early as the days of Lib, the Jaredite hunter-king, the narrow neck of land was a landmark of considerable importance. "And they built a great city by the narrow neck of land, by the place where the sea divides the land. And they did preserve the land southward for a wilderness, to get game.

And the whole face of the land northward was covered with inhabitants." (Ether 10:20-21)

The expression, "where the sea divides the land," is not clear. There could have been a lake, or an inlet of the sea, at the narrow place. Or it might just be an unusual way of saying that the land was very narrow. At any rate there seems to be no doubt that the place referred to here is the same to which attention is called many times later. ".... and thus the land of Nephi and the land of Zarahemla were nearly surrounded by water, there being a small neck of land between the land northward and the land southward." (Alma 22:32)

The land northward here was the land of Desolation as always. Between Desolation and Bountiful the distance from sea to sea was a day-and-a-half's journey for a Nephite. (Alma 22:32) Between Bountiful and Zarahemla the distance from sea to sea was a day's-journey for a Nephite. (Helaman 4:7) Bountiful, therefore, was for practical purposes the narrow neck of land-if the narrow neck was the place where the land was a day's-journey and a day-and-ahalf's journey. "And it came to pass that Hagoth, he being an exceedingly curious man, therefore he went forth and built him an exceedingly large ship, on the borders of the land Bountiful, by the land Desolation, and launched it forth into the west sea, by the narrow neck which led into the land northward." (Alma 63:5)

Here the same relative set-up is maintained. The land northward was Desolation; the land southward, Bountiful and Zarahemla. "And there they did fortify against the Lamanites, from the west sea even unto the east; it being a day's journey for a Nephite, on the line which they had fortified and stationed their armies to defend their north country." (Helaman 4:7)

One cannot help wondering if they were able to fortify the whole width of the land there, however small it might have been. Might there not have been a lake or an inland arm of the sea at that point or a mountain pass?

The following citation shows that after all we can know but little of the actual details. "And it came to pass that they did multiply and spread, and did go forth from the land southward to the land northward, and did spread insomuch that they began to cover the face of the whole earth, from the sea south to the sea north, from the sea west to the sea east." (Helaman 3:8)

Other statements add interest and importance to this problem of the narrow neck of land.

II. The Narrow Pass or Passage. 

"And it came to pass that they did not head them until they had come to the borders of the land Desolation; and there they did head them, by the narrow pass which led by the sea into the land northward, yea, by the sea on the west and on the east." (Alma 50:34)

THE KEY LOCATION 

197 Again the authors frankly confess that they have no explanation to offer as to the details. How the narrow pass led by the sea on the west and on the east, or what the narrow pass was they connot determine. There is little indication. It might have been a small strip of land between two bodies of water or a valley between two mountains, or a combination of them. The neck must have been extremely narrow if the pass was by both seas, east and west. "And he (Moroni) also sent orders unto him (Teancum) that he should fortify the land Bountiful, and secure the narrow pass which led into the land northward, (Desolation) lest the Lamanites should obtain that point and should have power to harass them on every side." (Alma 52:9)

Moroni was at that time on the west front. Once more we see the relative locations of land as the focal point, the land Bountiful, the place at the narrow neck. "And the Lamanites did give unto us the land northward, yea, even to the narrow passage which led into the land southward. And we did give unto the Lamanites all the land southward." (Mormon 2:29)

Now, this question will doubtless have arisen in the reader's mind: Do the authors assume that the narrow pass and the narrow neck of land were the same? The answer is in the negative, but the difference is more apparent than real.

The real truth seems to be, though it may be entirely wrong, that the narrow neck of land and the narrow pass were geographically the same. That is, they were at the same place, but they were not necessarily the same. The narrow neck of land was some point in Central America, was it not? The narrow pass was some sort of natural formation at or in the narrow neck of land. That is why it has already been suggested, for instance, that the narrow pass might have been a valley between two ranges of hills or mountains or between the mountains and the sea. If the isthmus was as narrow at that point as it seems to have been, the mountains on both sides would in the very nature of things have been very near each other. 

How can anyone read the foregoing quotations, showing identically the same relationship of lands to lands, Zarahemla on the south, Bountiful at the narrow place, and Desolation on the north, and still believe that the narrow neck of land and the narrow pass were separated by miles?

They might, indeed, have been the same. 

Another point is of interest here in passing. Whatever transformations took place in the land at the time of the crucifixon seem not to have altered materially the narrow neck of land. It is mentioned in the Jaredite record, at a period hundreds of years before the coming of the Savior.

It was important in the momentous years just before the birth of Christ, and it figured prominently in the last war of the Nephites and Lamanites. It might be noted in passing that Mormon does not call it the narrow neck of land; his expression is narrow pass or passage. That they were the same, however, can hardly be denied. 

III. Where Was the Narrow Neck of Land? 

Where was the narrow neck of land? Have the readers of the BOOK OF MORMON in the past been right in assuming that the present Isthmus of Panama is the place? 

The present writers doubt this very seriously. They feel confident that the narrow neck of land was farther north. One of the foremost authorities in the Church placed it at the Isthmus of Tehuantepec, and a number of other students have agreed with this. The present writers have no objection though this is the result of a feeling rather than any definite evidence. But, then, is there any proof that the Isthmus of Panama was the narrow neck of the 

BOOK OF MORMON? There is none. 

For want of something better the writers tentatively accept the view that the Isthmus of Tehuantepec was the narrow neck. It might perhaps as well have been the Isthmus of Honduras. As suggested above, there is little that will lead to certainty.

However, the rejection of the Isthmus of Panama is not based entirely upon a guess. There is good reason to believe, from the information of the text itself, that it could not have been the location in question. 

Note the following quotation. and thus the land of Nephi and the land of Zarahemla were nearly surrounded by water, there being a small neck of land between the land northward and the land southward." (Alma 22:32)

The lands of Nephi and Zarahemla were nearly surrounded by water! That is interesting! There was water on the west, the west sea (in all probability the Pacific 

Ocean); there was likewise water on the east, the east sea

THE KEY LOCATION 

199 (as probably the Atlantic Ocean), but where was the water on the north and south?

Granted that this is but a suggestion, without the confirmation of the text, is not this at least a logical explanation? The narrow neck of land on the north indicates that the two seas were relatively near each other there. In other words, the water of the two seas nearly came together at that place. When we remember that what is now the 

Isthmus of Panama might have been as it is now, or much the same, to the south of the land of Nephi, we can see at once that the seas were very near each other there. Thus the lands were nearly surrounded by water. 

Many students of the BOOK OF MORMON have placed the land of Nephi in South America. Some of these same students have the land of Zarahemla elsewhere. 

Yet, the lands of Nephi and Zarahemla were nearly surrounded by water. This can mean only that both lands were for all practical purposes the same territory. Indeed, they were separated only by the narrow strip of wilderness. If 

Nephi was in South America, does it not follow that Zarahemla would have had to be there too? How many people would have had to live there to fill that tremendous land from sea to sea? Would they have known that the land is but a vast island, that there is water all around? 

Yet, in justice of those who have presumed South America to have been the land of Nephi, it must be admitted that it is NEARLY surrounded by water. Only the isthmus keeps the water divided.

Panama is about four hundred miles long with a width that is at all places limited. When Morianton and his people started northward and were headed by Teancum's army, they reached the narrow pass. Is it likely that they traveled two or three or perhaps four hundred miles before being turned back?

It will be admitted by all that no one knows the width of the land at the narrow pass in the BOOK OF MORMON.

It is possible that all the isthmuses of today are too wide or too narrow. The convulsions that visited the earth at the time of the crucifixion may have changed all the boundary lines. Of these things we know nothing. 

It may be said in closing this chapter that the best evidence as to the location of the narrow neck of land is to be found in the field of archaeology. Science can give far more help on this question than the record itself.

CHAPTER TWENTY-SIX EXPANSION NOT ROOTING UP 

Here is a challenging, if not startling, statement, the truth of which will almost surely become more apparent with the passing of time: Nothing in the BOOK OF MOR- 

MON justifies the claim that it is the history of the western hemisphere. Is it not rather the history of a portion of 

God's people in the western hemisphere?

We have already seen, in the discussion of the Basques, the possibility that even some of the Jaredites broke away and led to a division. Though the book has nothing to say about this, it would seem to be in no way out of harmony with the text to think that in the sixteen centuries that the Jaredites lived in the new world many may have moved away from the knowledge of the historians. It is certain that many of the Nephites thus moved away and became lost to the record.

Here arises an important consideration which seems to have been missed heretofore in the study of the BOOK OF 

MORMON. EXPANSION IS NOT NECESSARILY ROOT- 

ING UP. A nation may, and most nations do, throw off groups, as colonists and pioneers leave their homes for the uncharted places. Does this involve the moving of the permanent seat of government? Did the coming of the Pilgrims lead to the dismantling of the British kingdom? Did the coming of the Pioneers bring about the destruction of the civilization in the East? If this were true, there would be no growth since the movement of one would involve the annihilation of the other.

Many Nephites moved away from the homelands, principally Zarahemla and Bountiful. These went away from the lands of their inheritance and established themselves in other lands. Did this cause the abandonment of the earlier homes? "And it came to pass that Hagoth, he being an exceed-

EXPANSION NOT ROOTING UP 

201 ingly curious man, therefore he went forth and built him an exceedingly large ship on the borders of the land Bountiful, by the land Desolation, and launched it forth into the west sea, by the narrow neck which led into the land northward. "And behold, there were many of the Nephites who did enter therein and did sail forth with much provisions, and also many women and children; and they took their course northward." (Alma 63:5-6)

The exact number who left the homeland at that time is not known. They served, however, only to diminish the number of those who remained and increase the number of those peopling the north country. "And in the thirty and eighth year, this man built other ships. And the first ship did also return, and many more people did enter into it; and they also took much provisions, and set out again to the land northward." (v. 7) "And it came to pass that one other ship also did sail forth; and whither she did go we know not." (v. 8)

The foregoing declaration shows clearly the historian's realization that many moved away from his knowledge. 

There were yet others. 

Later others went, Corianton among them, to carry food to those who had gone north. Corianton did not return and was lost to the history. Evidently he found things to his liking among those who had migrated. While we know absolutely nothing about them, we are perfectly safe in conjecturing that they were growing in number all the time. 

Not only did many of the Nephites go to the north, the Lamanites as well expanded in that direction. (Helaman 

6:6) The two brothers, Nephi and Lehi, preached in that new country and returned to their homes. (v. 6) 

It appears, therefore, that there was some contact between the old home and the new in the case of some of the colonists. Many, however, entirely disappeared as will be seen by reference to a verse already quoted. (Alma 63:8) 

It is commonly supposed among the Latter-day Saints that some of the Pacific islands were peopled by the Nephites, presumably by Hagoth, but the historian of the 

BOOK OF MORMON tells nothing of this. 

Alma 63:4 and 9 tell of others who migrated to the north.

One company alone (v. 4) counted 5,400 men besides women and children. This citation is of particular interest as the only one giving a definite number. Whether these people went beyond Desolation is uncertain, but the absence of cross references would seem to suggest that they did. They might have spread into and through the United States as ruins everywhere indicate.

If this company included as many women as men, which may have been unlikely, there would have been nearly eleven thousand adults. If this number be decreased to ten thousand, and four children be allowed to the family, making twenty thousand, we have about thirty thousand people in this one movement. Computing one doubling to the century, this one group alone by 1,000 A. D. could have numbered 45,000,000. The other companies, for which no numbers are given, might easily have swelled this total to a hundred million or more, depending, of course, upon the number of the people who went north. These may have peopled large sections of the United States or all of it. 

The authors have no quarrel with this; they believe it. 

They do not object to the thought that the Jaredites and Nephites spread over a large territory. They do not oppose the view that many people moved to immense distances. 

Their thesis is that those who did thus move went outside narrative of the BOOK OF MORMON. Did these migra tions leave Zarahemla, Bountiful, and Desolation unpeopled? 

The 5,400 men besides women and children left the old homes about 53 B. C. By 385 A. D. the old lands were still inhabited. Zarahemla, Desolation, and Bountiful were still the centers of Nephite government and civilization. It was at the head of the Sidon that the last war began between Nephites and Lamanites. It was at the narrow neck of land, at the city of Desolation that one of the bitterest battles took place, and if the correct interpretation has been placed upon the text of the BOOK OF MORMON, in this book, it was near there likewise that the Nephites were destroyed. 

Does the record clearly indicate that all who had Nephite blood were wiped out? Or is it more likely that all living in the original homes were destroyed? Might there not have been many, thousands or millions, farther north and south, both Jaredites and Nephites, to whom the knowledge of the final wars did not even come?

Though very interesting food for thought, the foregoing is after all pure speculation. It might have been equally

EXPANSION NOT ROOTING UP 

203 true that all the Nephites were destroyed. Word could well have been sent to those living in the northlands to come to the support of their friends in the first homes. This could account for the long period of gathering. 

The authors have no reason at all to disagree with this. The point they have tried throughout to make is that as they read the BOOK OF MORMON, the people lived and died in the same general section of the new world. 

If there was any traveling during the last four years, is must have been from north to south, not from south to north. It matters not how far nor in what numbers the 

Nephites moved, it seems perfectly clear that in the end their civilization came to an end in the old homeland. north

America

The circle indicates the probable home-lands of Book of mormon

Peoples-the territory whereon they landed lived, and died.

The arrows point to the lands into which the people probably Aushed by expansion rather than by migration involving the abandonment of their original and permanent homes. 

Though they spread widely, is it not doubtful that they ever en tirely left their original lands even towards the very end? 

Does this illustration repre- 

Sent a reasonable interpretation of the facts involved!

How similar in many respect this is to the Bible! The story proper is confined to a relatively small territory, but because of the caliber of the men and women in the story, because of our experience with flat country and rapid transportation, our active imaginations have magnified many aspects of the story quite beyond when the text seems to bear out. Nor has this situation been helped, apparently, by having a prescribed pattern into which to fit our reading of the record, a pattern calling for two great continents as the home of the ancient Americans, a pattern seemingly not provided by the record itself. 

We may and do feel at all times the presence of other peoples and nations, but such peoples and nations may not be legitimately part of the record as we have it. Hundreds of things, say the historians, were not written at all. If we had those things, we should know much more than we now do of the lives of the people.

CHAPTER TWENTY-SEVEN A GLANCE AT THE BIBLE 

Nothing could be more helpful or more pertinent than to bring this book to a close with a reference to the Book of Books. The Bible and its geopraphical setting offer much for the student of the BOOK OF MORMON.

Many things about the Bible are so completely illustrative of the point of view of this book that the authors cannot refrain from suggesting a few parallels. 

I. What the Bible Is. 

The Bible is a record of God's dealings with His people in the so-called ancient world, and of the hopes, fears, acts, and thoughts of that people.

II. The People of the Bible. 

Though it tells of many nations, the Bible deals primarily with one small branch of the human race, the Israelites. 

It treats of other races and nations only incidentally. 

Adam was the son of God. (Luke 3:38) In the years following him his posterity spread over a vast portion of the earth's surface. Of most of these the Bible gives nothing but their names. For example: Shem, one of the three sons of Noah, was himself the father of five sons, Elam, Asshur, Arphaxad, Lud, and Aram. (Genesis 10:22) Of four of them and their descendants the record offers little information. Arphaxad was the one through whom the chosen people came, so he alone is given additional importance. He was the father of Salah; (v. 24) Salah was the father of Eber, and Eber was the father of the Hebrews of whom the Israelites were a branch.

Thus the people of whom the Bible is a record were constantly casting off groups and nations of whom no history is given. It is easy to see, therefore, that the Bible is a story of a very small group, comparatively speaking. It follows the narrative of all Israel proper in Palestine, in Egypt, and back in Palestine again until the captivity of the Ten Tribes by the Assyrians in 722 B. C. There we lose one of the two main branches of the Hebrews.

A GLANCE AT THE BIBLE 

205 We do not know what became of the Ten Tribes, but we do know that they have somewhere become a mighty people under the care of God. They went outside the record we now have, but we are very sure that they have continued to live, no matter where they are. The fact that we have no further history of them should not be permitted to make us unmindful of them.

The Bible story is devoted, however, with no less earnestness to those who remained, a few people in a small land, small, that is, in extent though great in importance. 

At the same time there were around them millions of people of whom the historians were aware but whom they do not discuss.

III. The Land of the Bible.
