Indian Ocean

SIZE OF BIBLE LANDS COMPARED WITH 

OTHER LANDS Beersheba 

We are astonished when we learn the actual size of the main land of the Bible, Palestine. It is only one and onehalf times as large as the state of Connecticut, one of the smallest states in the Union. It is less than one-ninth as large as Utah and approximately three times the size of Utah County! The Jordan River, the eastern boundary, is less than fifty miles from the Mediterranean on the south and only twenty-five miles on the north.

Our imaginations have played some wonderful tricks on us, but tricks nonetheless. The magificent stories of

Homeric proportions we have read so many times in the Bible, and heard others tell under the power of their imaginations, have too often made us think of the settings in proportion to the characters.

From Egypt to Palestine, a distance in which Israel spent forty years, is less than three hundred miles. From

Dan to Beersheba, in the opposite extremes of Palestine, in 150 miles. From Jerusalem to Moab, where Naomi spent so many lonely years, and from which it was such a great undertaking to return, is about thirty miles. Hannah, who lived at Řamah and went once a year to see her son Samuel, traveled fifteen miles each way. David and the Philistines were separated by twenty-five miles! "And when the Master, driven from Nazareth, went to

Capernaum to make that the center of his ministry, he walked some twenty miles across the hills." 33

When Samuel went out on his circuits to judge Israel on journeys that have always seemed such great undertakings, he was at no time more than fifteen miles from home. 

To quote again from Dr. Fosdick: "How tiny and compact it (Palestine) is, no description, however vivid, is likely to make clear to one who has not been there." 34

Dr. Fosdick gives two reasons for our misconceptions concerning the size of the land. "For one thing," he writes, "our Bible-school maps naturally have made Palestine huge and central, with Egypt and Assyria hanging on the edge; for another, our modern life moves in continental areas and does business on a vast scale, so that where much has happened, as in Palestine, one instinctively imagines for it a large setting." 35 

How true this is! Furthermore, it is easy to forget that many events occurred in the same locality over the centuries, and that new names do not always mean new territory. Our youthful fancies have invested the Bible stories with a sort of romantic atmosphere. Let us take, for instance, the siege of Jericho. Who has not thought of Jericho as a gigantic fortress standing in the way of divine progress with its king and its mighty men of valor? Who has not seen the event of its fall magnified into epic proportions with the ceremonial of the Ark, the priests, the tramping of soldiers, the blowing of trumpets, and the shoutings of Israel? It was a tremendous siege that siege of Jericho, but not in miles nor in terms of population. 

Who can deny that the Bible is a record of a relatively small people in a very small land? Why should this not be equally true of the BOOK OF MORMON? 

33. Fosdick, Harry Emerson, A Pilgrimage to Palestine 

34. Op. cit., pp. 10-11 

35. Ibid., p. 10

CHAPTER TWENTY-EIGHT CONCLUSIONS 

A few comments may be in order as we look back upon the project now drawing to a close. Certain conclusions have been presented here, but all are tentative and subject' to revision or absolute rejection as new light and further study render them untenable.

There are a number of suggestions that may prove of value, and the authors call attention to them now. 

1. The BOOK OF MORMON does contain sufficient information to warrant the establishing of a pattern of geography. In other words, the material on the subject is not so fragmentary as is frequently supposed. 

2. Geography of the BOOK OF MORMON should be found in the book itself so far as that is possible. 

3. The whole record must be taken into consideration in the making of such a pattern. No single statement will contain sufficient information when used alone. 

4. Interpretations should be offered humbly with full realization that as mere men we may all be in error. 

5. The language of the record may oftentimes be misread or misunderstood by us in the light of our experience and our modern civilization, particularly as regards the size of lands and number of people. 

6. There were in some instances a large land and a small land of the same name as the city in them. Confusion on this point has been widespread and has resulted in many errors. 

7. There seems to be no evidence in the record to justify the universal belief among our people that the Jaredites and Nephites moved their entire civilizations more than four thousand miles from their original homes to northeastern

United States. It appears that where they lived, there also they died. 

8. The authors feel that the greatest contribution of their work is the contention that the lands and peoples of the ancient Americans were limited in extent. Should we not think in terms of hundreds of miles instead of thousands, and of millions of people instead of hundreds of millions? 

Of greatest and most immediate interest to all members of the Church, if conclusions 7 and 8 above are based upon good evidence, is the question how the sacred records from which the Prophet Joseph Smith translated the BOOK OF 

MORMON were found in New York State if the last battle of the Nephites was fought somewhere else, for, of course, it was in New York State that the prophet found the plates; let there be no mistake about that.

The statement has already been made, and apparently with good reason, that either the Jaredite records were moved after the destruction of the people, from New York to the country of the narrow neck of land, or the Nephite records were moved from the country of the narrow neck of land to New York State. That is perfectly obvious. If both nations were destroyed at the same place, and if the records were found in places separated by more than four thousand miles, one of them must have been moved. The evidence favors the view that the Nephite plates were thus transferred.

How and when were they moved from one place to the other?

The authors do not consider it their responsibility to answer this question even if it requires an answer, yet they are sincerely interested in the answer. If their conclusions are in error, as indicated in the introduction, there is no need to find an answer at all. If they are right, it then becomes the duty of every earnest man and woman to aid in finding the solution to such problems as face us. 

However, since they have been instrumental in throwing the problem into the clear, the authors here make a suggestion or two that may serve as a possible point of departure for further study and thought. 

The last battle of the Nephites closed about 385 A. D. 

Only Moroni survived. Thereafter for about thirty-six or thirty-seven years he remained, apparently alone, busying

CONCLUSIONS 

209 himself in some manner. 

What he did or where he went he does not tell us. He makes one statement, however, that is very interesting and into which it is possible to read something significant whether or not such manner of interpreting takes us any nearer the truth. "Therefore I will write and hide up the records in the earth; and whither I go it mattereth not. "Behold, my father hath made this record, and he hath written the intent thereof. And behold, I would write it also if I had room upon the plates, but I have not; and ore I have none, for I am alone." (Mormon 8:4-5)

It seems that Moroni had something to write though what it was we have little idea. "And ore I have none." Many will find it remarkable that Moroni should lack ore in the land in which his fathers had always had such an abundance, provided that he was in that land at the time of which he writes, and is it not strange that he could not have utilized some of the ore of the weapons of which there must have been tons? But if he had been in what is now New York State, as, indeed, he could have been, it is likely that he would have found difficulty in finding ore, certainly if the weapons were in another land. With no metal for new plates, and no room on the old ones, how could he have made mention of his having moved even if he had so intended?

The writers think it perhaps more likely that Moroni moved the plates, if at all, sometime during the fourteen centuries that elapsed after the last battle and before the coming of the Prophet Joseph. During part of that time a resurrected being he could easily have made the transfer. 

That the resurrected Moroni did handle and have custody of the plates we know from the history of the Church. 

May we not, then, say for the present that our sacred hill Cumorah in New York is a namesake of another oncebloodstained and no less appointed place in the homeland of the Jaredites and Nephites? Such at least is the trend of much of the thinking of today.

CHAPTER TWENTY-NINE A FINAL WORD 

The fair and careful reader must have noticed the many instances in the foregoing pages in which the authors have expressed the hope that they will not be misunderstood. 

This is not the result of timidity; it is rather due to the fact that on so many points they simply do not know. They have tried to keep from setting stakes that may have to be pulled up. They have endeavored to keep from making statements that will be misleading. They have earnestly desired throughout the study to preserve open minds consistent with a true scientific spirit. Yet, despite these many expressions, they once more state their position with a final word of caution.

It has not been in their hearts to engender doubt nor to raise disturbing questions in the minds of any. The last thing they want is argument and controversy. Their purpose has been two-fold: To determine what an unprejudiced study of the BOOK OF MORMON would yield with regard to the matters set forth herein, and to bring into the clear something that would act as a point of departure for further study and encourage a more widespread and critical reading of the record. Surely no one should fear the result of such study if it be sincere, and if truth be the aim. The record will vindicate itself. Just as the Bible has withstood all research concerning it and remains today more firmly established than ever before, so the BOOK OF MORMON will successfully justify the reasonable and righteous claims for it. Students will err in interpretations and conclusions, but in the end, truth will prevail.

The writers are not unmindful, as they have repeatedly asserted, of the great and grave questions raised by the evidences all about us of ancient civilizations throughout this country. Let us take, for example, the remains of the so-called Mound Builders.

These remarkable people at one time or another cover-

A FINAL WORD 

211 ed most of the territory of eastern United States. Mounds numbering many thousands, of almost every shape, size, and design contain evidences that their builders were in some respects equal to our people of today. They apparently had a standard unit of measurement; they worked metals; they did beautiful weaving and spinning; they had a vast knowledge of astronomy. 

Who were these people? Whence came they? How long ago did they flourish in this land? What caused their eventual downfall? For what purposes did they construct the mounds? Were they associated in any way with the peoples of the BOOK OF MORMON? Might they have been those who lived before the Flood?

There is no way at present of answering these important questions. To the Latter-day Saints, however, they are of especial interest inasmuch as the Mound Builders might have been some of those who left the central lands of the peoples of the BOOK OF MORMON. It is perhaps foolish even to suggest whether they were related to Jaredites or Nephites.

One phase of the problem merits a special comment. 

If, as suggested above, the Mound Builders were related to some of the peoples of the BOOK OF MORMON, how shall we account for the fact that whereas their friends in the homelands left the world magnificent monuments and buildings in imperishable stone, they have bequeathed to posterity nothing more than some mounds of dirt? Might it not with good reason be urged that such great differences in their works argue against any close relationship between them?

Let us see. Numerous massive stone pyramids have been found in Mexico among the remains of ancient civilizations. It is a known fact that the Mound Builders were likewise pyramid builders. Why, then, have they left nothing in stone?

Is not the answer to be found in the nature of the materials with which they had to work? The peoples in Mexico and Central America had an abundance of stone of many kinds, and they utilized it. Indeed, in some sections of the country it may have been more plentiful than timber. In much of what is now eastern United States, however, the reverse is true. If they had no thought of building for posterity, the Mound Builders would use such materials as

A FINAL WORD 

213 they had available; namely, timber and earth.

It is not known for what purpose or purposes the mounds were made. Various suggestions have been given to account for them. They may have been connected with the religious life of the people, or they could have been military fortifications. They might well have been dwellings or burial grounds. Again, they might have been but the foundations of buildings. In any case, without stone for foundations or superstructures, the buildings would have gone back to the earth.

Nor is this without a parallel. Egypt iş a treasure-land of enduring ancient structures made of stone. Babylon, once the peer of this same Egypt, is today a collection of mounds. 

Might this not be comparable to what happened in our own hemisphere?

The authors are as interested in these questions as anyone. They sincerely trust that nothing in the present volume will be construed as an attempt to argue against the possibility that the peoples of the BOOK OF MORMON covered the western world. Their only point is that the record itself does not tell about these things. It is limited to a relatively small area. As already shown, multitudes of 

Nephites moved away from the homelands, and after that the story does not follow them. This could well have been true also of the Jaredites, and the Mound Builders might well have been descendants of either group or of both.
